Estimates for commutators of
fractional differential operators

$C>0$ such that $\mathcal{I}_{1}\leq C\,\mathcal{I}_{2}$,}\\
&\quad\text{where $C$ may depend on the parameters such as $n,s,p,q,\ldots$ but not on the}\\
&\quad\text{variable quantities such as functions $f,g,\phi$ or variables $x,y,t$.}\\

\vskip 4.30554ptBMO({\mathbb{R}^{n}})&\quad\text{space of functions of bounded mean oscillation, see (\ref{eq:tt def BMO})}\\

\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

1  Introduction

The goal of this work is to present an interesting method for proving a variety of commutator estimates involving Riesz transforms, fractional Laplacians and Riesz potentials.
This thesis is based in large parts on the recent work
[
LS20
]
of Lenzmann and Schikorra, in which they proposed this method.
What makes the method interesting, is its accessibility.
Except for some elementary transformations, the only tools needed are harmonic extensions from
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
to
{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}
and integration by parts.
That is, as long as we accept some general estimates from
{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}
back to
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
as blackboxes.
Next to the theoretical background for these blackbox estimates, we also provide a number of applications for this method.
Many of the commutator estimates we show are already well known, but originally they were either proven via an individual approach or required a lot more effort.

Before describing the outline of this thesis, we give a short sketch on how the method works.
Usually, we estimate the commutators either in
L^{p}
or in the Hardy space
\mathcal{H}^{1}
.
These space norm estimates are typically obtained via the respective dual space characterization.
Therefore, we start with some integral term over
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
.
We run through three steps.
First, we interpret
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
as boundary of the higher dimensional half-space
{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}
.
We can then extend every occurring function to
{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}
by solving the Poisson equation with the respective function as boundary value, obtaining the harmonic extension.
Second, via a suitable integration by parts we obtain an integral over
{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}
.
Third, via some elementary transformations and estimates we reach a suitable form, to which we can apply one of the blackbox estimates.
During these transformations, we will usually observe “cancellation effects” due to the commutator’s structure, which are the reason why we obtain better estimates than if we would just estimate each component of the commutators separately.

We will further illustrate this approach with a first application in this section, showing a Hardy-space estimate for the Jacobian
\det(\nabla u)
as well as an intermediate estimate.
The remaining sections can be divided into two parts.
In Sections
2
and
3
, we show the application of the method, proving various commutator estimates.
Sections
4
and
5
provide the theoretical background.

In Section
2
, we first recall the classical harmonic extension and collect a variety of facts for later use.
We then prove estimates for three different commutators involving classical differential operators or the half-Laplacian
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}
.

•

Subsection
2.2
: The div-curl estimate by Coifman, Lions, Meyer, and Semmes.

•

Subsection
2.3
: The Coifman-Rochberg-Weiss commutator estimate for Riesz transforms.

•

Subsection
2.4
: An
L^{1}
-estimate for a double-commutator complementing Subsection
2.3
.

In Section
3
, we introduce the
s
-harmonic extension, also known as
s
-Poisson extension.
This is a generalization of the classical harmonic extension, which allows us to treat commutators involving fractional Laplacians
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}
.
After collecting some results for this generalized harmonic extension in Subsection
3.1
, we show four more applications for the method.

•

Subsection
3.2
: Estimates for commutators of multiplication and fractional Laplacians.

•

Subsection
3.3
: The Chanilo commutator estimate for Riesz potentials of order
<1
.

•

Subsection
3.4
: Fractional Leibniz rules.

•

Subsection
3.5
: The Da Lio-Rivière three-term commutator estimate.

For these two sections, we closely follow
[
LS20
]
.
However, we choose a different structure and go into more detail regarding the basic properties of the extensions in Subsections
2.1
and
3.1
.
Additionally, we were able to shorten some of the proofs.

The goal of the second part, Sections
4
and
5
, is to obtain the blackbox estimates, which are essential for the proofs in Sections
2
and
3
.
In Section
4
, we collect some well known facts about Riesz transforms, fractional Laplacians and Riesz potentials and prove some specific, elementary Lemmas in preparation for Section
5
.
If the reader is not familiar with these operators, we recommend to check out this section first.

In Section
5
, we prove the blackbox estimates by collecting a variety of building blocks, which can be combined to obtain these blackbox estimates.
There are two different sources for these building blocks.
First, in
[
BC17
]
, Bui and Candy showed that Triebel-Lizorkin and Besov-Lipschitz spaces can be characterized with Poisson like kernels.
From these characterizations we obtain
BMO
, Hölder and fractional Sobolev space estimates for the harmonic extensions.
Second, from Stein’s book (
[
Ste93
]
) we acquire square function estimates as well as pointwise estimates in terms of the maximal function.
Additionally, we introduce Lorentz spaces together with an interpolation theorem, with which we obtain finer Lorentz estimates from
L^{p}
-estimates.
The Section is structured in the following way.

•

Subsection
5.1
: Triebel-Lizorkin and Besov-Lipschitz spaces and the Bui-Candy result.

•

Subsection
5.2
: Triebel-Lizorkin and Besov-Lipschitz space characterizations.

•

Subsection
5.3
: Lorentz spaces and an interpolation theorem.

•

Subsection
5.4
:
BMO
, fractional Sobolev and Hölder space estimates.

•

Subsection
5.5
: Square function estimates.

•

Subsection
5.6
: The maximal function and pointwise estimates.

•

Subsection
5.7
: The blackbox estimates.

While the main ideas are also based on
[
LS20
]
, in these last two sections we take a wider approach, going into much more detail regarding the tools and the building blocks necessary for the blackbox estimates.
By gathering some additional building blocks, we are able to further generalize the final blackbox estimates while at the same time presenting them clearer and more intuitive for applications.

Before diving into the first example, we would like to thank A. Schikorra for his quick and comprehensive answers to our questions throughout the work on this thesis.

1.1  A first example: Jacobian estimates

For a function
u\colon{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
, we have the Jacobian
\det(\nabla u)
.
It is obvious, that

\|\det(\nabla u)\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\lesssim\|\nabla u\|_{L^{n}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}^{n}.

Making use of the determinants inherent structure, we obtain better regularity, that is

\|\det(\nabla u)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\lesssim\|\nabla u\|_{L^{n}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}^{n},

which is a consequence of (
1.1.1
) due to the duality of the Hardy space and
BMO
, see
[
FS72
]
.
This estimate was originally due to Coifman, Lions, Meyer, and Semmes, see
[
CLMS93
, Theorem II.1.1), p.250]
.
In the same work, they showed that this Jacobian estimate actually is a special case of the commutator estimate we show in Subsection
2.3
, confer
[
CLMS93
, Section III.1, pp.257-258]
.
We stated above that with our method the cancellation effects responsible for the better estimate can be easily observed during the third step of the proof.
This estimate is an exception though, since we already use the special determinant structure in the second step.
By coding the Jacobian as differential form, we are able to apply Stokes’ Theorem instead of a standard integration by parts.
For a definition of the occurring space norms, see Subsection
5.4
, i.e. (
5.4.1
) and (
5.4.12
).

Theorem
(Jacobian estimate).

Let
\phi\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
and
u=(u^{1},\ldots,u^{n})\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}},{\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
.
Then the following estimate holds.

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\phi\det(\nabla u)\lesssim[\phi]_{BMO}\|\nabla u\|^{n}_{L^{n}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}

(1.1.1)

Moreover, let
0<s_{i}<1
,
1<p_{i}<\infty
for
i=0,\ldots,n
satisfy

\sum_{i=0}^{n}s_{i}=n,\quad\sum_{i=0}^{n}\frac{1}{p_{i}}=1.

(1.1.2)

Then

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\phi\det(\nabla u)\lesssim[\phi]_{W^{s_{0},p_{0}}}[u^{1}]_{W^{s_{1},p_{1}}}\ldots[u^{n}]_{W^{s_{n},p_{n}}}.

(1.1.3)

\square

Proof
(Estimate (
1.1.3
)).

As explained above, this proof will be divided into 3 steps.
First, we extend the occurring functions to
{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}
.
Here, let
\Phi\colon{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
U\colon{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
be the usual harmonic extensions to
{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}
of
\phi
and
u
, meaning they fulfill

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}&\begin{cases}\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\Phi\equiv(\Delta_{x}+\partial_{tt})\Phi=0&\text{in }{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}},\\
\Phi(x,0)=\phi(x)&\text{in }{\mathbb{R}^{n}},\end{cases}\\
&\begin{cases}\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}U\equiv(\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}U_{i})_{i}=0&\text{in }{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}},\\
U(x,0)=u(x)&\text{in }{\mathbb{R}^{n}}.\end{cases}\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

In order for the integration by parts formulas to be applicable, the extensions need to sufficiently decrease for
|(x,t)|\rightarrow\infty
.
Choosing zero-boundary data at infinity,
\Phi
and
U
are obtained via the Poisson operator given in Definition
Definition
, i.e.
\Phi(x,t)\coloneqq P^{1}_{t}\phi(x)
and
U(x,t)\coloneqq P^{1}_{t}u(x)
.

For the second step, we integrate by parts to obtain an integral over the upper half-space in terms of
\Phi
and
U
.
Here, via Stokes’ theorem, see Theorem
Theorem
, we have

\mathcal{I}\coloneqq\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\phi~\det_{n\times n}(\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}u)\right|=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}\det_{(n+1)\times(n+1)}(\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\Phi,\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}U)\right|

(1.1.4)

since, according to Lemma
Lemma
below, we can rewrite both sides as

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\phi~\det_{n\times n}(\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}u)=\int_{\partial{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}\phi~du^{1}\wedge du^{2}\wedge\ldots\wedge du^{n},

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}\det_{(n+1)\times(n+1)}(\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\Phi,\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}U)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}d\Phi\wedge dU^{1}\wedge dU^{2}\wedge\ldots\wedge dU^{n}.

Additionally, in order to apply Stokes’ theorem to
w\coloneqq\Phi\,dU^{1}\wedge dU^{2}\wedge\ldots\wedge dU^{n}
, we have to confirm that
\phi~du^{1}\wedge du^{2}\wedge\ldots\wedge du^{n}
is integrable on
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
and that
w
and its first order derivatives are integrable on
{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}
.
The integrability on
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
is obvious since
\phi,u^{1},\ldots,u^{n}
are compactly supported.
Regarding the integrability on
{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}
,
w
and its derivatives are bounded according to Corollary
Corollary
.
Additionally, the product of multiple harmonic extensions and their derivatives decays sufficiently fast thanks to Corollary
Corollary
.
Note that
d
represents the
n+1
-dimensional exterior derivative on the left side of (
1.1.3
) and the
n
-dimensional one on the right side.
But since the pullback of
dt
to the boundary is zero, we still have

\int_{\partial{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}\Phi\,dU^{1}\wedge dU^{2}\wedge\ldots\wedge dU^{n}=\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}\phi~du^{1}\wedge du^{2}\wedge\ldots\wedge du^{n}.

In the third and last step, we apply one of the trace theorems from Section
5
to obtain the integral (semi-)norms on
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
.
In this case, the seminorms
[\cdot]_{W^{s_{i},p_{i}}}
can be obtained via (
5.4.3
) and (
5.4.2
) from Proposition
Proposition
.
Combined, these two estimates yield

\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{0}^{\infty}|t^{1-\frac{1}{p_{0}}-s_{0}}\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}\Phi(x,t)|^{p_{0}}~dt~dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p_{0}}}\approx[\phi]_{W^{s_{0},p_{0}}}

and, respectively for
i=1,\ldots,n
,

\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{0}^{\infty}|t^{1-\frac{1}{p_{i}}-s_{i}}\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}U^{i}(x,t)|^{p_{i}}~dt~dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p_{i}}}\approx[u^{i}]_{W^{s_{i},p_{i}}}.

We only need to transform
\mathcal{I}
into a fitting form.
Estimating the determinant against its arguments’ norms and using Hölder’s inequality, we obtain

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LCL}\vskip 4.30554pt\mathcal{I}&\lesssim&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}|\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}\Phi(x,t)||\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}U^{1}(x,t)|\ldots|\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}U^{n}(x,t)|\,dx\,dt\\
&\overset{(\ref{eq:JacEs2Req})}{=}&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{n+1-\sum_{i=0}^{n}\frac{1}{p_{i}}-\sum_{i=0}^{n}s_{i}}|\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}\Phi(x,t)||\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}U^{1}(x,t)|\ldots|\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}U^{n}(x,t)|\,dt\,dx\\
&\leq&\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{0}^{\infty}|t^{1-\frac{1}{p_{0}}-s_{0}}\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}\Phi(x,t)|^{p_{0}}~dt~dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p_{0}}}\\
&&\cdot\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{0}^{\infty}|t^{1-\frac{1}{p_{i}}-s_{1}}\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}U^{1}(x,t)|^{p_{1}}\,dt\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p_{1}}}\\
&&\vdots\\
&&\cdot\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{0}^{\infty}|t^{1-\frac{1}{p_{n}}-s_{n}}\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}U^{n}(x,t)|^{p_{n}}\,dt\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p_{n}}}\\
&\approx&[\phi]_{W^{s_{0},p_{0}}}[u^{1}]_{W^{s_{1},p_{1}}}\ldots[u^{n}]_{W^{s_{n},p_{n}}}.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

Thus, we have shown that (
1.1.3
) holds.

\square

Proof
(Estimate (
1.1.1
)).

First, let
\Phi
and
U
be the same harmonic extensions of
\phi
and
u
as in the proof of (
1.1.3
) above.
In the second step, we start with the same integration by parts and again have

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\mathcal{I}\coloneqq\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\phi~\det_{n\times n}(\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}u)\right|=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}\det_{(n+1)\times(n+1)}(\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}U,\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\Phi)\right|\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

This time, we need an additional derivative to apply the trace theorem we are aiming for. Therefore, we integrate by parts in
t
-direction to obtain an additional
t
-derivative,

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\mathcal{I}&=&\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\frac{1}{T}}^{T}\det(\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}U(x,t),\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}\Phi(x,t))~dt\right)dx\right|\\
&=&\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\left(\left[t\det(\nabla U(x,t),\nabla\Phi(x,t))\right]_{\frac{1}{T}}^{T}-\int_{\frac{1}{T}}^{T}t\partial_{t}\det(\nabla U(x,t),\nabla\Phi(x,t))~dt\right)dx\right|\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

Since the harmonic extensions
U
and
\Phi
are bounded, see Corollary
Corollary
, we have

\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}t|\nabla U^{1}(x,t)|\ldots|\nabla U^{n}(x,t)||\nabla\Phi(x,t)|=0.

Thanks to Corollary
Corollary
, the decay-estimate for the harmonic extensions, we obtain

\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}t|\nabla U^{1}(x,t)|\ldots|\nabla U^{n}(x,t)||\nabla\Phi(x,t)|=0

for the other limit.
Therefore, we conclude the second step having shown that

\mathcal{I}=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t\partial_{t}\det(\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}U^{1},\ldots,\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}U^{n},\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\Phi)\,dx\,dt\right|.

For the third step, it suffices to show that

\mathcal{I}\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\Phi|\,|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\nabla_{x}U|\,|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}U|^{n-1}\,dx\,dt.

(1.1.5)

Basically, we need to show that the added
t
-derivative can be converted to a derivative in
x
and that, when it hits
\Phi
, the derivative can be redistributed to instead hit a
U
-term.
Should we have (
1.1.5
), we can apply Proposition
Proposition
for
s=1
to obtain

\mathcal{I}\lesssim[\phi]_{BMO({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\|(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}u\|^{n}_{L^{n}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\approx[\phi]_{BMO({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\|\nabla u\|^{n}_{L^{n}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}

where the second estimate is due to Lemma
Proposition
.

Let us prove (
1.1.5
).
Recalling the Leibniz formula for the determinant, we observe that

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\mathcal{I}&\leq&\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t\det(\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}U^{1},\ldots,\partial_{t}\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}U^{i},\ldots,\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}U^{n},\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\Phi)\right|\\
&&+\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t\det(\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}U^{1},\ldots,\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}U^{n},\partial_{t}\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\Phi)\right|\\
&\eqqcolon&\mathcal{I}_{1}+\mathcal{I}_{2}.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

Therefore, we have to consider two cases.
Regarding
\mathcal{I}_{1}
, with
\partial_{tt}U=-\Delta_{x}U
we have

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt|\partial_{t}\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}U^{i}|\leq|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}^{2}U|&\approx&|\partial_{tt}U|+\sum_{i=1}^{n}(|\partial_{t}\partial_{x_{i}}U|+|\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{t}U|)+|\nabla_{x}^{2}U|\\
&\leq&|\nabla_{x}^{2}U|+2|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\nabla_{x}U|+|\nabla_{x}^{2}U|\\
&\lesssim&|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\nabla_{x}U|.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

Thus, we obtain

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\mathcal{I}_{1}&\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t\,|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}U|^{n-1}\,|\partial_{t}\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}U|\,|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\Phi|\\
&\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t\,|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}U|^{n-1}\,|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\nabla_{x}U|\,|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\Phi|.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

Regarding
\mathcal{I}_{2}
, we have to redistribute the additional derivative from
\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\Phi
to a
U
-term.
We rename the variables
(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n+1})=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},t)
.
Considering the Leibniz formula for the determinant, we obtain

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\mathcal{I}_{2}&=&\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t\det(\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}U^{1},\ldots,\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}U^{n},\partial_{t}\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\Phi)\,dx\,dt\right|\\
&\leq&\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}=1}^{n+1}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}z_{n+1}\,\partial_{z_{i_{1}}}U^{1}\ldots\partial_{z_{i_{n}}}U^{n}\,\partial_{z_{n+1}}\partial_{z_{k}}\Phi\,dx\,dt\right|\\
&&+\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}=1}^{n+1}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}z_{n+1}\,\partial_{z_{i_{1}}}U^{1}\ldots\partial_{z_{i_{n}}}U^{n}\,\partial_{z_{n+1}}\partial_{z_{n+1}}\Phi\,dx\,dt\right|.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

By harmonicity,
\partial_{z_{n+1}}\partial{z_{n+1}}\Phi=-\sum_{l=1}^{n}\partial_{z_{l}z_{l}}\Phi
and we have

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\mathcal{I}_{2}&\leq&\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}=1}^{n+1}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}z_{n+1}\,\partial_{z_{i_{1}}}U^{1}\ldots\partial_{z_{i_{n}}}U^{n}\,\partial_{z_{n+1}}\partial_{z_{k}}\Phi\,dx\,dt\right|\\
&&+\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}=1}^{n+1}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}z_{n+1}\,\partial_{z_{i_{1}}}U^{1}\ldots\partial_{z_{i_{n}}}U^{n}\,\partial_{z_{l}}\partial_{z_{l}}\Phi\,dx\,dt\right|.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

We redistribute the additional derivative with an integration by parts in
z_{k}
for the first term, and in
z_{l}
for the second term.
We denote
(z_{1},\ldots,z_{i-1},z_{i+1},\ldots z_{n+1})
by
\tilde{z_{i}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
for
1\leq i\leq n
and obtain

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\mathcal{I}_{2}&\leq&\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}=1}^{n+1}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\left(\lim_{Z\rightarrow\infty}\int_{-Z}^{Z}\partial_{z_{k}}(z_{n+1}\,\partial_{z_{i_{1}}}U^{1}\ldots\partial_{z_{i_{n}}}U^{n})\,\partial_{z_{n+1}}\Phi~dz_{k}\right)d\tilde{z_{k}}\right|\\
&&+\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}=1}^{n+1}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\left(\lim_{Z\rightarrow\infty}\int_{-Z}^{Z}\partial_{z_{l}}(z_{n+1}\,\partial_{z_{i_{1}}}U^{1}\ldots\partial_{z_{i_{n}}}U^{n})\,\partial_{z_{n+1}}\Phi~dz_{l}\right)d\tilde{z_{l}}\right|\\
&=&\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}=1}^{n+1}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}z_{n+1}\,\partial_{z_{k}}(\partial_{z_{i_{1}}}U^{1}\ldots\partial_{z_{i_{n}}}U^{n})\,\partial_{z_{n+1}}\Phi\,\right|\\
&&+\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}=1}^{n+1}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}z_{n+1}\,\partial_{z_{l}}(\partial_{z_{i_{1}}}U^{1}\ldots\partial_{z_{i_{n}}}U^{n})\,\partial_{z_{l}}\Phi\,\right|.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

The boundary terms for the above integration-by-parts in
z_{k}
and
z_{l}
direction disappear, since
k,l\leq n
and the harmonic extension decays to zero sufficiently fast as
|z_{j}|\rightarrow\infty
,
j\leq n
, see Corollary
Corollary
.
We conclude that

\mathcal{I}_{2}\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}z_{n+1}\,|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}U|^{n-1}\,|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\nabla_{x}U|\,|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\Phi|.

Thus, we have established (
1.1.5
) and therefore proven (
1.1.1
).

\square

Rounding off this first application of the method, we justify (
1.1.4
), the main integration-by-parts formula used in the above proofs.

Lemma
.

Let
U\subset{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
be open,
v^{1},\ldots,v^{n}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U)
. Then

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554ptdv^{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dv^{n}=\det(\nabla v^{1},\ldots\nabla v^{n})~dx^{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx^{n}.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

\square

Proof
.

Using the Einstein summation convention, with the rules for the exterior product and the exterior derivative we obtain

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554ptdv^{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dv^{n}&=&\left(\frac{\partial v^{1}}{\partial x^{i}}~dx^{i}\right)\wedge\ldots\wedge\left(\frac{\partial v^{1}}{\partial x^{i}}~dx^{i}\right)\\
&=&\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}\frac{\partial v^{1}}{\partial x^{\sigma(1)}}\ldots\frac{\partial v^{n}}{\partial x^{\sigma(n)}}~dx^{\sigma(1)}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx^{\sigma(n)}\\
&=&\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}\operatorname{sgn}(\sigma)\frac{\partial v^{1}}{\partial x^{\sigma(1)}}\ldots\frac{\partial v^{n}}{\partial x^{\sigma(n)}}~dx^{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx^{n}\\
&=&\det(\nabla v^{1},\ldots\nabla v^{n})~dx^{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx^{n}.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

\square

2  Commutator estimates via classical harmonic extension

In this section, we will prove the div-curl estimate by Coifman-Lions-Meyer-Semmes as well as an estimate for a commutator of Riesz transforms and pointwise multiplication.
We conclude the section with an
L^{1}
-estimate for a double commutator of the same type in one dimension.
First though, we recall some facts about the harmonic extension.

2.1  Harmonic extension to
{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}
via the Poisson operator
P^{1}_{t}

A harmonic extension of a function
f\colon{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
to
{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}
is a solution of the Poisson equation,

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Delta_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}F(x,t)\equiv(\partial_{tt}+\Delta_{x})F(x,t)=0&\text{in }{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}},\\
\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}F(x,t)=f(x)&\text{almost everywhere on }{\mathbb{R}^{n}}.\end{array}\right.

(2.1.1)

Note that the Poisson equation has no unique solution since linear functions with zero-boundary-value could be added to a solution. Therefore, we demand that additionally
\lim_{|(x,t)|\rightarrow\infty}F(x,t)=0
.
Under this assumption, the solution is unique due to the maximum principle for harmonic functions.
We obtain this solution via the Poisson operator.

Definition
(The Poisson operator).

The Poisson extension operator
P^{1}_{t}
is given via the convolution

P^{1}_{t}f(x)\coloneqq C_{n}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}\frac{t}{\left(|x-y|^{2}+t^{2}\right)^{\frac{n+1}{2}}}f(y)\,dy=C_{n}(p^{1}_{t}*f)(x)

for
f\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}},\mathbb{R}^{m})+L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}},\mathbb{R}^{m})
, where the kernel
p^{1}_{t}
is given by

p^{1}_{t}(x)\coloneqq\frac{t}{\left(|x|^{2}+t^{2}\right)^{\frac{n+1}{2}}}.

\square

the kernels
p^{1}_{t}(x)=t^{-n}p^{1}_{1}(t^{-1}x)=(p^{1}_{1})_{t}(x)
are dilations of
p^{s}_{1}
and therefore for all
t>0
we have

\|p^{1}_{t}\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}p^{1}_{t}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}p^{1}_{1}\eqqcolon\frac{1}{C_{n}}.

(2.1.2)

For the computation of
C_{n}
, see
[
Gra14
, Example 2.1.13, pp.92-93]
.
The
L_{\infty}
-norm of
p^{1}_{t}
is given by

\|p^{1}_{t}\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}=\frac{t}{(0^{2}+t^{2})^{\frac{n+1}{2}}}=t^{-n}.

(2.1.3)

For
f\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})\cap\mathcal{C}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
, the Poisson operator solves the Poisson equation in the sense that
F^{1}(x,t)\coloneqq P^{1}_{t}f(x)
fulfills (
2.1.1
).
We might also interpret the kernels
(p^{1}_{t})_{t>0}
as function
k^{1}
on
{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}
with
k^{1}(x,t)\coloneqq p^{1}_{t}(x)
.
For the details on why
F^{1}
fulfills the Dirichlet boundary condition, see again
[
Gra14
, Example 2.1.13]
.
The harmonicity of
F^{1}
is inherited from
k^{1}
(cf. Section
3.1
) since the convolution commutates with the derivations, see the proofs of Lemma
Lemma
and Lemma
Lemma
for details.

We make further use of this commutative property to describe the derivatives of the harmonic extension in two lemmas.
In the first lemma we apply the derivatives to the convolution kernel.

Lemma
.

Let
k_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
for
0\leq i\leq n
. Set
\tilde{k}\coloneqq k_{1}+\ldots+k_{n}
and
k\coloneqq\tilde{k}+k_{0}
.
For any
f\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
with
F^{1}(x,t)\coloneqq P^{1}_{t}f(x)
we have

\partial_{t}^{k_{0}}\partial_{x_{1}}^{k_{1}}\dots\partial_{x_{n}}^{k_{n}}F(x,t)=C_{n,s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{k}\frac{t^{1-k_{0}}q_{j}(x-y)}{(|x-y|^{2}+t^{2})^{\frac{n+1+\tilde{k}+j}{2}}}\right)f(y)\,dy,

where the
q_{j}
are homogeneous polynomials of degree
j
, which depend on
k_{0},\dots,k_{n}
.
In particular, we have
F^{1}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}})
.

\square

Proof
.

See the detailed proof for the generalized harmonic extensions, Lemma
Lemma
.

\square

As a direct consequence of this first lemma, we get a result about the decay of the harmonic extension and its derivatives.

Corollary
(Decay estimate for the harmonic extension).

Let
f\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
and
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
Denote with
F^{1}(x,t)\coloneqq P^{1}_{t}f(x)
the harmonic extension.
Then

\sup_{(x,t)\in{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}t^{k}|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}^{k}F^{1}(x,t)|\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

(2.1.4)

If additionally
f\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
, then

\sup_{(x,t)\in{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}t^{n+k}|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}^{k}F^{1}(x,t)|\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

(2.1.5)

Regarding the decay in
x
-direction, if for some
C>0
,
k>0
we have
|f(x)|\leq C|x|^{-l}
, then

t^{k}|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}^{k}F^{1}(x,t)|\lesssim|x|^{-l}+t\|f\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}|x|^{-n-1}.

(2.1.6)

\square

Proof
.

Regarding (
2.1.4
), according to Lemma
Lemma
or (
3.1.3
), for any
k_{0},\dots k_{n}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\tilde{k}\coloneqq k_{1}+\ldots+k_{n}
we have

|\partial_{t}^{k_{0}}\partial_{x_{1}}^{k_{1}}\dots\partial_{x_{n}}^{k_{n}}k^{1}(x,t)|=\left|\sum_{j=0}^{k}\frac{t^{1-k_{0}}q_{j}(x)}{(|x|^{2}+t^{2})^{\frac{n+1+\tilde{k}+j}{2}}}\right|\leq Ct^{1-k_{0}-\tilde{k}}(|x|^{2}+t^{2})^{-\frac{n+1}{2}}.

Therefore,

t^{k}|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}^{k}F^{1}(x,t)|\leq C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}t^{1}(|x-y|^{2}+t^{2})^{-\frac{n+1}{2}}|f(y)|\,dy\leq C\|f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\|p^{1}_{1}\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

Regarding (
2.1.5
), we analogously obtain

|\partial_{t}^{k_{0}}\partial_{x_{1}}^{k_{1}}\dots\partial_{x_{n}}^{k_{n}}k^{1}(x,t)|\leq Ct^{-k_{0}-\tilde{k}-n}

and therefore

t^{n+k}|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}^{k}F^{1}(x,t)|\leq C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|f(y)|\,dy=C\|f\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

Regarding the decay in
x
-direction, we have

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}^{k}F^{1}(x,t)|&\leq&\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\setminus B_{\frac{|x|}{2}}(0)}|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}^{k}k^{1}(y,t)||f(x-y)|\,dy+\int_{B_{\frac{|x|}{2}}(0)}|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}^{k}k^{1}(y,t)||f(x-y)|\,dy\\
&\leq&Ct^{1-k}\|f\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\left(\frac{|x|}{2}\right)^{-n-1}+Ct^{-k}\|p^{1}_{1}\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\left(\frac{|x|}{2}\right)^{-l}\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

and so obtain (
2.1.6
).

\square

Now, we want to apply the
x
-derivatives to
f
. For the derivatives in
t
-direction, we need the Fourier transform of the kernel
p^{1}_{t}
.

Proposition
(Fourier transform of the Poisson Kernel).

The Fourier transform of the Poisson kernel is

{\mathcal{F}}(p^{1}_{t})(\xi)=e^{-2\pi t|\xi|}.

\square

Proof
.

This result can be achieved via a direct computation, see for example Theorem 1.11. of
[
Hao16
]
. Alternatively, (
2.1.1
) can be transformed into an ordinary differential equation by applying the
n
-dimensional Fourier transform, see for example Section A.1 of
[
LS20
]
.

\square

With this result, we can now turn to the second lemma about the derivatives of the harmonic extension.
For the definition of the half-Laplacian
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}
, see Definition
Definition
.

Lemma
.

Let
k_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
for
0\leq i\leq n
and
f\in W^{\infty,\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
.
Set
F^{1}(x,t)\coloneqq P^{1}_{t}f(x)
.
Then

\partial_{t}^{k_{0}}\partial_{x_{1}}^{k_{1}}\dots\partial_{x_{n}}^{k_{n}}F^{1}(x,t)=(-1)^{k_{0}}P_{t}^{1}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{k_{0}}{2}}}\partial_{x_{1}}^{k_{1}}\dots\partial_{x_{n}}^{k_{n}}f)(x).

In particular, we obtain

\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}\partial_{t}F^{1}(x,t)=-{(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}f(x)\quad\text{on }{\mathbb{R}^{n}}.

(2.1.7)

\square

Proof
.

Assuming
k_{0}=0
, we show Lemma
Lemma
via induction over
\tilde{k}\coloneqq k_{1}+\ldots+k_{n}
.
In a second step, we then deal with the derivatives in
t
direction.
For
k=0
the statement is obvious.
Since
\partial_{x_{1}}^{k_{1}}\dots\partial_{x_{n}}^{k_{n}}f\in W^{\infty,\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
, it is enough to show that
\partial_{x_{l}}P_{t}^{1}f=P_{t}^{1}(\partial_{x_{l}}f)
.
Thanks to Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem, we have

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}D_{h,x_{l}}P_{t}^{1}f(x)&=&\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}C_{n}\int_{{}^{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}p^{1}_{t}(y)\frac{f(x+he_{l}-y)-f(x-y)}{h}dy\\
&=&C_{n}\int_{Rn}p^{1}_{t}(y)\partial_{x_{l}}f(x-y)=P_{t}^{1}(\partial_{x_{l}}f)(x)\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

where
p^{1}_{t}\|\partial_{x_{l}}f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
is an integrable majorant thanks to the mean value theorem.

For the derivatives in
t
-direction, we now only need to handle the case
k_{0}=1
. The reason is that
\partial_{tt}F^{1}(x,t)=-\Delta_{x}F^{1}(x,t)=P_{t}^{1}((-\Delta)f)(x)
due to harmonicity, the linearity of
P_{t}^{1}
and the result for
x
-derivatives we just proved.
We show that
-\partial_{t}F^{1}(x,t)=P_{t}^{1}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}f)(x)
by applying the
n
-dimensional Fourier transform to both sides.
For the right side, we obtain

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt{\mathcal{F}}(-p_{t}^{1}\ast{(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}f)(\xi)=-C_{n}{\mathcal{F}}(p^{1}_{t})(\xi){\mathcal{F}}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}f)(\xi)=-C_{n}e^{-2\pi t|\xi|}\,2\pi|\xi|{\mathcal{F}}(f)(\xi)\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

with Proposition
Proposition
and Definition
Definition
.
For the left side, we obtain

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt{\mathcal{F}}(\partial_{t}F^{1}(x,t))(\xi)=\frac{d}{dt}{\mathcal{F}}(F^{1}(x,t))=\frac{d}{dt}C_{n}e^{-t|\omega||\xi|}{\mathcal{F}}(f)(\xi)=-2\pi C_{n}|\xi|e^{-2\pi t|\xi|}{\mathcal{F}}(f)(\xi)\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

where we can switch the Fourier transform and
\partial_{t}
according to Lemma
Lemma
below.
The local boundedness condition is fulfilled due to Lemma
Lemma
.

\square

While as a consequence of Lemma
Lemma
we got a corollary about the decay of the harmonic extension’s derivatives, out of this second lemma we get the boundedness of these derivatives, at least combined with an
L^{\infty}
estimate for the half-Laplacian, see for example Lemma
Lemma
.

Corollary
(
L^{\infty}
-estimate for the harmonic extension).

Let
k_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
for
0\leq i\leq n
and
f\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
. Then

\|\partial_{t}^{k_{0}}\partial_{x_{1}}^{k_{1}}\dots\partial_{x_{n}}^{k_{n}}P_{t}^{1}f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\leq\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{k_{0}}{2}}}\partial_{x_{1}}^{k_{1}}\dots\partial_{x_{n}}^{k_{n}}f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

\square

Proof
.

The result follows immediately with Lemma
Lemma
and
C_{n}\|p^{1}_{t}\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}=1
.

\square

Wrapping this section up, we still need to make sure that we were allowed to switch the
n
-dimensional Fourier transform and the
t
-derivative in the proof of Lemma
Lemma
.
The following lemma provides a condition under which this switch is possible.

Lemma
.

Let
F\in\mathcal{C}^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}})
.
Assume
t_{0}>0
. Suppose that there exists
0<\epsilon<t_{0}
such that
\partial_{t}F
and
|x|^{n+1}\partial_{t}F(x,t)
are bounded on
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\times B_{\epsilon}(t_{0})
.
Then

\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=t_{0}}{\mathcal{F}}(F(\cdot,t))={\mathcal{F}}(\partial_{t}F(\cdot,t_{0})).

\square

Proof
.

Since
\partial_{t}F
and
(x,t)\rightarrow|x|^{n+1}\partial_{t}F(x,t)
are bounded, there exists
C>0
such that for all
x\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
,
t\in B_{\epsilon}(t_{0})

\partial_{t}F(x,t)\leq\min\left\{C,\frac{C}{|x|^{n+1}}\right\}.

Applying Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem with
\min\left\{C,\frac{C}{|x|^{n+1}}\right\}
as majorant, we obtain

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=t_{0}}{\mathcal{F}}(F(\cdot,t))(\xi)&=&\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=t_{0}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}F(x,t)\,e^{-2\pi ix\cdot\xi}\,dx\right)\\
&=&\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{F(x,t_{0}+h)-F(x,t_{0})}{h}\,e^{-2\pi ix\cdot\xi}\,dx\\
&=&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\partial_{t}F(x,t_{0})\,e^{-2\pi ix\cdot\xi}\,dx&={\mathcal{F}}(\partial_{t}F)(\xi).\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

\square

2.2  The general div-curl estimate by Coifman-Lions-Meyer-Semmes

The Jacobian estimate (
1.1.1
) is actually a special case of the general div-curl estimate below, which was proven by Coifman-Lions-Meyer-Semmes, see
[
CLMS93
, Theorem II.1, pp. 250-251]
.
While curl is not explicitly mentioned in the formulation we chose, on
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
every vectorfield
v
with
\operatorname{curl}(v)=0
can be written as
v=\nabla f
for a suitably chosen scalar field
f
.
In addition to the original result, we again obtain an intermediate estimate.

Theorem
(Coifman-Lions-Meyer-Semmes).

Assume
\phi,f\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
,
g\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}},{\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
with

\operatorname{div}(g)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\partial_{i}g_{i}=0.

Suppose
1<p_{1},p_{2}<\infty
,
1\leq q_{1},q_{2}\leq\infty
with
\frac{1}{p_{1}}+\frac{1}{p_{2}}=\frac{1}{q_{1}}+\frac{1}{q_{2}}=1
.
Then

\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\partial_{i}f\cdot g_{i}\,\phi\lesssim[\phi]_{BMO}\|\nabla f\|_{L^{(p_{1},q_{1})}}\|g\|_{L^{(p_{2},q_{2})}}.

(2.2.1)

Suppose
0<s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}<1
with
s_{1}+s_{2}+s_{3}=2
and
1<p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}<\infty
,
1\leq q_{1},q_{2},q_{3}\leq\infty
with
\frac{1}{p_{1}}+\frac{1}{p_{2}}+\frac{1}{p_{3}}=\frac{1}{q_{1}}+\frac{1}{q_{2}}+\frac{1}{q_{3}}=1
.
Then

\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\partial_{i}f\cdot g_{i}\,\phi\lesssim\|(-\Delta)^{\frac{s_{1}}{2}}\phi\|_{L^{(p_{1},q_{1})}}\|(-\Delta)^{\frac{s_{2}}{2}}f\|_{L^{(p_{2},q_{2})}}\|I^{1-s_{3}}g\|_{L^{(p_{3},q_{3})}}.

\square

For our method, it is advantageous to code the functions as differential forms, since this opens up Stokes’ Theorem as a means to obtain the integral over
{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}
.
Using Stokes’ Theorem instead of a simpler integration by parts already makes use of the “div-curl” expression’s inherent structure and therefore replaces the cancellation effects we would otherwise observe in more detail running through the elementary transformations.

Clearly,
f
can be interpreted as
0
-form, coding
\nabla f
as
df
.
Likewise,
g
can be interpreted as
(n-1)
-form,
g\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}\left(\bigwedge^{n-1}{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\right)
.
By suitably choosing the signs for the components of
g
in the encoding, we can then rewrite the left side of the estimates as

\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\partial_{i}f\cdot g_{i}\,\phi=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\phi\,df\wedge g.

Due to the way we chose the signs for
g
, we can identify the divergence with the exterior derivative, and therefore have
dg=0
.
Thus, by the Poincaré Lemma on differential forms, there is an
(n-2)
-form
h\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}\left(\bigwedge^{n-2}{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\right)
such that
g=dh
.

In the language of differential forms, the above theorem can be stated as follows.

Theorem
.

Let
l\in\{0,\ldots,n-2\}
.
Assume
\phi\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
,
f\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(\bigwedge^{l}{\mathbb{R}^{n}})
and
h\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(\bigwedge^{n-l-2}{\mathbb{R}^{n}})
.

Suppose
1<p_{1},p_{2}<\infty
,
1\leq q_{1},q_{2}\leq\infty
with
\frac{1}{p_{1}}+\frac{1}{p_{2}}=\frac{1}{q_{1}}+\frac{1}{q_{2}}=1
.
Then

\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}\phi\,df\wedge dh\lesssim[\phi]_{BMO}\|\nabla f\|_{L^{(p_{1},q_{1})}}\|\nabla h\|_{L^{(p_{2},q_{2})}}.

(2.2.2)

The second, intermediate estimate has the following form.
Suppose
0<s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}<1
with
s_{1}+s_{2}+s_{3}=2
and
1<p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}<\infty
,
1\leq q_{1},q_{2},q_{3}\leq\infty
with
\frac{1}{p_{1}}+\frac{1}{p_{2}}+\frac{1}{p_{3}}=\frac{1}{q_{1}}+\frac{1}{q_{2}}+\frac{1}{q_{3}}=1
.
Then

\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}\phi\,df\wedge dh\lesssim\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s_{1}}{2}}}\phi\|_{L^{(p_{1},q_{1})}}\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s_{2}}{2}}}f\|_{L^{(p_{2},q_{2})}}\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s_{3}}{2}}}h\|_{L^{(p_{3},q_{3})}}.

(2.2.3)

\square

Before proving the theorem, let us briefly explain the norms in (
2.2.2
) and (
2.2.3
).
We can write any
l
-form
f
as

f=\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\ldots<i_{l}\leq n}f_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{l}}\,dx^{i_{1}}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx^{i_{l}}.

We can extend all of our norms to
l
-forms by applying them to
f_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{l}}
.
In particular, we have

\|\nabla f\|_{L^{(p,q)}}\coloneqq\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\ldots<i_{l}\leq n}\|\nabla f_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{l}}\|_{L^{(p,q)}}

and

\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f\|_{L^{(p,q)}}\coloneqq\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\ldots<i_{l}\leq n}\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{l}}\|_{L^{(p,q)}}.

In the same manner, we may apply the Poisson operator
P^{1}_{t}
to differential forms,

P^{1}_{t}f\coloneqq\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\ldots<i_{l}\leq n}P^{1}_{t}f_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{l}}\,dx^{i_{1}}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx^{i_{l}}.

Then
F(x,t)=P^{1}_{t}f(x)
is an
l
-form on
{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}
.

Notice that in general we only have

\|g\|_{L^{(p,q)}}=\|dh\|_{L^{(p,q)}}\lesssim\|\nabla h\|_{L^{(p,q)}}

and

\|I^{1-s}g\|_{L^{(p,q)}}=\|d(I^{1-s}h)\|_{L^{(p,q)}}\lesssim\|\nabla I^{1-s}h\|_{L^{(p,q)}}\approx\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}I^{1-s}h\|_{L^{(p,q)}}=\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}h\|_{L^{(p,q)}}.

Therefore, Theorem
Theorem
clearly implies Theorem
Theorem
for
l=0
.
The other direction is not as obvious.
However, one can argue with the Gaffney inequality that Theorem
Theorem
indeed also implies Theorem
Theorem
.
We will not go into further detail though, since these considerations are out of our original scope.

Proof
(Theorem
Theorem
).

Let
\Phi(x,t)=P^{1}_{t}\phi(x)
,
F(x,t)=P^{1}_{t}f(x)
and
H(x,t)=P^{1}_{t}h(x)
be the harmonic extensions of
\phi
,
f
,
h
.
By Stokes’ Theorem on differential forms, see Theorem
Theorem
, we have

\mathcal{I}\coloneqq\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\phi\,df\wedge dh\,\right|=\left|\int_{\partial{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}\Phi\,dF\wedge dH\,\right|=\left|\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}d\Phi\wedge dF\wedge dH\,\right|.

The integrability conditions for Theorem
Theorem
are met due to
\phi,f,h\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
and the decay and boundedness of the harmonic extensions, see Corollary
Corollary
and Corollary
Corollary
.

Regarding the intermediate estimate, we have

\mathcal{I}\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\Phi|\,|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}F|\,|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}H|=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{2-s_{1}-s_{2}-s_{3}}|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\Phi|\,|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}F|\,|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}H|.

Thus, (
2.2.3
) follows with Proposition
Proposition
(a,b).

In order to obtain (
2.2.2
) from this term with Proposition
Proposition
, we need another derivative on either
|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}F|
or
|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}H|
.
An integration by parts in
t
grants this additional derivative.
For an integrable function
E\in\mathcal{C}^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}})\cap L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
with integrable derivatives and
\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}tE(x,t)=0
we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}E(x,t)\,dx\,dt=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left[tE(x,t)\right]_{0}^{\infty}dx-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t\,\partial_{t}E(x,t)\,\,dx\,dt=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t\,\partial_{t}E(x,t)\,\,dx\,dt

Applying this formula to
\mathcal{I}
, we have

\mathcal{I}=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t\,\partial_{t}\left(d\Phi\wedge dF\wedge dH\right)\,\right|

due to the decay and the boundedness of the harmonic extensions.
We claim that

\mathcal{I}\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t\,|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\Phi|\,\left(|\nabla_{x}\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}F|\,|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}H|+|\nabla_{x}\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}H|\,|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}F|\right).

(2.2.4)

Essentially, we will show that we might redistribute the additional derivative if it hits
\Phi
, and replace the second
t
-derivative with
x
-derivatives. Then (
2.2.2
) follows with Proposition
Proposition
.

We rename the variables to
(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n+1})
from
(x_{1},\ldots x_{n},t)
and have

d\Phi\wedge dF\wedge dH=\sum_{k,i,j=1}^{n+1}\sum_{I,J}\partial_{z_{k}}\Phi\,\partial_{z_{i}}F_{I}\,\partial_{z_{j}}H_{J}\,dz^{k}\wedge dz^{i}\wedge dz^{I}\wedge dz^{j}\wedge dz^{J}

where the second sum is over all
I=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{l})
with
i_{1}<i_{2}<\ldots<i_{l}
and
J=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{n-2-l})
with
j_{1}<j_{2}<\ldots<j_{n-2-l}
.
Distributing the
t
-derivative, we obtain three terms,

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\partial_{t}(d\Phi\wedge dF\wedge dH)&=&\sum_{k,i,j=1}^{n+1}\sum_{I,J}\partial_{z_{k}}\Phi\,\partial_{z_{n+1}}\left(\partial_{z_{i}}F_{I}\,\partial_{z_{j}}H_{J}\right)\,dz^{k}\wedge dz^{i}\wedge dz^{I}\wedge dz^{j}\wedge dz^{J}\\
&&+\sum_{i,j=1}^{n+1}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{I,J}\partial_{z_{n+1}}\partial_{z_{k}}\Phi\,\partial_{z_{i}}F_{I}\,\partial_{z_{j}}H_{J}\,dz^{k}\wedge dz^{i}\wedge dz^{I}\wedge dz^{j}\wedge dz^{J}\\
&&+\sum_{i,j=1}^{n+1}\sum_{I,J}\partial_{z_{n+1}}\partial_{z_{n+1}}\Phi\,\partial_{z_{i}}F_{I}\,\partial_{z_{j}}H_{J}\,dz^{n+1}\wedge dz^{i}\wedge dz^{I}\wedge dz^{j}\wedge dz^{J}\\
&\eqqcolon&\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{2}+\mathcal{J}_{3}.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

With the harmonicity of the extensions in mind, i.e.
\partial_{z_{n+1}}\partial_{z_{n+1}}F_{I}=-\sum_{r=1}^{n}\partial_{z_{r}}\partial_{z_{r}}F_{I}
, we see that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t\mathcal{J}_{1}
can already be estimated as in (
2.2.4
).
Regarding the second term, we have
z_{k}=x_{k}
and can therefore redistribute the additional
z_{k}
-derivative via an integration by parts in
z_{k}
since both boundary terms disappear, see Corollary
Corollary
.

\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t\mathcal{J}_{2}\right|=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}z_{n+1}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n+1}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{I,J}\partial_{z_{n+1}}\Phi\,\partial_{z_{k}}\left(\partial_{z_{i}}F_{I}\,\partial_{z_{j}}H_{J}\right)\,dz^{k}\wedge dz^{i}\wedge dz^{I}\wedge dz^{j}\wedge dz^{J}\,\right|

Obviously, this term can be estimated as in (
2.2.4
).
For the third term, we use the harmonicity of
\Phi
, that is
\partial_{z_{n+1}}\partial_{z_{n+1}}\Phi=-\sum_{r=1}^{n}\partial_{z_{r}}\partial_{z_{r}}\Phi
.
With an integration by parts in
z_{r}=x_{r}
we obtain

\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t\mathcal{J}_{3}\right|=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}z_{n+1}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n+1}\sum_{I,J}\sum_{r=1}^{n}\partial_{z_{r}}\Phi\,\partial_{z_{r}}\left(\partial_{z_{i}}F_{I}\,\partial_{z_{j}}H_{J}\right)\,dz^{n+1}\wedge dz^{i}\wedge dz^{I}\wedge dz^{j}\wedge dz^{J}\,\right|

and have therefore shown that
\mathcal{I}
can be estimated just as we claimed.

\square

2.3  The Coifman-Rochberg-Weiss commutator estimate

In this section, we examine the commutators between pointwise multiplication and the Riesz transforms
{\mathcal{R}_{j}}
,
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
For
\phi\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
, the commutator
[{\mathcal{R}_{j}},\phi]
is defined by

[{\mathcal{R}_{j}},\phi](f)\coloneqq{\mathcal{R}_{j}}[\phi f]-\phi{\mathcal{R}_{j}}[f],\qquad f\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

Coifman-Rochberg-Weiss showed that these commutators are bounded operators, mapping
L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
,
1<p<\infty
, onto itself with
[\phi]_{BMO}
providing an upper bound for the operator norm.
They did not only prove this for the Riesz transforms, but for all Calderón-Zygmund operators, see
[
CRW76
, Section 2, Theorem 1, p.613]
.
While we only prove this fact for the Riesz transforms, due to our method we easily obtain two additional intermediate estimates.

Actually, the div-curl estimate (
2.2.1
) and therefore also the Jacobian estimate (
1.1.1
) are special cases of the following
BMO
-estimate, see
[
CLMS93
, Section III.1, pp.257-258]
.

Theorem
(Coifman-Rochberg-Weiss).

Let
\phi,f\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
and
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
Suppose
p\in(1,\infty)
. Then

\|[{\mathcal{R}_{j}},\phi](f)\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\lesssim[\phi]_{BMO}\|f\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

(2.3.1)

Moreover, for
p\in(1,\infty)
,
\sigma\in(0,1)

\|[{\mathcal{R}_{j}},\phi](f)\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\lesssim[{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\sigma}{2}}}\phi]_{BMO}\|I^{\sigma}f\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

(2.3.2)

Suppose
p,p_{1},p_{2}\in(1,\infty)
and
q_{1},q_{2}\in[1,\infty]
with
\frac{1}{p_{1}}+\frac{1}{p_{2}}=\frac{1}{q_{1}}+\frac{1}{q_{2}}=\frac{1}{p}
and
\sigma\in[0,1)
. Then

\|[{\mathcal{R}_{j}},\phi](f)\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\lesssim\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\sigma}{2}}}\phi\|_{L^{(p_{1},q_{1})}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\|I^{\sigma}f\|_{L^{(p_{2},q_{2})}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

(2.3.3)

\square

The definition of the Riesz transforms and a collection of results regarding these operators can be found in Subsection
4.1
.
Next to these results, we will use some specific interactions between Riesz transforms and the harmonic extension for the proof of the above theorem.

Lemma
.

Let
f\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
and
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
Denote with
F(x,t)=P^{1}_{t}f(x)
the harmonic extension of
f
.
We write
\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[F](x,t)\coloneqq P^{1}_{t}{\mathcal{R}_{j}}f
.
Then

\partial_{t}\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[F]=\partial_{x_{j}}F,\qquad\partial_{tt}\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[F]=\partial_{t}\partial_{x_{j}}F,\qquad\Delta_{x}\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[F]=-\partial_{t}\partial_{x_{j}}F.

\square

Remark
.

Note that when considering the Poisson operator
P^{1}_{t}
for fixed
t>0
, since they are Fourier multipliers,
P^{1}_{t}
and the
n
-dimensional Riesz transform
{\mathcal{R}_{j}}
commutate.
We connect this with a brief warning: While
\mathcal{\tilde{R}}_{j}
can be interpreted as operator acting on
{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}
, this is not an actual
(n+1)
-dimensional Riesz transform.

\square

Proof
(Lemma
Lemma
).

Due to Lemma
Lemma
we have

\partial_{t}\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[F](x,t)=-P^{1}_{t}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\mathcal{R}_{j}}f)=P^{1}_{t}(\partial_{x_{j}}f)=\partial_{x_{j}}F,

where we used that
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\mathcal{R}_{j}}f=-\partial_{x_{j}}f
.
This can easily be seen for the Fourier transforms.
The Fourier symbol of
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}
is
2\pi|\xi|
while the symbol of
{\mathcal{R}_{j}}
is
-i\frac{\xi_{j}}{|\xi|}
, see Definition
Definition
and Proposition
Proposition
. Thus,

{\mathcal{F}}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\mathcal{R}_{j}}f)(\xi)=-2\pi i|\xi|\frac{\xi_{j}}{|\xi|}{\mathcal{F}}(f)(\xi)=-2\pi i\xi_{j}{\mathcal{F}}(f)={\mathcal{F}}(-\partial_{x_{j}}f)(\xi).

The second equation of the lemma immediately follows from the first equation, the third by harmonicity from the second.

\square

Equipped with these additional tools, we can now prove Theorem
Theorem
.

Proof
(Theorem
Theorem
).

By
L^{p}
-duality, it suffices to show that for
p^{\prime}\in(1,\infty)
with
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}=1
and for any
g\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}

we have

\mathcal{I}\coloneqq\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}[{\mathcal{R}_{j}},\phi](f)\,g\,\right|\lesssim\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{[}\phi{]}_{BMO}\,\|f\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\,\|g\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})},\\
{[}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\sigma}{2}}}\phi{]}_{BMO}\,\|I^{\sigma}f\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\,\|g\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})},\\
\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\sigma}{2}}}\phi\|_{L^{(p_{1},q_{1})}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\,\|I^{\sigma}f\|_{L^{(p_{2},q_{2})}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\,\|g\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.\end{array}\right.

(2.3.4)

Let
\Phi(x,t)=P^{1}_{t}\phi(x)
,
F(x,t)=P^{1}_{t}f(x)
,
G(x,t)=P^{1}_{t}g(x)
be the harmonic extensions of
\phi,f,g
.
Due to Lemma
Lemma
, the integration-by-parts property of the Riesz transforms, we have

\mathcal{I}=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}{\mathcal{R}_{j}}[\phi f]g-\phi{\mathcal{R}_{j}}[f]g\right|=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\phi f{\mathcal{R}_{j}}[g]+\phi{\mathcal{R}_{j}}[f]g\right|

Applying integration by parts twice in
t
-direction, we obtain

\mathcal{I}=\left|\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}\partial_{t}\left(\Phi F\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[G]+\Phi\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[F]G\right)\right|=\left|\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}t\partial_{tt}\left(\Phi F\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[G]+\Phi\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[F]G\right)\right|

since for all
x\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
the harmonic extensions satisfy the boundary condition of the Dirichlet problem,

\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}\left(\Phi F\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[G]+\Phi\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[F]G\right)(x,t)=\phi(x)f(x){\mathcal{R}_{j}}[g](x)+\phi(x){\mathcal{R}_{j}}[f](x)g(x).

All other boundary terms disappear since due to Corollary
Corollary
and Corollary
Corollary
we have

\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\left(\Phi F\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[G]+\Phi\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[F]G\right)(x,t)=0,

\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}t\partial_{t}\left(\Phi F\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[G]+\Phi\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[F]G\right)(x,t)=\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}t\partial_{t}\left(\Phi F\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[G]+\Phi\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[F]G\right)(x,t)=0.

For the rest of the proof, we will establish that

\mathcal{I}\lesssim\max_{\tilde{F}\in\{F,\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}F\}}\max_{\tilde{G}\in\{G,\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}G\}}t|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}\Phi|\,\left(|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}\tilde{F}|\,|\tilde{G}|+|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}\tilde{G}|\,|\tilde{F}|\right).

(2.3.5)

Then due to Proposition
Proposition
, the
L^{p}
-boundedness of the Riesz transforms, we immediately obtain (
2.3.4
) with Proposition
Proposition
(c,d), Proposition
Proposition
(e,f,g) and Proposition
Proposition
(a,b) respectively.
For (
2.3.3
) we need the
L^{(p,q)}
-boundedness of the Riesz transforms, which we easily obtain from the
L^{p}
-boundedness via interpolation, see Theorem
Theorem
.

Let us prove (
2.3.5
).
Distributing the two
t
-derivatives, we obtain three terms,

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\mathcal{I}&\leq&\left|\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}t\left(\partial_{t}\Phi\,\partial_{t}(F\,\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[G])+\partial_{t}\Phi\,\partial_{t}(\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[F]\,G)\right)\right|\\
&&+\left|\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}t\left(\partial_{tt}\Phi\,(F\,\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[G]+\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[F]\,G)\right)\right|+\left|\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}t\left(\Phi\,\partial_{tt}(F\,\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[G]+\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[F]\,G)\right)\right|\\
&&\eqqcolon\mathcal{I}_{1}+\mathcal{I}_{2}+\mathcal{I}_{3}.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

The term
\mathcal{I}_{1}
can already be estimated as in (
2.3.5
) by further distributing the derivative to
F
and
G
.
For the second term, we apply the harmonicity of the extensions.
With
\partial_{tt}\Phi=-\Delta_{x}\Phi=-\nabla_{x}\cdot\nabla_{x}\Phi
we have

\mathcal{I}_{2}=\left|\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}t\left(\Delta_{x}\Phi(F\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[G]+\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[F]G)\right)\right|=\left|\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}t\left(\nabla_{x}\Phi\cdot\nabla_{x}(F\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[G]+\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[F]G)\right)\right|

Again, by further distributing the derivatives we can estimate this term as in (
2.3.5
).

Regarding the third term, we will observe multiple cancellation effects. The goal is to isolate additional
x
-derivatives, which we then may redistribute to
\Phi
via partial integration.
Further distributing the derivatives, we have

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LCL}\vskip 4.30554pt\mathcal{I}_{3}&=&\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}t\Phi\left(\partial_{t}\left(F\partial_{t}\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[G]+\partial_{t}\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[F]G\right)+\partial_{t}\left(\partial_{t}F\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[G]+\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[F]\partial_{t}G\right)\right)\right|\\
&\eqqcolon&\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}t\Phi(\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{2})\right|\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

After applying Lemma
Lemma
, we observe the first cancellation effect;

\mathcal{J}_{1}=\partial_{t}\left(F\,\partial_{x_{j}}G+\partial_{x_{j}}F\,G\right)=\partial_{t}\partial_{x_{j}}(FG)=\partial_{x_{j}}\partial_{t}(FG).

For the second integrand, with
\partial_{tt}F=-\Delta_{x}F
and again Lemma
Lemma
we obtain

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\mathcal{J}_{2}&=&-\Delta_{x}F\,\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[G]-\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[F]\,\Delta_{x}G+\partial_{t}F\,\partial_{x_{j}}G+\partial_{x_{j}}F\,\partial_{t}G\\
&=&-\Delta_{x}F\,\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[G]-\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[F]\,\Delta_{x}G+\partial_{x_{j}}(\partial_{t}F\,G+F\,\partial_{t}G)+\Delta_{x}\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[F]\,G+F\,\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[G].\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

Thanks to the cancellation of the term
\nabla_{x}F\cdot\nabla_{x}\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[G]+\nabla_{x}\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[F]\cdot\nabla_{x}G
we have

\mathcal{J}_{2}=-\nabla_{x}\cdot\left(\nabla_{x}F\,\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[G]+\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[F]\,\nabla_{x}G\right)+\nabla_{x}\cdot\left(\nabla_{x}\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[F]\,G+F\,\nabla_{x}\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[G]\right)+\partial_{x_{j}}(\partial_{t}F\,G+F\,\partial_{t}G).

Combining the calculations of
\mathcal{J}_{1}
and
\mathcal{J}_{2}
,

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\mathcal{J}_{1}+\mathcal{J}_{2}&=&\partial_{x_{j}}\partial_{t}(F\,G+\partial_{t}F\,G+F\,\partial_{t}G)\\
&&+\nabla_{x}\cdot\left(-\nabla_{x}F\,\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[G]-\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[F]\,\nabla_{x}G+\nabla_{x}\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[F]\,G+F\,\nabla_{x}\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}[G]\right).\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

Plugging this into
\mathcal{I}_{3}
, via integration by parts in
x
-direction we can redistribute
\partial_{x_{j}}
and
\nabla_{x}
to
\Phi
since the boundary terms disappear due to decay of the harmonic extensions, confer Corollary
Corollary
.
Thus, we estimate
\mathcal{I}_{3}
as in (
2.3.5
).

\square

2.4  1-dimensional
L^{1}
-estimate for a double-commutator

In the previous subsection, we were not able to obtain an
L^{1}
-estimate for the commutator
[{\mathcal{R}_{j}},\phi](f)
.
This provides us with an opportunity to showcase an application for the interaction between harmonic extensions and fractional Laplacians. In this subsection, we will prove a replacement estimate, where instead of
[{\mathcal{R}_{j}},\phi](f)
itself we estimate the commutators of these commutators.
We only obtain this replacement estimate in the one-dimensional case
n=1
though, since we will us some properties that are exclusive to the
1
-dimensional Riesz transform
\mathcal{R}_{1}
.
This
1
-dimensional Riesz transform is called the Hilbert transform, for which we write
\mathcal{H}
.

Regarding the estimates in Theorem
Theorem
, the proof for an
L^{1}
-version of (
2.3.1
) and (
2.3.2
) already fails since the trace theorems from Section
5
only allow to estimate against
\|f\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
for
p>1
.
This is not a concern for (
2.3.3
) though, since we could still choose
p_{1},p_{2}\in(1,\infty)
.
However, we also do not have the
L^{\infty}
-boundedness of the Riesz transforms, which we would need to obtain
\|g\|_{L^{\infty}}
from (
2.3.5
).
Therefore, while the theorem below at first glance might look like an easy consequence from (
2.3.3
), with the proof in Subsection
2.3
we can not obtain a result which covers the estimates below.

Theorem
.

Assume
\phi,f\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
.
Suppose
s_{1},s_{2}\in(0,1)
with
s_{1}+s_{2}=1
and
p,p^{\prime}\in(1,\infty)
,
q,q^{\prime}\in[1,\infty]
with
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{q^{\prime}}=1
.
Then

\|[{\mathcal{H}},\phi]({(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}f)-[{\mathcal{H}},f]({(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\phi)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s_{1}}{2}}}\phi\|_{L^{(p,q)}(\mathbb{R})}\,\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s_{2}}{2}}}f\|_{L^{(p^{\prime},q^{\prime})}(\mathbb{R})}

(2.4.1)

and

\left\|{\mathcal{H}}\left([{\mathcal{H}},\phi]({(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}f)+[{\mathcal{H}},f]({(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\phi)\right)\right\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s_{1}}{2}}}\phi\|_{L^{(p,q)}(\mathbb{R})}\,\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s_{2}}{2}}}f\|_{L^{(p^{\prime},q^{\prime})}(\mathbb{R})}.

(2.4.2)

\square

Just as for the Riesz transforms, given the harmonic extension
F(x,t)=P^{1}_{t}f(x)
of
f\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
we denote
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}F(x,t)=P^{1}_{t}\mathcal{H}f(x)
.
Before the main proof, we collect some additional facts about the Hilbert transform.

Lemma
.

Let
f\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
.
Then

\mathcal{H}\mathcal{H}f=-f.

Denote with
F(x,t)=P^{1}_{t}f
the harmonic extension of f.
Then

F_{t}\equiv\partial_{t}F=-\tilde{\mathcal{H}}F_{x}.

These two properties specifically hold for
\mathcal{H}
.
Of course, as in Lemma
Lemma
we also have

\tilde{\mathcal{H}}F_{t}=F_{x}.

\square

Proof
(Lemma
Lemma
).

The first equation is due to the Fourier symbol of
\mathcal{H}
.
With Proposition
Proposition
we have

{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{H}\mathcal{H}f)(\xi)=\left(-i\frac{\xi}{|\xi|}\right)\left(-i\frac{\xi}{|\xi|}\right){\mathcal{F}}(f)(\xi)=-\frac{\xi^{2}}{|\xi|^{2}}{\mathcal{F}}(f)(\xi)={\mathcal{F}}(-f)(\xi).

Regarding the second equation, due to Lemma
Lemma
we have

F_{t}(x,t)=-P^{1}_{t}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}f)(x)

as well as

\tilde{\mathcal{H}}F_{x}(x,t)=P^{1}_{t}(\mathcal{H}\partial_{x}f)(x)=P^{1}_{t}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}f)(x).

The last equation can easily be verified on the side of the Fourier transforms;

{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{H}\partial_{x}f)=\left(-i\frac{\xi}{|\xi|}\right)(2\pi i\xi){\mathcal{F}}(f)(\xi)=2\pi\frac{\xi^{2}}{|\xi|}{\mathcal{F}}(f)(\xi)={\mathcal{F}}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}f)(\xi).

As already mentioned above, see Lemma
Lemma
for the last equation.

\square

Proof
(Estimate (
2.4.1
)).

Let
g\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
.
Redistributing the Hilbert transform to
g
with Lemma
Lemma
, we obtain

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LCL}\vskip 4.30554pt\mathcal{I}&\coloneqq&\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left([{\mathcal{H}},\phi]({(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}f)-[{\mathcal{H}},f]({(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\phi)\right)g\,\right|\\
&=&\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\phi\,{\mathcal{H}}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}f\,g+\phi\,{(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}f\,{\mathcal{H}}g-{\mathcal{H}}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\phi\,f\,g-{(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\phi\,f\,{\mathcal{H}}g\,\right|.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

In order to prove (
2.4.1
), by
L^{1}
-duality it suffices to show that

\mathcal{I}\lesssim\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s_{1}}{2}}}\phi\|_{L^{(p,q)}(\mathbb{R})}\,\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s_{2}}{2}}}f\|_{L^{(p^{\prime},q^{\prime})}(\mathbb{R})}\,\|g\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}.

(2.4.3)

As usual, let
\Phi(x,t)=P^{1}_{t}\phi(x)
,
F(x,t)=P^{1}_{t}f(x)
,
G(x,t)=P^{1}_{t}g(x)
be the harmonic extensions of
\phi,f,g
.
Now, regarding the integration by parts, the situation differs a little bit from what we have grown used to.
There are fractional Laplacians in the boundary term.
Recalling Lemma
Lemma
though, these can be viewed as the limits of the harmonic extension’s
t
-derivatives.
Therefore, via integration by parts in
t
direction,

\mathcal{I}=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}\partial_{t}\left(\Phi\,\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}F_{t}\,G+\Phi\,F_{t}\,\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}G-\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}\Phi_{t}\,F\,G-\Phi_{t}\,F\,\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}G\right)\right|\eqqcolon\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}\mathcal{J}\,\right|.

Of course, the other boundary term vanishes due to Corollary
Corollary
.
We claim that

\mathcal{I}\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Phi||\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}F||G|=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}t^{1-s_{1}-s_{2}}|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Phi||\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}F||G|.

(2.4.4)

Then (
2.4.1
) follows with Proposition
Proposition
.
Therefore, during the distribution of the additional derivative, the goal is to replace the occurring Hilbert transforms via Lemma
Lemma
whenever possible, hoping that the remaining terms with Hilbert transforms cancel each other out.
Applying Lemma
Lemma
, we obtain

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\mathcal{J}&=&\partial_{t}\left(\Phi\,F_{x}\,G+\Phi\,F_{t}\,\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}G-\Phi_{x}\,F\,G-\Phi_{t}\,F\,\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}G\right)\\
&=&\partial_{t}\left(\Phi\,F_{x}\,G-\Phi_{x}\,F\,G\right)+\partial_{t}\left(\Phi\,F_{t}\,\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}G-\Phi_{t}\,F\,\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}G\right).\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

Distributing the derivative, we observe the cancellation of the term
\Phi_{t}\,F_{t}\,\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}G
in the second group.

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\mathcal{J}&=&\left(\Phi_{t}\,F_{x}\,G-\Phi_{x}\,F_{t}\,G\right)+\left(\Phi\,F_{xt}\,G-\Phi_{xt}\,F\,G\right)+\left(\Phi\,F_{x}\,G_{t}-\Phi_{x}\,F\,G_{t}\right)\\
&&+\left(\Phi\,F_{tt}\,\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}G-\Phi_{tt}\,F\,\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}G\right)+\left(\Phi\,F_{t}\,\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}G_{t}-\Phi_{t}\,F\,\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}G_{t}\right)\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

Applying Lemma
Lemma
as well as the harmonicity of the extensions, i.e.
\partial_{tt}F=-\partial_{xx}F
, we have

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\mathcal{J}&=&\left(\Phi_{t}\,F_{x}\,G-\Phi_{x}\,F_{t}\,G\right)+\left(\Phi\,F_{xt}\,G-\Phi_{xt}\,F\,G\right)+\left(\Phi\,F_{x}\,G_{t}-\Phi_{x}\,F\,G_{t}\right)\\
&&+\left(-\Phi\,F_{xx}\,\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}G+\Phi_{xx}\,F\,\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}G\right)+\left(\Phi\,F_{t}\,G_{x}-\Phi_{t}\,F\,G_{x}\right).\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

We can rewrite the terms of the second to last group as

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}RCL}\vskip 4.30554pt-\Phi\,F_{xx}\,\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}G&=&-(\Phi\,F_{x}\,\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}G)_{x}+\Phi_{x}\,F_{x}\,\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}G-\Phi\,F_{x}\,G_{t}\\
\vskip 4.30554pt\Phi_{xx}\,F\,\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}G&=&(\Phi_{x}\,F\,\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}G)_{x}-\Phi_{x}\,F_{x}\,\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}G+\Phi_{x}\,F\,G_{t}\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

where for the last term we applied Lemma
Lemma
respectively.
Plugging these terms back into
\mathcal{J}
, we observe the cancellations of
\Phi_{x}\,F_{x}\,\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}G
as well as
\Phi\,F_{x}\,G_{t}
and
\Phi_{x}\,F\,G_{t}
;

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\mathcal{J}&=&\left(\Phi_{t}\,F_{x}\,G-\Phi_{x}\,F_{t}\,G\right)+\left(\Phi\,F_{xt}\,G-\Phi_{xt}\,F\,G\right)\\
&&+\left(-(\Phi\,F_{x}\,\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}G)_{x}+(\Phi_{x}\,F\,\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}G)_{x}\right)+\left(\Phi\,F_{t}\,G_{x}-\Phi_{t}\,F\,G_{x}\right).\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

Repeating this reorganization for the other group with double derivatives on one function,

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}RCL}\vskip 4.30554pt\Phi\,F_{xt}\,G&=&(\Phi\,F_{t}\,G)_{x}-\Phi_{x}\,F_{t}\,G-\Phi\,F_{t}\,G_{x}\\
\vskip 4.30554pt-\Phi_{xt}\,F\,G&=&-(\Phi_{t}\,F\,G)_{x}+\Phi_{t}\,F_{x}\,G+\Phi_{t}\,F\,G_{x}.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

Thus, with the cancellation of
\Phi\,F_{t}\,G_{x}
and
\Phi_{t}\,F\,G_{x}
,

\mathcal{J}=2\left(\Phi_{t}\,F_{x}\,G-\Phi_{x}\,F_{t}\,G\right)+\left(\Phi\,F_{t}\,G-\Phi_{x}\,F\,\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}G-\Phi\,F_{x}\,\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}G+\Phi_{x}\,F\,\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}G\right)_{x}.

The second term vanishes when integrating in
x
due to the decay estimates for harmonic extensions and Riesz transforms, see Corollary
Corollary
and Lemma
Lemma
.
Therefore, when we plug
\mathcal{J}
back into (
Proof
), we get

\mathcal{I}=2\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\Phi_{t}\,F_{x}\,G-\Phi_{x}\,F_{t}\,G\,\right|=2\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\det\left(\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Phi,\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}F\right)G\,\right|.

Concluding this proof, we can estimate
\mathcal{I}
as in (
2.4.4
) and then obtain (
2.4.3
) and therefore (
2.4.1
) with Proposition
Proposition
(a,b).

\square

Proof
(Estimate (
2.4.2
)).

Let
g\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
.
Applying the double commutator to
g
, with Lemma
Lemma
and
{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{H}}=-Id
we obtain

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LCL}\vskip 4.30554pt\mathcal{I}&\coloneqq&\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{H}}\left([{\mathcal{H}},\phi]({(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}f)+[{\mathcal{H}},f]({(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\phi)\right)g\,\right|\\
&=&\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left([{\mathcal{H}},\phi]({(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}f)+[{\mathcal{H}},f]({(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\phi)\right){\mathcal{H}}g\,\right|\\
&=&\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi\,{\mathcal{H}}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}f\,{\mathcal{H}}g-\phi\,{(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}f\,g+{\mathcal{H}}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\phi\,f\,{\mathcal{H}}g-{(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\phi\,f\,g\,\right|.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

In order to prove (
2.4.2
), again by duality, it suffices to show that

\mathcal{I}\lesssim\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s_{1}}{2}}}\phi\|_{L^{(p,q)}(\mathbb{R})}\,\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s_{2}}{2}}}f\|_{L^{(p^{\prime},q^{\prime})}(\mathbb{R})}\,\|g\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}.

(2.4.5)

Denote with
\Phi(x,t)=P^{1}_{t}\phi(x)
,
F(x,t)=P^{1}_{t}f(x)
,
G(x,t)=P^{1}_{t}g(x)
the harmonic extensions.
As in the proof of (
2.4.1
), with integration by parts in
t
and due to the interaction of fractional Laplacian and
t
-derivatives,

\mathcal{I}=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\partial_{t}\left(\Phi\,\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}F_{t}\,\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}G-\Phi\,F_{t}\,G+\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}\Phi_{t}\,F\,\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}G-\Phi_{t}\,F\,G\right)\right|\eqqcolon\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\mathcal{J}\right|.

Before distributing the additional
t
-derivative, with Lemma
Lemma
we obtain

\mathcal{J}=\partial_{t}\left(\Phi\,F_{x}\,\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}G-\Phi\,F_{t}\,G+\Phi_{x}\,F\,\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}G-\Phi_{t}\,F\,G\right).

Again, we try to write multiple terms as single
x
-derivative, which will vanish when plugging
\mathcal{J}
into
\mathcal{I}
. With Lemma
Lemma
, more specifically
\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}G_{x}=-G_{t}
,

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\mathcal{J}&=&\partial_{t}\left((\Phi\,F)_{x}\,\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}G\right)-\partial_{t}\left((\Phi\,F)_{t}\,G\right)\\
&=&\left(\Phi\,F\,\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}G\right)_{xt}-\partial_{t}\left(-(\Phi\,F)G_{t}\right)-\partial_{t}\left((\Phi\,F)_{t}\,G\right).\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

Due to the cancellation of the term
(F\,G)_{t}\,\Phi_{t}
and then with harmonicity,

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\mathcal{J}&=\left(\Phi\,F\,\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}G\right)_{xt}+(\Phi\,F)\,G_{tt}-(\Phi\,F)_{tt}\,G\\
&=\left(\Phi\,F\,\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}G\right)_{xt}-(\Phi\,F)\,G_{xx}-(\Phi\,F)_{tt}\,G.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

As mentioned above, the first term vanishes when integrating in
x
due to the decay estimates for the harmonic extension and the Riesz transforms, see Corollary
Corollary
and Lemma
Lemma
.
Via a double integration by parts in
x
-direction we obtain

\mathcal{I}=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}(\Phi\,F)\,G_{xx}+(\Phi\,F)_{tt}\,G\,\right|=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}(\Phi\,F)_{xx}\,G+(\Phi\,F)_{tt}\,G\,\right|=2\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Phi\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}F\,G\,\right|

since
(\Phi\,F)_{xx}+(\Phi\,F)_{tt}=2\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Phi\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}F
due to harmonicity.
Therefore, we have

\mathcal{I}\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Phi|\,|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}F|\,|G|=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}t^{1-s_{1}-s_{2}}|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Phi|\,|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}F|\,|G|

and thus obtain (
2.4.5
) with Proposition
Proposition
(a,b).

\square

3  Commutator estimates via
s
-harmonic extension

In Subsection
2.4
, we just used the harmonic extension’s behavior near the boundary, that is
\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}F^{1}(x,t)=-{(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}f(x)
, to show estimates for a commutator involving the half-Laplacian
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}
.
Modifying the extension such that we instead obtain
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f
as boundary term, might allow us to deal with commutators involving arbitrary fractional Laplacians.
In the first subsection, we introduce the so-called
s
-harmonic extensions, which do just that.
Most of the results from Subsection
2.1
can be transferred to the
s
-harmonic extensions.
Equipped with this new tool, we then prove estimates for four different commutators involving fractional Laplacians and Riesz potentials.

3.1
s
-harmonic extension to
{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}
via the generalized Poisson operator

We obtain the
s
-harmonic extension to
{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}
via a generalized Poisson operator, which is defined as follows.

Definition
(The generalized Poisson operator).

Let
s\in(0,2)
.
The generalized Poisson extension operator
P^{s}_{t}
is given via the convolution

P^{s}_{t}f(x)\coloneqq C_{n,s}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}\frac{t^{s}}{\left(|x-y|^{2}+t^{2}\right)^{\frac{n+s}{2}}}f(y)\,dy=C_{n,s}(p^{s}_{t}*f)(x)

where
f\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})+L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
and the kernel
p^{s}_{t}
is given by

p^{s}_{t}(x)\coloneqq\frac{t^{s}}{\left(|x|^{2}+t^{2}\right)^{\frac{n+s}{2}}}.

\square

The function
F^{s}(x,t)\coloneqq P^{s}_{t}f(x)
is called the
s
-harmonic extension of
f
to
{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}
and, for example for
f\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})\cap\mathcal{C}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
, satisfies the Dirichlet problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{llll}\operatorname{div}_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}(t^{1-s}\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}F^{s}(x,t))&=&0&\text{in }{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}},\\
\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}F^{s}(x,t)&=&f(x)&\text{on }{\mathbb{R}^{n}},\\
\lim_{|(x,t)|\rightarrow\infty}F^{s}(x,t)&=&0.&\end{array}\right.

(3.1.1)

As suggested above, the behavior of
\partial_{t}F^{s}
towards the boundary is of central interest to us.
Caffarelli and Silvestre showed in
[
CS07
]
that

\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}-t^{1-s}\partial_{t}F^{s}(x,t)=c{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f(x)\quad\text{on }{\mathbb{R}^{n}}

(3.1.2)

for a fixed constant
c
depending on
n
and
s
.
