We further observe that, as for the classical harmonic extension, the
p^{s}_{t}
are dilations of
p^{s}_{1}
, i.e.
p^{s}_{t}(x)=t^{-n}p^{s}_{1}(t^{-1}x)=(p^{s}_{1})_{t}(x)
.
Therefore, the boundary condition in (
3.1.1
) is indeed satisfied according to Example 1.2.17 and Theorem 1.2.19 in
[
Gra14
]
if
C_{n,s}
is chosen correctly.
In fact, we have

\|p^{s}_{t}\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}p^{s}_{t}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}p^{s}_{1}\eqqcolon\frac{1}{C_{n,s}}.

Just as we did with the classical Poisson kernel, we might also interpret the kernels
(p^{s}_{t})_{t>0}
as function
k^{s}
on
{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}
with
k^{s}(x,t)\coloneqq p^{s}_{t}(x)
.

These kernels
k^{s}
are
s
-harmonic, meaning they satisfy the partial differential equation in (
3.1.1
),

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\operatorname{div}\left(t^{1-s}\nabla k^{s}(x,t)\right)&=&^{1-s}\Delta_{x}p^{s}_{t}(x)+\partial_{t}(t^{1-s}\partial_{t}k^{s})(x,t)\\
&=&^{1-s}\left((n+s)(n+s+2)\frac{|x|^{2}t^{s}}{(|x|^{2}+t^{2})^{\frac{n+s}{2}+2}}-(n+s)n\frac{t^{s}}{(|x|^{2}+t^{2})^{\frac{n+s}{2}+1}}\right)\\
&&-(2+s)(n+s)\frac{|x|^{2}t}{(|x|^{2}+t^{2})^{\frac{n+s}{2}+1}}+(n+s)n\frac{t^{3}}{(|x|^{2}+t^{2})^{\frac{n+s}{2}+1}}\\
&=&0.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

The extension via the generalized Poisson operator inherits this property.
We can therefore confirm that
F^{s}
satisfies the partial differential equation in (
3.1.1
).
The arguments for this inheritance are analogous to the ones in the proof of the following lemma, which we use to investigate the behavior of
F^{s}
and its derivatives towards infinity in the same fashion as for the classical harmonic extension.

Lemma
.

Let
s\in(0,2)
,
k_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
for
0\leq i\leq n
. Set
\tilde{k}\coloneqq k_{1}+\ldots+k_{n}
and
k\coloneqq\tilde{k}+k_{0}
.
For any
f\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
with
F^{s}(x,t)\coloneqq P^{s}_{t}f(x)
we have

\partial_{t}^{k_{0}}\partial_{x_{1}}^{k_{1}}\dots\partial_{x_{n}}^{k_{n}}F^{s}(x,t)=C_{n,s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{k}\frac{t^{s-k_{0}}q_{j}(x-y)}{(|x-y|^{2}+t^{2})^{\frac{n+s+\tilde{k}+j}{2}}}\right)f(y)\,dy,

where the
q_{j}
are homogeneous polynomials of degree
j
, which depend on
k_{0},\dots,k_{n}
.
In particular, we have
F^{s}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}})
.

\square

Proof
.

We first show that for the derivatives of the kernel we have

\partial_{t}^{k_{0}}\partial_{x_{1}}^{k_{1}}\dots\partial_{x_{n}}^{k_{n}}k^{s}(x,t)=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\frac{t^{s-k_{0}}q_{j}(x)}{(|x|^{2}+t^{2})^{\frac{n+s+\tilde{k}+j}{2}}}.

(3.1.3)

Then the lemma will follow with Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem and via induction.

To this end, we first investigate the interaction between a dilation
f_{t}(x)=t^{-n}f(t^{-1}x)
and the derivatives for a sufficiently differentiable function
f
.
Regarding derivatives in
x
-direction, we have

\nabla_{x}^{k}f_{t}(x)=\nabla_{x}^{k}\left((x,t)\mapsto t^{-n}f(t^{-1}x)\right)(x)=t^{-k}\left(\nabla_{x}^{k}f\right)_{t}(x)

Regarding the derivatives in direction of the the dilation parameter,

\frac{d}{dt}t^{-k}f_{t}(x)=-t^{-n-k-1}\nabla f(t^{-1}x)\cdot(t^{-1}x)-(n+k)t^{-n-k-1}f(t^{-1}x).

Therefore, via induction we obtain

\partial_{t}^{k_{0}}\left((x,t)\mapsto t^{-k}f_{t}(x)\right)=t^{-k-n-l}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k_{0}}c_{\alpha}\partial_{x_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}}\ldots\partial_{x_{n}}^{\alpha_{n}}f(t^{-1}x)(t^{-1}x)^{\alpha}

for some constants
c_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}
depending on
k_{0}
.
Specifically for the kernel
p^{s}_{1}
, we see that

\partial_{x_{1}}^{k_{1}}\dots\partial_{x_{n}}^{k_{n}}p^{s}_{1}(x)=\sum_{i=0}^{\tilde{k}}\frac{\tilde{q}_{i}(x)}{(1+|x|^{2})^{\frac{n+s+\tilde{k}+i}{2}}}

where the
\tilde{q}_{i}
are homogeneous polynomials of degree
i
depending on
k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}
.
Combining these three observations, we obtain

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\partial_{t}^{k_{0}}\partial_{x_{1}}^{k_{1}}\dots\partial_{x_{n}}^{k_{n}}k^{s}(x,t)&=&^{-k-n-l}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k_{0}}c_{\alpha}(t^{-1}x)^{\alpha}\partial_{x_{1}}^{k_{1}+\alpha_{1}}\ldots\partial_{x_{n}}^{k_{n}+\alpha_{n}}p_{s}^{1}(t^{-1}x)\\
&=&^{-k-n-l}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k_{0}}\sum_{i=0}^{\tilde{k}+|\alpha|}\frac{(t^{-1}x)^{\alpha}\tilde{q}_{\alpha,i}(t^{-1}x)}{(1+|x|^{2})^{\frac{n+s+\tilde{k}+|\alpha|+i}{2}}}\\
&=&^{-k-n-l}\sum_{j=0}^{\tilde{k}+k_{0}}\frac{q_{j}(t^{-1}x)}{(1+|x|^{2})^{\frac{n+s+\tilde{k}+j}{2}}}\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

where the
q_{j}
are homogeneous polynomials of degree
j
depending on
k_{0},\ldots,k_{n}
.
Thus, we have shown (
3.1.3
).

The lemma now follows via induction and Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem where the majorants can be chosen as follows.
For the derivatives in
x_{l}
-direction with
0<h\leq 1
we have

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}RRL}&|D_{h,x_{l}}&(\partial_{t}^{k_{0}}\partial_{x_{1}}^{k_{1}}\dots\partial_{x_{n}}^{k_{n}}(k^{s}(x-y,t)f(y)))|\\
&=&|f(y)|\left|\frac{\partial_{t}^{k_{0}}\partial_{x_{1}}^{k_{1}}\dots\partial_{x_{n}}^{k_{n}}k^{s}(x+he_{l}-y,t)-\partial_{t}^{k_{0}}\partial_{x_{1}}^{k_{1}}\dots\partial_{x_{n}}^{k_{n}}k^{s}(x-y,t)}{h}\right|\\
&\leq&\|f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\sup_{\tilde{x}\in B_{1}(x)}|\partial_{x_{l}}(\partial_{t}^{k_{0}}\partial_{x_{1}}^{k_{1}}\dots\partial_{x_{n}}^{k_{n}}k^{s})(\tilde{x}-y,t)|\\
&\leq&\begin{cases}C\|f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}t^{s-k_{0}}((|x-y|-1)^{2}+t^{2})^{-\frac{n+s+(\tilde{k}+1)}{2}}\quad&\text{for }|x-y|>2\\
\|\partial_{x_{l}}(\partial_{t}^{k_{0}}\partial_{x_{1}}^{k_{1}}\dots\partial_{x_{n}}^{k_{n}}k^{s})(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\quad&\text{for }|x-y|\leq 2\end{cases}\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

where we first use the mean value theorem and then that
|x|^{-j}q_{j}(x)\leq C
for each
0\leq j\leq k
due to the homogeneity of
q_{j}
.
For the derivative in
t
-direction we have

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}RRL}&|D_{h,t}&(\partial_{t}^{k_{0}}\partial_{x_{1}}^{k_{1}}\dots\partial_{x_{n}}^{k_{n}}(k^{s}(x-y,t)f(y)))|\\
&=&|f(y)|\left|\frac{\partial_{t}^{k_{0}}\partial_{x_{1}}^{k_{1}}\dots\partial_{x_{n}}^{k_{n}}k^{s}(x-y,t+h)-\partial_{t}^{k_{0}}\partial_{x_{1}}^{k_{1}}\dots\partial_{x_{n}}^{k_{n}}k^{s}(x-y,t)}{h}\right|\\
&\leq&\|f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\sup_{\tilde{t}\in\left[\frac{t}{2},2t\right]}|\partial_{t}(\partial_{t}^{k_{0}}\partial_{x_{1}}^{k_{1}}\dots\partial_{x_{n}}^{k_{n}}k^{s})(x-y,\tilde{t})|\\
&\leq&C\|f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}(2t)^{s}\left(\frac{t}{2}\right)^{-k_{0}}\left((|x-y|^{2}+\left(\frac{t}{2}\right)^{2}\right)^{-\frac{n+s+(\tilde{k}+1)}{2}}\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

for
h\in\left[-\frac{t}{2},t\right]
thanks to the mean value theorem.
It is easy to see that these majorants are integrable.

\square

As a consequence of this lemma, we get the following result for the decay of the
s
-harmonic extension and its derivatives with the same arguments as in Corollary
Corollary
for the classical harmonic extension.

Corollary
(Decay estimate for the
s
-harmonic extension).

Let
s\in(0,2)

f\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
and
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
Denote with
F^{s}(x,t)\coloneqq P^{s}_{t}f(x)
the
s
-harmonic extension.
Then

\sup_{(x,t)\in{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}t^{k}|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}^{k}F^{s}(x,t)|\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

(3.1.4)

If additionally
f\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
, then

\sup_{(x,t)\in{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}t^{n+k}|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}^{k}F^{s}(x,t)|\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

(3.1.5)

Regarding the decay in
x
-direction, if further for some
C>0
,
k>0
we have
|f(x)|\leq C|x|^{-l}
, then

t^{k}|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}^{k}F^{s}(x,t)|\lesssim|x|^{-l}+t^{s}\|f\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}|x|^{-n-s}.

(3.1.6)

\square

We conclude this section with a short collection of some more results, which are analogous to those for the classical Poisson operator.

Proposition
(Fourier transform of the generalized Poisson Kernels).

Let
s\in(0,2)
. The Fourier transform of the Poisson kernel is

{\mathcal{F}}(p^{s}_{t})(\xi)=c_{n,s}\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda^{\frac{s}{2}}e^{-\lambda-\frac{|t\xi|^{2}}{c\lambda}}\frac{d\lambda}{\lambda}.

for some constants
c_{n,s}\neq 0
,
c>0
.

\square

Proof
.

Proposition 7.6 in
[
Hao16
]
yields

{\mathcal{F}}(p^{s}_{1})(\xi)=(2\pi)^{n}c_{n,s}\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda^{\frac{s}{2}}e^{-\lambda-\frac{|2\pi\xi|^{2}}{4\lambda}}\frac{d\lambda}{\lambda}.

(3.1.7)

The result follows immediately since
{\mathcal{F}}(p^{s}_{t})=t^{-n}{\mathcal{F}}(\delta^{t^{-1}}p^{s}_{1})=\delta^{t}{\mathcal{F}}(p^{s}_{1})
thanks to elementary properties of the Fourier transform. Here
\delta^{t}
denotes
\delta^{t}g(x)=g(tx)
for any
t>0
and measurable functions
g
on
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
.

\square

In comparison to the classical Poisson kernel, the kernel of the generalized Poisson operator has a way more complex Fourier transform, which makes it more difficult to quantify the derivatives of the
s
-harmonic extension in
t
-direction.
For our purposes, due to (
3.1.1
) and (
3.1.2
) we already know enough regarding the
t
-derivatives of the extension.
Therefore, we settle for a weaker result compared to the corresponding result for the harmonic extension, Lemma
Lemma
.

Lemma
.

Let
s\in(0,2)
,
k_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
for
1\leq i\leq n
and
f\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
.
Set
F^{s}(x,t)\coloneqq P^{s}_{t}f(x)
.
Then

\partial_{x_{1}}^{k_{1}}\dots\partial_{x_{n}}^{k_{n}}F^{s}(x,t)=P_{t}^{s}(\partial_{x_{1}}^{k_{1}}\dots\partial_{x_{n}}^{k_{n}}f)(x).

\square

Proof
.

Analogous to Lemma
Lemma
.

\square

As for the classical harmonic extension, we easily obtain the boundedness of the derivatives in
x
-direction from this lemma.

Corollary
(
L^{\infty}
-estimate for the
s
-harmonic extenison).

Let
s\in(0,2)
,
k_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
for
1\leq i\leq n
and
f\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
. Then

\|\partial_{x_{1}}^{k_{1}}\dots\partial_{x_{n}}^{k_{n}}P_{t}^{s}f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\leq\|\partial_{x_{1}}^{k_{1}}\dots\partial_{x_{n}}^{k_{n}}f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

\square

Proof
.

Analogous to Corollary
Corollary
.

\square

3.2  Estimating the commutator
[{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}},\phi]

The motivation for introducing the generalized harmonic extensions was the ability to deal with arbitrary fractional Laplacians in the integral term we want to estimate.
Therefore, an obvious first application is the commutator of fractional Laplacians and pointwise multiplication.
For
s>0
and
\phi\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
the commutator
[{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}},\phi]
is defined by

[{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}},\phi](f)\coloneqq{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}(\phi f)-\phi\,{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f,\quad\text{for }f\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

Theorem
.

Let
s\in(0,1)
and
\phi,f\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
.

Suppose
p\in(1,\infty)
and
\sigma\in[s,1)
. Then

\|[{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}},\phi](f)\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\lesssim[{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\sigma}{2}}}\phi]_{BMO}\|I^{\sigma-s}f\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

(3.2.1)

Suppose
q_{1},q_{2},p\in(1,\infty)
with
\frac{1}{q_{1}}+\frac{1}{q_{2}}=\frac{1}{p}
and
\sigma\in[s,1)
. Then

\|[{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}},\phi](f)\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\lesssim\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\sigma}{2}}}\phi\|_{L^{q_{1}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\|I^{\sigma-s}f\|_{L^{q_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

(3.2.2)

\square

Proof
.

By
L^{p}
-duality, it suffices to show that for every
g\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}

\mathcal{I}\coloneqq\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}[{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}},\phi](f)\,g\,\right|\lesssim\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{[}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\sigma}{2}}}\phi{]}_{BMO}\|I^{\sigma-s}f\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\|g\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})},\\
\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\sigma}{2}}}\phi\|_{L^{q_{1}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\|I^{\sigma-s}f\|_{L^{q_{2}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\|g\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.\\
\end{array}\right.

(3.2.3)

With the integration by parts formula for the fractional Laplacian, see Lemma
Lemma
, we have

\mathcal{I}=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}(\phi f)\,g-\phi\,{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f\,g\,\right|=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\phi\,f\,{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}g-\phi\,{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f\,g\,\right|.

Let
\Phi(x,t)=P^{s}_{t}\phi(x)
,
F(x,t)=P^{s}_{t}f(x)
,
G(x,t)=P^{s}_{t}f(x)
be the
s
-harmonic extensions of
\phi
,
f
,
g
.
Then with (
3.1.2
), up to a constant
c
which we omit, we have

\phi\,f\,{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}g-\phi\,{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f\,g=\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}\Phi\left(F\,t^{1-s}\partial_{t}G-t^{1-s}\partial_{t}F\,G\right).

With a partial integration in
t
, where the other boundary term disappears due to the decay of the
s
-harmonic ectensions, see Corollary
Corollary
,

\mathcal{I}=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}\partial_{t}\left(\Phi\left(F\,t^{1-s}\partial_{t}G-t^{1-s}\partial_{t}F\,G\right)\right)\right|\eqqcolon\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}\mathcal{J}\,\right|.

For the rest of the proof, we will show that

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}\lesssim&\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}t^{1-s}|\nabla_{x}\Phi|\,(|\nabla_{x}F|\,|G|+|\nabla_{x}G|\,|F|)\\
&+\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}t^{2-s}|\nabla_{x}\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}\Phi|\,(|\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}F|\,|G|+|\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}G|\,|F|).\end{split}

(3.2.4)

From this estimate we obtain (
3.2.3
) by applying Proposition
Proposition
and Proposition
Proposition
(c,d,e) respectively, sticking to the sequence of the functions suggested here for each summand.
Note that for Proposition
Proposition
, in order to obtain
\|g\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
from
|G|
, we have to rely on variation (e) and therefore obtain no actual Hölder estimate.

Distributing the
t
-derivative, we observe the cancellation of the term
t^{1-s}\Phi\,F_{t}\,G_{t}
, where
G_{t}\equiv\partial_{t}F
.

\mathcal{J}=t^{1-s}\Phi_{t}\,(F\,G_{t}-F_{t}\,G)+\Phi\,\left(F\,\partial_{t}(t^{1-s}G_{t})-\partial_{t}(t^{1-s}F_{t})G\right)

With the
s
-harmonicity, that is
\operatorname{div}(t^{1-s}\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}F)=0
and therefore
\partial_{t}(t^{1-s}F_{t})=-t^{1-s}\Delta_{x}F
, we have

\mathcal{J}=t^{1-s}\Phi_{t}\,(F\,G_{t}-F_{t}\,G)-t^{1-s}\Phi\,\left(F\,\Delta_{x}G-\Delta_{x}F\,G\right).

With a second cancellation, this time of the term
\nabla_{x}F\cdot\nabla_{x}G
,

\mathcal{J}=t^{1-s}\Phi_{t}\,(F\,G_{t}-F_{t}\,G)-t^{1-s}\Phi\,\nabla_{x}\cdot\left(F\,\nabla_{x}G-\nabla_{x}F\,G\right).

Plugging this back into
\mathcal{I}
, we obtain

\mathcal{I}\leq\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{1-s}\Phi_{t}\,(F\,G_{t}-F_{t}\,G)\right|+\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{1-s}\Phi\,\nabla_{x}\cdot\left(F\,\nabla_{x}G-\nabla_{x}F\,G\right)\right|\eqqcolon\mathcal{I}_{1}+\mathcal{I}_{2}

Regarding the second term, with an integration by parts in
x
where the boundary terms vanish due to the decay of the
s
-harmonic extensions, see Corollary
Corollary
, we can estimate the term as in (
3.2.4
),

\mathcal{I}_{2}=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{1-s}\nabla_{x}\Phi\,\cdot\left(F\,\nabla_{x}G-\nabla_{x}F\,G\right)\right|\lesssim\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}t^{1-s}|\nabla_{x}\Phi|\,(|\nabla_{x}F|\,|G|+|\nabla_{x}G|\,|F|).

Regarding the first term, we need an additional derivative, since with
\partial_{t}\Phi
via Proposition
Proposition
we only get an estimate against
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\nu}{2}}}\phi
for
\nu<s
.
Therefore, we use another integration by parts in
t
to then obtain

\mathcal{I}_{1}=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left[\frac{t^{s}}{s}\,t^{1-s}\Phi_{t}\,(F\,t^{1-s}G_{t}-t^{1-s}F_{t}\,G)\right]_{0}^{\infty}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}\frac{t^{s}}{s}\,\partial_{t}\left(t^{1-s}\Phi_{t}\,(F\,t^{1-s}G_{t}-t^{1-s}F_{t}\,G)\right)\right|,

where the boundary terms disappear due to (
3.1.1
), (
3.1.2
) and Corollary
Corollary
.
Distributing the additional derivative, we observe the cancellation of the term
t^{2-s}\Phi_{t}\,F_{t}\,G_{t}
. Applying the
s
-harmonicity for the other terms, i.e.
\partial_{t}(t^{1-s}F)=-t^{1-s}\Delta_{x}F
, we have

\mathcal{I}_{1}=\frac{1}{s}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{2-s}\Delta_{x}\Phi\,(F\,G_{t}-F_{t}\,G)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{2-s}\Phi_{t}(F\,\Delta_{x}G-\Delta_{x}F\,G)\right|.

(3.2.5)

With yet another cancellation, this time of the term
\nabla_{x}F\cdot\nabla_{x}G
,

\mathcal{I}_{1}=\frac{1}{s}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{2-s}\Delta_{x}\Phi\,(F\,G_{t}-F_{t}\,G)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{2-s}\Phi_{t}\,\nabla_{x}\cdot(F\,\nabla_{x}G-\nabla_{x}F\,G)\right|.

After another integration by parts in
x
for the second term, we can estimate
\mathcal{I}_{1}
as in (
3.2.4
) and thus finish this proof.

\square

Remark
.

At first glance, it might seem possible to obtain an additional estimate for
\|[{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}},\phi](f)\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
from (
3.2.4
) with Proposition
Proposition
instead of Proposition
Proposition
.
However,
[\phi]_{BMO}
and
\|g\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
would be fixed as factors, leaving
\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
as the remaining factor.
To obtain such an estimate with Proposition
Proposition
is not possible for terms where
F
occurs without derivative, such as for example
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{2-s}|\nabla_{x}\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}\Phi|\,|\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}G|\,|F|
.

\square

3.3  The Chanilo commutator estimate for Riesz potentials of order
<1

Next, we take a look at the commutators of Riesz potentials and pointwise multiplication.
For
\phi\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
and
s\in(0,n)
we define the commutator
[I^{s},\phi]
by

[I^{s},\phi](u)\coloneqq I^{s}(\phi\,u)-\phi\,I^{s}u,\quad\text{for all }u\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

In
[
Cha82
]
, Chanillo proved the following
L^{q}
-estimate for these commutators,
q\in\left(\frac{n}{n-s},\infty\right)
.

Theorem
(Chanillo).

Let
\phi,u\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
and
1<p<\frac{n}{s}
.
Define
q\in\left(\frac{n}{n-s},\infty\right)
by
\frac{1}{q}=\frac{1}{p}-\frac{s}{n}
.
Then

\|[I^{s},\phi](u)\|_{L^{q}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\lesssim[\phi]_{BMO}\,\|u\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

\square

The specific choice of
p
and
q
is due to the Hardy-Littlewood-Sobolev theorem, see Theorem
Theorem
.
For this combination we have

\|I^{s}u\|_{L^{q}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\lesssim\|u\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

Since the fractional Laplacians are inverse to the Riesz potentials, plugging in
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f
for
u
we also obtain

\|f\|_{L^{q}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\lesssim\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

(3.3.1)

In general, we would prefer to state the theorem above in terms of fractional Laplacians instead of Riesz potentials, since only then we are able to make full use of the
s
-harmonic extension’s behavior near the boundary.
By duality, Theorem
Theorem
is equivalent to

\mathcal{I}\coloneqq\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(I^{s}(\phi\,u)-\phi\,I^{s}u\right)\,v\,\right|\lesssim[\phi]_{BMO}\|u\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\|v\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\qquad\text{for all }v\in L^{q^{\prime}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})

with
\frac{1}{q^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{q}=1
.
By again plugging in
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f
for
u
and with the integration-by-parts formula for Riesz potentials, see Lemma
Lemma
, we obtain

\mathcal{I}=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\phi\,{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f\,I^{s}v-\phi\,f\,v\,\right|.

Note that
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}({\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})})
is dense in
L^{q^{\prime}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})=\dot{F}^{0}_{q^{\prime},2}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
.
This is the case since
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}
is an isomorphism from the Triebel-Lizorkin space
\dot{F}^{s}_{q^{\prime},2}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
to
L^{q^{\prime}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})=\dot{F}^{0}_{q^{\prime},2}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
, see Theorem
Theorem
, and
{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
is dense in
\dot{F}^{s}_{q^{\prime},2}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
, confer
[
Tri83
, Theorem 5.1.5(ii), p.240]
.
Therefore, we may replace the test function
v
with
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}g
. Then,

\mathcal{I}=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\phi\,{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f\,g-\phi\,f\,{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}g\,\right|=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}[{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}},\phi](f)\,g\,\right|.

Thus, by setting
f\coloneqq I^{s}u
, we obtain Theorem
Theorem
from the following theorem.
Note that we only prove this theorem for
s\in(0,1)
.
Lenzmann and Schikorra suggest though, that by iterating the integration-by-parts procedure the result can be extended to
s\in(0,2)
and even to the full range with a suitable higher order extension replacing the
s
-harmonic extensions, confer
[
LS20
, Section 5]
.

Theorem
.

Let
s\in(0,1)
and
p,q
as above in Theorem
Theorem
.
Assume
\phi,f,g\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
.
Then for
\frac{1}{q^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{q}=1
we have

\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}[{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}},\phi](f)\,g\,\right|\lesssim[\phi]_{BMO}\,\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\,\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}g\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

(3.3.2)

\square

Proof
.

Let
\Phi(x,t)=P^{s}_{t}\phi(x)
,
F(x,t)=P^{s}_{t}f(x)
,
G(x,t)=P^{s}_{t}g(x)
be the
s
-harmonic extensions of
\phi
,
f
,
g
.
Following the proof of Theorem
Theorem
up to (
3.2.5
), we have

\mathcal{I}\coloneqq\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}[{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}},\phi](f)\,g\,\right|\leq\mathcal{I}_{1}+\mathcal{I}_{2}

with

\mathcal{I}_{2}=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{1-s}\nabla_{x}\Phi\,\cdot\left(F\,\nabla_{x}G-\nabla_{x}F\,G\right)\right|\lesssim\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}t^{1-s}|\nabla_{x}\Phi|\,(|\nabla_{x}F|\,|G|+|\nabla_{x}G|\,|F|)

and

\mathcal{I}_{1}=\frac{1}{s}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{2-s}\Delta_{x}\Phi\,(F\,G_{t}-F_{t}\,G)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{2-s}\Phi_{t}(\Delta_{x}G\,F-\Delta_{x}F\,G)\right|.

Applying Proposition
Proposition
(d) to
\mathcal{I}_{2}
we have

\mathcal{I}_{2}\lesssim[\phi]_{BMO}\left(\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\|g\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}+\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}g\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\|f\|_{L^{q}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\right)

(3.3.3)

where
\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{p}=1
.

Regarding
\mathcal{I}_{1}
, with an integration by parts in
x
, since
\Delta_{x}=\nabla_{x}\cdot\nabla_{x}
,

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LCL}\vskip 4.30554pt\mathcal{I}_{1}&=&\frac{1}{s}\left|-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{2-s}\nabla_{x}\Phi\cdot\nabla_{x}(F\,G_{t}-F_{t}\,G)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{2-s}\Phi_{t}(\Delta_{x}G\,F-\Delta_{x}F\,G)\right|\\
&\lesssim&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{2-s}|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\Phi|\,(|\nabla_{x}\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}F|\,|G|+|\nabla_{x}\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}G|\,|F|)\\
&&+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{2-s}|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\Phi|\,(|\nabla_{x}F|\,|\partial_{t}G|+|\nabla_{x}G|\,|\partial_{t}F|).\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

Respecting the sequence of the functions in the respective summands, with Proposition
Proposition
(c,d,e) we then obtain

\mathcal{I}_{1}\lesssim[\phi]_{BMO}\left(\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\|g\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}+\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}g\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\|f\|_{L^{q}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\right).

(3.3.4)

Since

\frac{1}{q}=\frac{1}{p}-\frac{s}{n}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad 1-\frac{1}{q^{\prime}}=1-\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}-\frac{s}{n}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{q^{\prime}}-\frac{s}{n},

with (
3.3.1
) applied to (
3.3.3
) and (
3.3.4
) we obtain

\mathcal{I}\lesssim[\phi]_{BMO}\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}g\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

\square

3.4  Fractional Leibniz rules: The three-term-commutator
H_{s}(f,g)

The classical derivatives satisfy the Leibniz rule, that is

\nabla(f\,g)-\nabla f\,g-f\,\nabla g\equiv 0.

This is not true for all differential operators though.
For example, for the Laplacian we have

\Delta(f\,g)-\Delta f\,g-f\,\Delta g=2\nabla f\cdot\nabla g.

In this subsection, we prove two
fractional
Leibniz-rules, which are estimates of the three-term-commutator

H_{s}(f,g)\coloneqq{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}(f\,g)-{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f\,g-f\,{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}g,

where
s>0
,
f,g\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
.
Such fractional Leibniz-rules were originally introduced by Kenig-Ponce-Vega, confer
[
KPV93
, Theorem A.9, p.611]
.

Theorem
.

Assume
s\in(0,1]
,
p\in(1,\infty)
and
\phi,f\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
. Then

\|H_{s}(\phi,f)\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\lesssim[\phi]_{BMO}\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

(3.4.1)

Moreover, suppose
\sigma\in(0,s)
,
p_{1},p_{2}\in(1,\infty)
,
q,q_{1},q_{2}\in[1,\infty]

with
\frac{1}{p}=\frac{1}{p_{1}}+\frac{1}{p_{2}}
,
\frac{1}{q}=\frac{1}{q_{1}}+\frac{1}{q_{2}}
.
Then

\|H_{s}(\phi,f)\|_{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\lesssim\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\sigma}{2}}}\phi\|_{L^{(p_{1},q_{1})}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s-\sigma}{2}}}f\|_{L^{(p_{2},q_{2})}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

(3.4.2)

\square

Proof
(Estimate (
3.4.2
)).

By duality, we only need to show that for any
g\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}

\mathcal{I}\coloneqq\left|\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}H_{s}(\phi,f)\,g\,\right|\lesssim\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\sigma}{2}}}\phi\|_{L^{(p_{1},q_{1})}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s-\sigma}{2}}}f\|_{L^{(p_{2},q_{2})}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\|g\|_{L^{(p^{\prime},q^{\prime})}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}

(3.4.3)

where
\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{p}=\frac{1}{q^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{q}=1
, see
[
Gra14
, Theorem 1.4.16, p.57]
for duality regarding Lorentz spaces.
With the integration-by-parts formula for fractional Laplacians, Lemma
Lemma
, we have

\mathcal{I}=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\phi\,f\,{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}g-{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}\phi\,f\,g-\phi\,{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f\,g\,\right|.

Let
\Phi(x,t)=P^{s}_{t}\phi(x)
,
F(x,t)=P^{s}_{t}f(x)
,
G(x,t)=P^{s}_{t}g(x)
be the
s
-harmonic extensions of
\phi
,
f
,
g
.
Then, via an integration by parts in
t
we obtain

\mathcal{I}=\left|\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}\partial_{t}\left(t^{1-s}\,\Phi\,F\,\partial_{t}G-t^{1-s}\,\partial_{t}\Phi\,F\,G-t^{1-s}\,\Phi\,\partial_{t}F\,G\right)\,\right|\eqqcolon\left|\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}\mathcal{J}\,\right|

as always due to the decay towards infinity and the behavior of the
s
-harmonic extensions near the boundary, Corollary
Corollary
and (
3.1.2
), where we omit the constant
c
.
Distributing the additional derivative, we observe the cancellation of the terms
t^{1-s}\partial_{t}\Phi\,F\,\partial_{t}G
and
t^{1-s}\Phi\,\partial_{t}F\,\partial_{t}G
;

\mathcal{J}=\Phi\,F\,\partial_{t}(t^{1-s}\partial_{t}G)-\partial_{t}(t^{1-s}\partial_{t}\Phi)\,F\,G-\Phi\,\partial_{t}(t^{1-s}\partial_{t}F)\,G-2t^{1-s}\,\partial_{t}\Phi\,\partial_{t}F\,G

Then, by the
s
-harmonicity of the extensions,

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\mathcal{J}&=&-t^{1-s}\,\Phi\,F\,\Delta_{x}G+t^{1-s}\,\Delta_{x}\Phi\,F\,G+t^{1-s}\,\Phi\,\Delta_{x}F\,G-2t^{1-s}\,\partial_{t}\Phi\,\partial_{t}F\,G\\
&=&^{1-s}\,(\Delta_{x}(\Phi\,F)\,G-\Phi\,F\,\Delta_{x}G)-2t^{1-s}\,\nabla_{x}\Phi\cdot\nabla_{x}F\,G-2t^{1-s}\,\partial_{t}\Phi\,\partial_{t}F\,G.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

Plugging this back into
\mathcal{I}
, the first term disappears since
\Delta_{x}=\nabla_{x}\cdot\nabla_{x}
and therefore, via integration by parts in
x
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{1-s}\,(\Delta_{x}(\Phi\,F)\,G-\Phi\,F\,\Delta_{x}G)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{1-s}\,(\nabla_{x}(\Phi\,F)\cdot\nabla_{x}G-\nabla_{x}(\Phi\,F)\cdot\nabla_{x}G)=0.

Thus, we are left with

\mathcal{I}\lesssim\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{1-s}\nabla_{x}\Phi\cdot\nabla_{x}F\,G\,+t^{1-s}\partial_{t}\Phi\,\partial_{t}F\,G\,\right|\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{1-s}\,|\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}\Phi|\,|\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}F|\,|G|.

(3.4.4)

Applying Proposition
Proposition
, we estimate
\mathcal{I}
as in (
3.4.3
) and therefore have shown (
3.4.2
).
Note that we have
\sigma,\,s-\sigma<s=\min\{1,s\}
since
\sigma\in(0,s)
.

\square

Proof
(Estimate (
3.4.1
)).

Again, by duality, we only need to show that for any
g\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}

\mathcal{I}\coloneqq\left|\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}H_{s}(\phi,f)\,g\,\right|\lesssim[\phi]_{BMO}\,\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\,\|g\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

(3.4.5)

Following the proof of (
3.4.2
) until (
3.4.4
), we have

\mathcal{I}\lesssim\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{1-s}\nabla_{x}\Phi\cdot\nabla_{x}F\,G\,\right|+\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{1-s}\partial_{t}\Phi\,\partial_{t}F\,G\,\right|\eqqcolon\mathcal{I}_{1}+\mathcal{I}_{2}.

Regarding the first term, for
s<1
, with Proposition
Proposition
we can already estimate

\mathcal{I}_{1}\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{1-s}|\nabla_{x}\Phi|\,|\nabla_{x}F|\,|G|\lesssim[\phi]_{BMO}\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\|g\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

For
s=1
, to obtain an estimate against
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}f
, we either need a second derivative on
f
or any derivative on
g
.
Therefore, we again integrate by parts in
t
-direction, obtaining

\mathcal{I}_{1}=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}\nabla_{x}\Phi\cdot\nabla_{x}F\,G\,\right|=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t\partial_{t}\left(\nabla_{x}\Phi\cdot\nabla_{x}F\,G\right)\,\right|.

Distributing the
t
-derivative, where we integrate by parts in
x
when that derivative hits
\nabla_{x}\Phi
,

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\mathcal{I}_{1}&=&\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}-t\,\partial_{t}\Phi\,\nabla_{x}\cdot(\nabla_{x}F\,G)+t\,\nabla_{x}\Phi\cdot\partial_{t}\nabla_{x}F\,G+t\,\nabla_{x}\Phi\cdot\nabla_{x}F\,\partial_{t}G\,\right|\\
&\lesssim&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t\,|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\Phi|\,(|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\nabla_{x}F|\,|G|+|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}G|\,|\nabla_{x}F|).\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

Applying Proposition
Proposition
while respecting the sequence of functions, we can estimate
\mathcal{I}_{1}
as in (
3.4.5
).
Regarding
\mathcal{I}_{2}
, we also need a second derivative on
f
or any derivative on
g
to estimate against
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f
.
With an integration by parts in
t
-direction,

\mathcal{I}_{2}=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left[\frac{t^{s}}{s}\,t^{1-s}\partial_{t}\Phi\,t^{1-s}\partial_{t}F\,G\,\right]_{0}^{\infty}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}\frac{t^{s}}{s}\,\partial_{t}\left(t^{1-s}\partial_{t}\Phi\,t^{1-s}\partial_{t}F\,G\right)\,\right|

Distributing the
t
-derivative, by the
s
-harmonicity of the extensions, i.e.
\partial_{t}(t^{1-s}\partial_{t}\Phi)=t^{1-s}\Delta_{x}\Phi
,

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\mathcal{I}_{2}&=&\frac{1}{s}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{2-s}\left(\Delta_{x}\Phi\,\partial_{t}F\,G+\partial_{t}\Phi\,\Delta_{x}F\,G-\partial_{t}\Phi\,\partial_{t}F\,\partial_{t}G\right)\,\right|\\
&\lesssim&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{2-s}\,|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\Phi|\,(\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\nabla_{x}F|\,|G|+|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}G|\,|\partial_{t}F|),\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

where we used that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{2-s}\,\Delta_{x}\Phi\,\partial_{t}F\,G=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{2-s}\nabla_{x}\Phi\cdot\nabla_{x}(\partial_{t}F\,G).

With Proposition
Proposition
we can estimate
\mathcal{I}_{2}
as in (
3.4.5
) and therefore obtain (
3.4.1
).

\square

3.5  A Hardy-space estimate for
H_{s}(f,g)

We further investigate the three-term-commutator
H_{s}(f,g)
from Subsection
3.4
, defined as

H_{s}(f,g)\coloneqq{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}(f\,g)-{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f\,g-f\,{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}g.

In the previous subsection, we estimated
L^{p}
or Lorentz-norm for this commutator. Da Lio and Rivière showed the following Hardy-space estimate for the commutator with an additional fractional Laplacian in the case
s=1/2
, see
[
DR11
, Theorem 1.7]

Theorem
(Da Lio-Rivière).

Assume
f,g\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
. Then, with
\mathcal{H}^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
denoting the Hardy-space,

\|(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{4}}H_{\frac{1}{2}}(f,g)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\lesssim\|(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{4}}f\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\|(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{4}}g\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

\square

We proof a generalization of this result to
s\in(0,1]
.
This generalization is already known, see
[
Sch15
, Theorem 1.4]
. The original proof is substantially longer and more complicated than the proof from
[
LS20
]
, which we show here.

Theorem
.

Let
s\in(0,1]
.
Assume
f,\phi\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
and
p,p^{\prime}\in(1,\infty)
,
q,q^{\prime}\in[1,\infty]
with
\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{p}=\frac{1}{q^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{q}=1
. Then

\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}H_{s}(\phi,f)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}\lesssim\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}\phi\|_{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\,\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f\|_{L^{(p^{\prime},q^{\prime})}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

\square

Proof
(s<1).

Assume
s<1
.
Since
BMO
is the topological dual of
\mathcal{H}^{1}
, see the well known result in
[
FS72
]
, we only need to show that for any
g\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}

\mathcal{I}\coloneqq\left|\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}H_{s}(\phi,f)\,g\right|\lesssim\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}\phi\|_{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\,\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f\|_{L^{(p^{\prime},q^{\prime})}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\,[g]_{BMO}.

(3.5.1)

Let
\Phi(s,t)=P^{s}_{t}\phi(x)
,
F(s,t)=P^{s}_{t}f(x)
,
\tilde{G}(s,t)=P^{s}_{t}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}g)(x)
be the
s
-harmonic extensions of
\phi
,
f
and
\tilde{g}\coloneqq{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}g
.
For the rest of the proof, we are going to show that

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{1-s}\,|\tilde{G}|\,|\nabla_{x}\Phi|\,|\nabla_{x}F|\,

(3.5.2)

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{2-s}\,|\tilde{G}|\,(|\nabla_{x}\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\Phi|\,|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}F|+|\nabla_{x}\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}F|\,|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\Phi|)

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{3-s}\,|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\tilde{G}|\,(|\nabla_{x}\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\Phi|\,|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}F|+|\nabla_{x}\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}F|\,|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\Phi|)

Then, since
\tilde{G}(s,t)=P^{s}_{t}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}g)(x)
and
s<1
, applying Proposition
Proposition
(a,b) yields (
3.5.1
).

Before extending the integral to
{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}
, we redistribute some of the fractional Laplacians with Lemma
Lemma
,

\mathcal{I}=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}\phi\,f\,{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}g)-(\phi\,{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f+{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}\phi\,f)\,{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}g\,\right|.

With an integration by parts in
t
we obtain

\mathcal{I}=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}\partial_{t}\left(t^{1-s}\,\Phi\,F\,\partial_{t}\tilde{G}-t^{1-s}\Phi\,\partial_{t}F\,\tilde{G}-t^{1-s}\partial_{t}\Phi\,F\,\tilde{G}\right)\,\right|

due to the extensions’ decay towards infinity and their behavior near the boundary, that is
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}\phi=c\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}\partial_{t}\Phi
, where we omit the constant
c
.
Distributing the additional derivative, we observe the cancellation of the term
t^{1-s}\partial_{t}(\Phi\,F)\,\partial_{t}\tilde{G}
,

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\mathcal{I}&=&\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}\partial_{t}\left(t^{1-s}\,\Phi\,F\,\partial_{t}\tilde{G}-t^{1-s}\partial_{t}(\Phi\,F)\,\tilde{G}\right)\,\right|\\
&=&\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}(\Phi\,F)\,\partial_{t}\left(t^{1-s}\partial_{t}\tilde{G}\right)-\partial_{t}\left(t^{1-s}\partial_{t}(\Phi\,F)\right)\,\tilde{G}\,\right|.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

With the
s
-harmonicity of
\tilde{G}
, that is
\partial_{t}\left(t^{1-s}\partial_{t}\tilde{G}\right)=-t^{1-s}\Delta_{x}\tilde{G}
,

\mathcal{I}=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}\left(t^{1-s}\Delta_{x}(\Phi\,F)+\partial_{t}\left(t^{1-s}\partial_{t}(\Phi\,F)\right)\right)\,\tilde{G}\,\right|=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}L_{s}(\Phi\,F)\,\tilde{G}\,\right|,

where we set

L_{s}(H)\coloneqq\operatorname{div}(t^{1-s}\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}H)=t^{1-s}\Delta_{x}H+\partial_{t}\left(t^{1-s}\partial_{t}H\right).

For this operator, we calculate the product rule

L_{s}(\Phi\,F)=L_{s}(\Phi)\,F+\Phi\,L_{s}(F)+2t^{1-s}\,\nabla_{x}\Phi\cdot\nabla_{x}F+2t^{1-s}\,\partial_{t}\Phi\,\partial_{t}F.

Obviously, with the
s
-harmonicity of
\Phi
and
F
we have
L(\Phi)=L(F)=0
and therefore obtain

\mathcal{I}\lesssim\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{1-s}\,\nabla_{x}\Phi\cdot\nabla_{x}F\,\tilde{G}\,\right|+\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{1-s}\,\partial_{t}\Phi\,\partial_{t}F\,\tilde{G}\,\right|\eqqcolon\mathcal{I}_{1}+\mathcal{I}_{2}.

The frist term can already be estimated as in (
3.5.2
).
Regarding the second term, if we wanted to estimate this term as in (
3.5.1
) via Proposition
Proposition
, we would need an additional derivative either on
\partial_{t}\Phi
or
\partial_{t}F
.
Therefore, we again integrate by parts in
t
,

\mathcal{I}_{2}=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left[\frac{t^{s}}{s}\,t^{1-s}\partial_{t}\Phi\,t^{1-s}\partial_{t}F\,\tilde{G}\right]_{0}^{\infty}-\frac{1}{s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{s}\,\partial_{t}\left(t^{1-s}\partial_{t}\Phi\,t^{1-s}\partial_{t}F\,\tilde{G}\right)\right|.

Distributing the additional derivative, due to the
s
-harmonicity of
\Phi
and
F
we obtain

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LCL}\vskip 4.30554pt\mathcal{I}_{2}&\lesssim&\frac{1}{s}\left(\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{2-s}\,\Delta_{x}\Phi\,\partial_{t}F\,\tilde{G}\,\right|+\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{2-s}\,\partial_{t}\Phi\,\Delta_{x}F\,\tilde{G}\,\right|+\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{2-s}\,\partial_{t}\Phi\,\partial_{t}F\,\partial_{t}\tilde{G}\,\right|\right)\\
&\eqqcolon&\mathcal{I}_{2a}+\mathcal{I}_{2b}+\mathcal{I}_{2c}.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

The first two terms can already be estimated as in (
3.5.2
).
For the third term, we use yet another integration by parts in
t
.

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pts\mathcal{I}_{2c}&=&\frac{1}{2s}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left[t^{2s}\,t^{1-s}\partial_{t}\Phi\,t^{1-s}\partial_{t}F\,t^{1-s}\partial_{t}\tilde{G}\right]_{0}^{\infty}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{2s}\,\partial_{t}\left(t^{1-s}\partial_{t}\Phi\,t^{1-s}\partial_{t}F\,t^{1-s}\partial_{t}\tilde{G}\right)\,\right|\\
&\lesssim&\frac{1}{2s}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{3-s}\Delta_{x}\Phi\,\partial_{t}F\,\partial_{t}\tilde{G}\right|+\frac{1}{2s}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{3-s}\partial_{t}\Phi\,\Delta_{x}F\,\partial_{t}\tilde{G}\right|\\
&&+\frac{1}{2s}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{3-s}\nabla_{x}\left(\partial_{t}\Phi\,\partial_{t}F\right)\cdot\nabla_{x}\tilde{G}\right|\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

Thus,
\mathcal{I}_{2c}
can also be estimated as in (
3.5.1
).

\square

Proof
(s=1).

The setup is exactly the same as in the proof for
s<1
.
It suffices to show (
3.5.1
).
For
s<1
, we obtained this estimate by applying Proposition
Proposition
to (
3.5.2
).
The only term in (
3.5.2
), which we are not able to immediately estimate analogously, is the first one, which corresponds to

\mathcal{I}_{1}=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{1-s}\,\nabla_{x}\Phi\cdot\nabla_{x}F\,\tilde{G}\,\right|=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}\nabla_{x}\Phi\cdot\nabla_{x}F\,\tilde{G}\,\right|

For
s=1
, we need an additional derivative on
\nabla_{x}\Phi
or
\nabla_{x}F
.
We deal with
\mathcal{I}_{1}
just as we dealt with
\mathcal{I}_{2}
for
s<1
.
With an integration by parts in
t
we obtain

\mathcal{I}_{1}=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t\,\partial_{t}\left(\nabla_{x}\Phi\cdot\nabla_{x}F\,\tilde{G}\right)\,\right|.

Distributing the additional derivative,

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LCL}\vskip 4.30554pt\mathcal{I}_{1}&\lesssim&\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t\,\nabla_{x}\partial_{t}\Phi\cdot\nabla_{x}F\,\tilde{G}\,\right|+\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t\,\nabla_{x}\Phi\cdot\nabla_{x}\partial_{t}F\,\tilde{G}\,\right|+\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t\,\nabla_{x}\Phi\cdot\nabla_{x}F\,\partial_{t}\tilde{G}\,\right|\\
&\eqqcolon&\mathcal{I}_{1a}+\mathcal{I}_{1b}+\mathcal{I}_{1c}.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

The first two terms can already be estimated as in (
3.5.2
).
The third term requires another integration by parts, where we use the harmonicity of
\tilde{G}
when the additional derivative hits
\partial_{t}\tilde{G}
,

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\mathcal{I}_{1c}&=&\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t\,\partial_{t}\left(t\,\nabla_{x}\Phi\cdot\nabla_{x}F\,\partial_{t}\tilde{G}\right)\,\right|\\
&\lesssim&\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{2}\,\nabla_{x}\partial_{t}\Phi\cdot\nabla_{x}F\,\partial_{t}\tilde{G}\,\right|+\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{2}\,\nabla_{x}\Phi\cdot\nabla_{x}\partial_{t}F\,\partial_{t}\tilde{G}\,\right|\\
&&+\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}t^{2}\,\nabla_{x}\left(\nabla_{x}\Phi\cdot\nabla_{x}F\right)\cdot\partial_{t}\nabla_{x}\tilde{G}\,\right|.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

Thus, we can estimate
\mathcal{I}_{1}
just as
\mathcal{I}_{2}
and therefore are left only with terms where we can obtain (
3.5.1
) for
s=1
with Proposition
Proposition
.

\square

4  Riesz transforms, fractional Laplacians, Riesz potentials

In this section, we introduce Riesz transforms, the fractional Laplacian, and its inverse, the Riesz potential.
For each operator, we gather some essential and well known results from the literature, mainly from
[
Ste70
]
.
Additionally, for the Riesz transforms and the fractional Laplacians we provide specific
L^{\infty}
- and decay-estimates, which are needed for Section
5
.

4.1  Riesz transforms

Riesz transforms are the prototypical singular integral operators, see
[
Ste70
, Chapter III, §1]
for a more detailed introduction.
One of their most common applications is the mediation between multiple differential operators of the same order.

Definition
(Riesz transforms).

For each
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
the corresponding Riesz transform is given by

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt{\mathcal{R}_{j}}(f)(x)=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}C_{n}\int_{|y|\geq\epsilon}\frac{y_{j}}{|y|^{n+1}}f(x-y)\,dy\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

for all
f\in L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
,
1\leq p<\infty
with
C_{n}=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{n+1}{2}\right)}{\pi^{\frac{n+1}{2}}}
.

\square

These singular integral operators are well defined, see Theorem 4 in
[
Ste70
, Chapter II]
.
That theorem further implies the
L^{p}
-boundedness of the Riesz transforms.

Proposition
(
L^{p}
-boundedness of
{\mathcal{R}_{j}}
).

Assume
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and
1<p<\infty
.
Then there exists
A_{p}>0
such that for each
f\in L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\|{\mathcal{R}_{j}}(f)\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\leq A_{p}\|f\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

\square

An important observation, which in fact justifies the definition of the Riesz transform, regards the interplay with the Fourier transform.
Since
[
Ste70
]
uses a different variation of the Fourier transform than e.g.
[
Gra14
]
, we will prefer the other sources for references related to the Fourier transform.
The following result is Proposition 5.1.14. in
[
Gra14
]
.
An analogous statement can be found in Chapter III of
[
Ste70
]
.

Proposition
(Fourier symbol of Riesz transforms).

The
j
th Riesz transform
{\mathcal{R}_{j}}
is given on the Fourier transform side by multiplication with the function
-i\frac{\xi_{j}}{|\xi|}
.
That is, for any
f\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
it holds

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{R}_{j}}(f))(\xi)=-i\frac{\xi_{j}}{|\xi|}{\mathcal{F}}(f)(\xi).\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

\square

An easy to see consequence of this proposition is the following lemma.

Lemma
(An integration by parts formula for Riesz transforms).

Assume
f,g\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
and
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
. Then

\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}g{\mathcal{R}_{j}}f=-\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}{\mathcal{R}_{j}}g\,f.

\square

Proof
.

Since the Fourier transform is an isometry on
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
, with
{\mathcal{R}_{j}}f(x)\in\mathbb{R}
for almost every
x\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
and Proposition
Proposition
we have

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}g{\mathcal{R}_{j}}f&=\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}g\overline{{\mathcal{R}_{j}}f}=\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}{\mathcal{F}}(g)\overline{{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{R}_{j}}f)}=-\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}\left(-i\frac{\xi_{i}}{|\xi|}\right){\mathcal{F}}(g)(\xi)\overline{{\mathcal{F}}(f)(\xi)}\,d\xi\\
&=-\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{R}_{j}}g)\overline{{\mathcal{F}}(f)}=-\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}{\mathcal{R}_{j}}g\,f.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

\square

Another consequence of Proposition
Proposition
is the following application, the already mentioned mediation between differential operators of the same order.
For example, Riesz transforms can be used to mediate between the partial derivatives and the Laplacian of a function.

Proposition
.

Assume
\phi\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
and
j,k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
. Then

\partial_{j}\partial_{k}\phi(x)={\mathcal{R}_{j}}\mathcal{R}_{k}(-\Delta)\phi(x).

(4.1.1)

Together with the
L^{p}
-boundedness of Riesz transforms, for every
1<p<\infty
this implies

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\|\nabla^{2}\phi\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\leq A_{p}^{2}\|\Delta\phi\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

\square

Proof
.

For any
f\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
we have

{\mathcal{F}}(\partial_{j}f)(\xi)=2\pi i\xi_{j}{\mathcal{F}}(f)(\xi).

Therefore, applying the Fourier transform to the left side of (
4.1.1
), we have

{\mathcal{F}}(\partial_{j}\partial_{k}\phi)(\xi)=-4\pi^{2}\xi_{j}\xi_{k}{\mathcal{F}}(\phi)(\xi).

Thanks to Proposition
Proposition
, for the right side we have

{\mathcal{F}}(-{\mathcal{R}_{j}}\mathcal{R}_{k}\Delta\phi)(\xi)=\frac{\xi_{j}\xi_{k}}{|\xi|^{2}}{\mathcal{F}}(\Delta\phi)(\xi)=\frac{\xi_{j}\xi_{k}}{|\xi|^{2}}\left(\sum_{l=1}^{n}-4\pi^{2}\xi_{l}^{2}\right){\mathcal{F}}(\phi)(\xi)=-4\pi^{2}\frac{\xi_{j}\xi_{k}}{|\xi|^{2}}|\xi|^{2}{\mathcal{F}}(\phi)(\xi).

Now, applying the inverse Fourier transform, we have shown (
4.1.1
).

\square

This same type of argument is used in Section
2.3
to show similar relations between other differential operators.
For the last two results of this subsection, we do not use the Fourier characterization of the Riesz transforms, but rather fall back on the original definition as a singular integral operator.

Lemma
(The decay of
{\mathcal{R}_{j}}f
).

Let
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
Assume
f\in C^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})\cap L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
,
1\leq p<\infty
, with
k\in\mathbb{R}
and
C>0
such that for all
x\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}

|\nabla f(x)|\leq\frac{C}{|x|^{k}}.

Then there exists a constant
C_{f}>0
such that for all
x\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}

|{\mathcal{R}_{j}}f(x)|\leq C_{f}\left(\frac{1}{|x|^{\frac{n}{p}}}+\frac{1}{|x|^{k-1}}\right).

\square

Proof
.

Let
x\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
.
By the definition of the Riesz transforms we have

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt|{\mathcal{R}_{j}}f(x)|&\leq&\left|\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\setminus B_{\frac{|x|}{2}}(x)}\frac{x_{i}-y_{i}}{|x-y|^{n+1}}f(y)\,dy\right|+\left|\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\int_{B_{\frac{|x|}{2}}(x)\setminus B_{\epsilon}(x)}\frac{x_{i}-y_{i}}{|x-y|^{n+1}}f(y)\,dy\right|\\
&\eqqcolon&C_{1}+C_{2}.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

Regarding the second term, first note that, since
y\mapsto(x_{i}-y_{i})|x-y|^{-(n+1)}
is odd, for any
0<r<R\leq\infty
we have

\int_{B_{R}(x)\setminus B_{r}(x)}\frac{x_{i}-y_{i}}{|x-y|^{n+1}}dy=0.

(4.1.2)

Applying this observation, we obtain

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554ptC_{2}&=&\left|\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\int_{B_{\frac{|x|}{2}}(x)\setminus B_{\epsilon}(x)}\frac{x_{i}-y_{i}}{|x-y|^{n+1}}(f(y)-f(x))\,dy\right|\\
&\leq&\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\int_{B_{\frac{|x|}{2}}(x)\setminus B_{\epsilon}(x)}\frac{|f(y)-f(x)|}{|x-y|^{n}}\,dy\\
&\leq&\sup_{\tilde{x}\in B_{\frac{|x|}{2}}(x)}|\nabla f(\tilde{x})|\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\int_{B_{\frac{|x|}{2}}(x)\setminus B_{\epsilon}(x)}\frac{|x-y|}{|x-y|^{n}}\,dy\\
&\leq&\frac{2^{k}C}{|x|^{k}}\omega_{n}\int_{0}^{\frac{|x|}{2}}\frac{r^{n-1}}{r^{n-1}}dr\\
&=&2^{k-1}C\omega_{n}\frac{1}{|x|^{k-1}}\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

where
\omega_{n}
denotes the
n-1
-dimensional volume of the sphere
S^{n-1}
.

Regarding the first term, with Hölder’s inequality and
\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{p}=1
we obtain

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554ptC_{1}&\leq&\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\setminus B_{\frac{|x|}{2}}(x)}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n}}|f(y)|\,dy\\
&\leq&\|y\mapsto|y|^{-n}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}}\setminus B_{|x|/2}(0))}\|f\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\\
&=&C|x|^{-\frac{n}{p}}\|f\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

for a constant
C>0
depending on
n
and
p
.

\square

Lemma
(An
L^{\infty}
-estimate for
{\mathcal{R}_{j}}f
).

Let
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
Assume
f\in C^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})\cap L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
with
1\leq p<\infty
and
\nabla f\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
.
Then

\|{\mathcal{R}_{j}}f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}+\|\nabla f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

\square

Proof
.

Let
x\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
.
Similar to the previous proof, we have

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt|{\mathcal{R}_{j}}f(x)|&\leq&\left|\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\setminus B_{1}(x)}\frac{x_{i}-y_{i}}{|x-y|^{n+1}}f(y)\,dy\right|+\left|\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\int_{B_{1}(x)\setminus B_{\epsilon}(x)}\frac{x_{i}-y_{i}}{|x-y|^{n+1}}f(y)\,dy\right|\\
&\eqqcolon&C_{1}+C_{2}.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

Regarding the first term, with Hölder’s inequality,

C_{1}\leq\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\setminus B_{1}(x)}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n}}|f(y)|\,dy\leq\|y\rightarrow|y|^{-n}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}}\setminus B_{1}(0))}\|f\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

where
\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{p}=1
, in particular
p^{\prime}\in(1,\infty]
and
\|y\rightarrow|y|^{-n}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}}\setminus B_{1}(0))}<\infty
.
For the second term we obtain

C_{2}=\left|\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\int_{B_{1}(x)\setminus B_{\epsilon}(x)}\frac{x_{i}-y_{i}}{|x-y|^{n+1}}(f(y)-f(x))\,dy\,\right|\leq\|\nabla f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\int_{B_{1}(x)}\frac{|x-y|}{|x-y|^{n}}\,dy,

where we used (
4.1.2
) as well as the mean value theorem.

\square

4.2  Fractional Laplacians

We already saw in the proof of Proposition
Proposition
above how the Laplacian interacts with the Fourier transform,

{\mathcal{F}}(-\Delta f)(\xi)=(2\pi|\xi|)^{2}{\mathcal{F}}(f)(\xi)\qquad\text{for any $f\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})$.}

This motivates the following definition of the fractional Laplacian operator.

Definition
(Fractional Laplacian).

Let
s>0
and
f\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
.
Then the fractional Laplacian operator
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}
is defined by

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt{\mathcal{F}}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f)(\xi)=(2\pi|\xi|)^{s}{\mathcal{F}}(f)(\xi).\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

\square

Note that for each
s>0
and
f\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
the function
\xi\mapsto(2\pi|\xi|)^{s}{\mathcal{F}}(f)(\xi)
is still rapidly decreasing, although it might not be differentiable in
\xi=0
anymore.
In particular, we have an
L^{1}
function and therefore can apply the inverse Fourier transform.
Thus, the fractional Laplacian operator is well defined.
Throughout this thesis, fractional Laplacians will often be applied to functions that are not strictly of the Schwartz space, see for example Lemma
Lemma
below.
In most cases, it is easy to see that the function the fractional Laplacian is applied to is in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
or
L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
with a sufficiently decreasing Fourier transform, resulting in the fractional Laplacian being well defined with the same reasoning as for Schwartz functions.
We will only investigate the well-definedness further, if this is not the case.

For later applications, we will mostly consider the case
s\in(0,2)
.
Under this restriction, the definition can be extended to other function spaces such as
L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
with
1\leq p\leq 2
, see
[
Kwa17
]
.
Note that there is a variety of different, equivalent ways to introduce and define the fractional Laplacian, again see
[
Kwa17
]
.
For example, the fractional Laplacian may be defined similar to the Riesz transform as a singular integral operator.
We pick up this equivalent definition towards the end of this chapter.

Since the fractional Laplacians act as Fourier multipliers by definition, there are some easy to see consequences, similar to those we got for the Riesz transforms after Proposition
Proposition
.
First, we get an integration by parts formula.

Lemma
(An integration by parts formula for fractional Laplacians).

Let
f,g\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
and
s>0
.
Then

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}g{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f=\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}g\,f.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

\square

Proof
.

Analogous to Lemma
Lemma
. We have
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f(x)\in\mathbb{R}
for all
x\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
because of Proposition
Proposition
, the alternative definition of the fractional Laplacian.

\square

Second, we observe an interaction between multiple fractional Laplacians which we can describe as a semigroup property.

Lemma
(Semigroup property of fractional Laplacians).

Let
f\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
and
s_{1},s_{2}>0
.
Then

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s_{1}}{2}}}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s_{2}}{2}}}f={(-\Delta)^{\frac{s_{1}+s_{2}}{2}}}f.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

\square

Proof
.

Applying the Fourier transform to the equation, via the definition of the fractional Laplacian we get

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt{\mathcal{F}}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{s_{1}}{2}}}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s_{2}}{2}}}f)(\xi)=(2\pi|\xi|)^{s_{1}}(2\pi|\xi|)^{s_{2}}{\mathcal{F}}(f)(\xi)=(2\pi|\xi|)^{s_{1}+s_{2}}{\mathcal{F}}(f)(\xi)={\mathcal{F}}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{s_{1}+s_{2}}{2}}}f).\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

Now applying the inverse Fourier transform, we have proven the equation.

\square

Comparing the Fourier symbols of
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}
and
\partial_{j}
,
1\leq j\leq n
, one would expect a relation similar to Proposition
Proposition
, which dealt with the usual Laplacian operator.
And indeed, we get a similar result for the fractional Laplacian with
s=1
, although the proof is not quite as simple.

Proposition
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
and
1<p<\infty
.
Then

\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}f\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\approx\|\nabla f\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

(4.2.1)

\square

Proof
.

Both
\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}f\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
and
\|\nabla f\|_{L^{(}{\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
are equivalent norms on the Triebel-Lizorkin space
\dot{F}^{1}_{p,2}
, see
Theorem
or
[
Tri83
, Theorem 5.2.3.1]
for the original source.

\square

We conclude this subsection about the fractional Laplacian estimating the
L^{\infty}
-norm and investigating the decay of
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f
.
In order to show these estimates, we make use of one of the alternative definitions of the fractional Laplacian.

Proposition
(Alternative definition of fractional Laplacians).

Let
s\in(0,2)
.
Then for
f\in\mathcal{S}

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f(x)&=&C_{n,s}\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\setminus B_{\epsilon}(x)}\frac{f(x)-f(y)}{|x-y|^{n+s}}dy\\
&=&-\frac{1}{2}C_{n,s}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}\frac{f(x+y)+f(x-y)-2f(x)}{|y|^{n+s}}dy\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

with
C_{n,s}=\frac{2^{s}\Gamma\left(\frac{n+s}{2}\right)}{\pi^{\frac{n}{2}}\left|\Gamma\left(-\frac{s}{2}\right)\right|}=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{1-\cos(\xi_{1})}{|\xi|^{n+2s}}d\xi\right)^{-1}
.

\square

Proof
.

For the first equation see
[
Kwa17
]
or Proposition 3.3 in
[
DPV12
]
.
The second equation follows from rewriting the principal value integral and then using a second order taylor expansion to remove the singularity, see Lemma 3.2 in
[
DPV12
]
.

\square

Again, this proposition or alternative definition respectively can be extended to
L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
,
p\in[1,\infty)
, see
[
Kwa17
]
.

Lemma
(An
L^{\infty}
-estimate for
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f
).

Let
s\in(0,2)
and
f\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
.
Then

\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}+\|\nabla^{2}f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

(4.2.2)

For
s\in(0,1)
, we also have

\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}+\|\nabla f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

(4.2.3)

\square

Proof
.

We show (
4.2.3
) first.
Let
s\in(0,1)
and
x\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
. Then

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f(x)|&=&\left|\lim_{r\rightarrow 0}C_{n,s}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\setminus B_{r}(0)}\frac{f(x+y)-f(x)}{|y|^{n+s}}dy\right|\\
&\lesssim&\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\setminus B_{1}(0)}\frac{|f(x+y)-f(x)|}{|y|^{n+s}}dy+\lim_{r\rightarrow 0}\int_{B_{1}(0)\setminus B_{r}(0)}\frac{|f(x+y)-f(x)|}{|y|^{n+s}}dy\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

With the mean value theorem we have
|f(x+y)-f(x)|\leq\|\nabla f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}|y|
for the second term.
Therefore, we obtain

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f(x)|&\leq&2\|f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\setminus B_{1}(0)}\frac{1}{|y|^{n+s}}dy+\|\nabla f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\lim_{r\rightarrow 0}\int_{B_{1}(0)\setminus B_{r}(0)}\frac{|y|}{|y|^{n+s}}dy\\
&\leq&\left(2\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\setminus B_{1}(0)}|y|^{-(n+s)}dy+\int_{B_{1}(0)}|y|^{-(n+s-1)}dy\right)(\|f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}+\|\nabla f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}).\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

The first integral exists because
s>0
, the second one because
s<1
.
Thus, we have shown (
4.2.3
).

Now, let
s\in(0,2)
and
x\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
. Then with a second order Taylor expansion yielding

|f(x+y)+f(x-y)-2f(x)|=|y^{T}\nabla^{2}f(x+\theta(y-x))y|\leq\|\nabla^{2}f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}|y|^{2},

(4.2.4)

where
\theta\in[-1,1]
, we obtain

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f(x)|&=&\left|\frac{1}{2}C_{n,s}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}\frac{f(x+y)+f(x-y)-2f(x)}{|y|^{n+s}}dy\right|\\
&\lesssim&\|f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\setminus B_{1}(0)}|y|^{-(n+s)}dy+\|\nabla^{2}f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\int_{B_{1}(0)}|y|^{n+s-2}dy\\
&\lesssim&\left(4\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\setminus B_{1}(0)}|y|^{-(n+s)}dy+\int_{B_{1}(0)}|y|^{n+s-2}dy\right)(\|f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}+\|\nabla^{2}f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}).\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

Since the integrals exist due to
s\in(0,2)
, we have shown (
4.2.2
).

\square

Remark
.

By not dividing
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
into
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\setminus B_{1}(0)
and
B_{1}(0)
but rather varying the radius for the cut out ball, one can easily obtain weighted inequalities such as

\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\leq C(\epsilon)\|f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}+\epsilon\|\nabla^{2}f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})},

where
\epsilon>0
can be chosen arbitrarily and
C(\epsilon)>0
is a constant depending on the choice.
An analogous observation can be made for example for Lemma
Lemma
.

\square

For our last basic result regarding the fractional Laplacian, we investigate its decay.
The approach for the proof of the following lemma is similar to the one for Lemma
Lemma
.

Lemma
(The decay of
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f
).

Let
s\in(0,2)
.
Assume
f\in C^{2}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})\cap W^{2,\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})\cap L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
with
k,l\in\mathbb{R}
and
C>0
such that for all
x\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}

|f(x)|\leq\frac{C}{|x|^{k}},\quad|\nabla^{2}f(x)|\leq\frac{C}{|x|^{l}}.

Then there exists a constant
C_{f}>0
such that for all
x\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}

|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f(x)|\leq C_{f}\left(\frac{1}{|x|^{n+s}}+\frac{1}{|x|^{k+s}}+\frac{1}{|x|^{l+s-2}}\right).

\square

Proof
.

Let
x\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
.
With Proposition
Proposition
we have

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f(x)|&\lesssim&\left|\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\setminus B_{\frac{|x|}{2}}(0)}\frac{f(x+y)+f(x-y)-2f(x)}{|y|^{n+s}}dy\right|\\
&&+\left|\int_{B_{\frac{|x|}{2}}(0)}\frac{f(x+y)+f(x-y)-2f(x)}{|y|^{n+s}}dy\right|\\
&\eqqcolon&C_{1}+C_{2}.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

Let
\omega_{n}
denote the
n-1
-dimensional volume of the sphere
S^{n-1}
. For the first term we have

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554ptC_{1}&\leq&2\frac{2^{n+s}}{|x|^{n+s}}\|f\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}+2|f(x)|\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\setminus B_{\frac{|x|}{2}}(0)}\frac{1}{|y|^{n+s}}dy\\
&\leq&\frac{2^{n+s+1}}{|x|^{n+s}}\|f\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}+\frac{2C}{|x|^{k}}\omega_{n}\int_{\frac{|x|}{2}}^{\infty}\frac{r^{n-1}}{r^{n+s}}dr\\
&=&\frac{2^{n+s+1}}{|x|^{n+s}}\|f\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}+\frac{2C\omega_{n}}{|x|^{k}}\frac{1}{s}\frac{2^{s}}{|x|^{s}}.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

Regarding the second term, with the second order Taylor expansion (
4.2.4
) we obtain

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554ptC_{2}&\leq&\sup_{\tilde{x}\in B_{\frac{|x|}{2}}(x)}|\nabla^{2}f(\tilde{x})|\int_{B_{\frac{|x|}{2}}(0)}\frac{1}{|y|^{n+s-2}}dy\leq\frac{2^{l}C}{|x|^{l}}\omega_{n}\int_{0}^{\frac{|x|}{2}}\frac{r^{n-1}}{r^{n+s-2}}dr=\frac{2^{l}C\omega_{n}}{|x|^{l}}\frac{1}{2-s}\frac{|x|^{2-s}}{|2|^{2-s}}\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

\square

Having now covered the basics for fractional Laplacians, we turn to their inverse, the Riesz potentials.

4.3  Riesz potentials

Concluding this section, we introduce the Riesz potentials, which are inverse to the corresponding fractional Laplacians.
These operators are defined via singular integrals, see
[
Ste70
, Chapter V]
.

Definition
(Riesz potential).

Let
0<s<n
and
f\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
.
Then the fractional Laplacian operator
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}
is defined by

I^{s}f(x)=C_{n,s}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}\frac{f(x-y)}{|y|^{n-s}}

(4.3.1)

with
C_{n,s}=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{n-s}{2}\right)}{2^{s}\pi^{\frac{n}{2}}\Gamma\left(\frac{s}{2}\right)}
.

\square

From
[
Ste70
, Chapter V]
we also obtain a result regarding the pointwise convergence of these singular integrals as well as potential
L^{p}
-boundedness.

Theorem
(Hardy-Littlewood-Sobolev theorem of fractional integration).

Let
0<s<n
,
1\leq p<q<\infty
,
\frac{1}{q}=\frac{1}{p}-\frac{s}{n}
, in particular
p<\frac{n}{s}
.
Then for any
f\in L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
the integral (
4.3.1
), defining
I^{s}f
, converges absolutely for almost every
x\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
.

If, in addition,
1<p
, then there exists
C_{p,q}>0
such that for all
f\in L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})

\|I^{s}f\|_{L^{q}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\leq C_{p,q}\|f\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}

\square

Proof
.

See
[
Ste70
]
, Theorem 1 in Chapter V.

\square

Thanks to this result, we can extend the definition of the Riesz Potentials from
\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
to
L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
,
1\leq p<\frac{n}{s}
.
Regarding the following results, we will not consider the original definition though, but rather focus on the interplay with the Fourier transform.
Like Riesz transforms and fractional Laplacians, Riesz potentials are multiplier operators.

Proposition
(Fourier symbol of Riesz potentials).

Let
0<s<n
.
The corresponding Riesz potential is given on the Fourier transform side by multiplication with the function
(2\pi\xi)^{-s}
.
That is, for any
f\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
we have

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt{\mathcal{F}}(I^{s}f)(\xi)=(2\pi|\xi|)^{-s}{\mathcal{F}}(f)(\xi).\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

\square

Proof
.

See
[
Ste70
]
, Lemma 1 in Chapter V.

\square

As in the subsections above, we list some easy to see consequences of this result.
First, we can immediately confirm that indeed fractional Laplacians and Riesz potentials are inverse to each other.
Second and third, we again get an integration by parts formula and a semigroup property.

Lemma
.

Let
s\in(0,n)
and
f\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
.
Then

I^{s}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f={(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}I^{s}f=f.

\square

Proof
.

We have
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
due to Lemma
Lemma
and Lemma
Lemma
.
Moreover, we have
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
because obviously
{\mathcal{F}}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f)\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
.
Combining these, for any
1\leq p\leq 2
we have
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f\in L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
.
Therefore, the application of the Riesz potential
I^{s}
to
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f
is well defined with Theorem
Theorem
, choosing
p_{0}\in\left(1,\min\left\{\frac{n}{s},2\right\}\right)
and
\frac{1}{q_{0}}=\frac{1}{p_{0}}-\frac{s}{n}
.
Furthermore, we can find a sequence
(g_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
such that
\|g_{k}-{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\overset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0
for all
1\leq p\leq 2
.
In particular, this implies that

\|{\mathcal{F}}(g_{k})-{\mathcal{F}}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f)\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\overset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0\quad\text{for all }1\leq p\leq 2\text{ and }\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{p}=1

(4.3.2)

thanks to the Hausdorff-Young inequality.
Additionally, because of Theorem
Theorem
,
I_{s}g_{k}
converges against
I^{s}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f)
in
L^{q_{0}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
and therefore also in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
.
For the Fourier transform of
I^{s}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f)
in the sense of tempered distributions we then have

{\mathcal{F}}(I^{s}g_{k})\overset{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{F}}(I^{s}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f))\quad\text{for }k\rightarrow\infty.

Now let
h\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
. We have

C\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}{\mathcal{F}}(I^{s}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f)h=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}{\mathcal{F}}(I^{s}g_{k})h=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(2\pi|\xi|)^{-s}{\mathcal{F}}(g_{k})(\xi)h(\xi)\,d\xi

thanks to Proposition
Proposition
.
We now observe that
(2\pi|\cdot|)^{-s}h\in L^{\tilde{p}}
for some
1<\tilde{p}<\min\left\{\frac{n}{s},2\right\}
since
s<n
.
With (
4.3.2
), via the duality of
L^{p}
-spaces and the definition of the fractional Laplacian,

C=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}{\mathcal{F}}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f)(\xi)(2\pi|\xi|)^{-s}h(\xi)\,d\xi=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(2\pi|\xi|)^{s}{\mathcal{F}}(f)(\xi)(2\pi|\xi|)^{-s}h(\xi)\,d\xi=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}{\mathcal{F}}(f)h,

yielding
{\mathcal{F}}(I^{s}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f)={\mathcal{F}}(f)\in\mathcal{S}
and therefore
I^{s}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}f=f
.

On the other hand, with the definition of the fractional Laplacian we have

{\mathcal{F}}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}}I^{s}f)(\xi)=(2\pi|\xi|)^{s}{\mathcal{F}}(I^{s}f)(\xi)=(2\pi|\xi|)^{s}(2\pi|\xi|)^{-s}{\mathcal{F}}(f)(\xi)={\mathcal{F}}(f)(\xi),

which also justifies applying the fractional Laplacian to
I^{s}f
since the inverse Fourier transform of
(2\pi|\xi|)^{s}{\mathcal{F}}(I^{s}f)
is obviously well defined.

\square

Lemma
(An integration by parts formula for Riesz potentials).

Let
f,g\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
and
0<s<n
.
Then

\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}gI^{s}f=\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}I^{s}f\,g.

\square

Proof
.

Analogous to Lemma
Lemma
.

\square

Lemma
(Semigroup property of Riesz potentials).

Let
f\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
and
s_{1},s_{2}>0
with
s_{1}+s_{2}<n
.
Then

I^{s_{1}}I^{s_{2}}f=I^{s_{1}+s_{2}}f.

\square

Proof
.

First note that the composition is well defined. With Theorem
Theorem
we have
I^{s_{2}}f\in L^{q}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
for all
\frac{1}{q}=\frac{1}{p}-\frac{s}{n}
where
1<p<\frac{n}{s_{2}}
since
f\in L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
for all
1\leq p\leq\infty
.
Because of

q=\frac{pn}{n-s_{2}p}\overset{p\rightarrow 1}{\longrightarrow}\frac{n}{n-s_{2}}<\frac{n}{n-(n-{s_{1}})}=\frac{n}{s_{1}},

we find
1<p<\frac{n}{s_{2}}
such that
q<\frac{n}{s_{1}}
.
Then, again thanks to Theorem
Theorem
,
I^{s_{1}}(I^{s_{2}}f)
is well defined.
The rest of the proof is analogous to Lemma
Lemma
.

\square

5  Trace Theorems

The goal of this section is to establish the blackbox estimates we use throughout the previous sections.
These complex estimates are listed and proven in Subsection
5.7
.
The proofs depend on combining multiple smaller building blocks, which we collect in Subsections
5.4
-
5.6
.

The first and largest group of these building blocks originate from Triebel-Lizorkin or Besov-Lipschitz space characterizations via the generalized Poisson kernel, which are due to a recent result from Bui-Candy,
[
BC17
]
.
Providing the necessary background, in the first subsection we briefly introduce the framework of Tiebel-Lizorkin and Besov-Lipschitz spaces as well as the Bui-Candy result.
In the second subsection, we show that this result is indeed applicable to our situation and obtain a variety of space characterizations.
Since Triebel-Lizorkin or Besov-Lipschitz spaces include fractional Sobolev spaces, the
BMO
-space and Hölder spaces, we obtain a variety of estimates from these space characterizations.
These are listed and proven in Subsection
5.4
.
The second group of estimates originate from the theory of so-called square functions.
This theory is closely related to the theory of maximal functions, which provides a third group of estimates.
These estimates are shown in Subsection
5.5
and Subsection
5.6
respectively.
In their original form, many of these building blocks are
L^{p}
-estimates.
From these we can easily obtain estimates on the much finer Lorentz scale via interpolation.
Therefore, we introduce these Lorentz spaces and give the corresponding interpolation theorem in Subsection
5.3
.

5.1  A short introduction to Triebel-Lizorkin and Besov-Lipschitz spaces

The theory of Triebel-Lizorkin and Lipschitz-Besov spaces provides a unified framework for a wide variety of important and frequently used function spaces.
In this subsection, all functions, distributions etc. are defined on
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
.
Therefore, we omit
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
in the notation of the function spaces, i.e. instead of
A({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
we write
A
.
First, we give a short list of spaces included in this framework and state some definitions, from which we will procede to the central result from
[
