BC17
]
.
At the end of this subsection, we collect some elementary embeddings and lifting properties.

The Triebel-Lizorkin spaces are denoted with
F^{\alpha}_{p,q}
, the Besov-Lipschitz spaces with
B^{\alpha}_{p,q}
, where
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
,
0<p,q\leq\infty
.
Additionally, there are their homogeneous counterparts,
\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{p,q}
and
\dot{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}
.
All the function spaces listed below can be identified with the corresponding Triebel-Lizorkin or Besov-Lipschitz spaces, the respective norms are equivalent.

\mathscr{C}^{s}

=

B^{s}_{\infty,\infty}

if
0<s

[
Tri83
, Theorem 2.5.7.]

\mathcal{C}^{s}

=

B^{s}_{\infty,\infty}

if
0<s\notin\mathbb{Z}

[
Tri83
, Proof of Theorem 2.5.7.]

\Lambda^{s}_{p,q}

=

B^{s}_{p,q}

if
0<s
,
1\leq p<\infty
,
1\leq q\leq\infty

[
Tri83
, Theorem 2.5.7.]

W^{s}_{p}

=

B^{s}_{p,p}=F^{s}_{p,p}

if
0<s\notin\mathbb{Z}
,
1\leq p<\infty

[
Tri83
, Proof of Theorem 2.5.7.]

h^{p}

=

F^{0}_{p,2}

if
0<p<\infty

[
Tri83
, Theorem 2.5.8.1.]

\mathcal{H}^{p}

=

\dot{F}^{0}_{p,2}

if
0<p<\infty

[
Tri83
, Theorem 5.2.4.]

L^{p}

=

F^{0}_{p,2}

if
1<p<\infty

[
Tri83
, Proof of Theorem 2.5.6.]

L^{p}

=

\dot{F}^{0}_{p,2}

if
1<p<\infty

[
Tri83
, Theorem 5.2.3.1.(ii)]

H^{s}_{p}

=

F^{s}_{p,2}

if
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
1<p<\infty

[
Tri83
, Theorem 2.5.6.(i)]

W^{m}_{p}

=

F^{m}_{p,2}

if
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
1<p<\infty

[
Tri83
, Theorem 2.5.6.(ii)]

bmo

=

F^{0}_{\infty,2}

[
Tri83
, Theorem 2.5.8.2.]

BMO

=

\dot{F}^{0}_{\infty,2}

[
Tri83
, Theorem 5.2.4.]

Here,
\mathscr{C}^{s}
are the Zygmund spaces,
\mathcal{C}^{s}
the Hölder spaces,
\Lambda^{s}_{p,q}
the Besov (or Lipschitz) spaces,
W^{s}_{p}
the Slobodeckij spaces,
\mathcal{H}^{p}
the Hardy spaces and
h^{p}
the local Hardy spaces,
H^{s}_{p}
the Bessel-potential spaces and
W^{m}_{p}
the Sobolev spaces.

BMO
is the space of functions of bounded mean oscillation, the dual of
\mathcal{H}_{1}
(see
[
FS72
]
).

bmo
is the inhomogeneous counterpart of
BMO
and accordingly the dual of
h^{1}
(see
[
Gol79
]
).
For definitions of these spaces see
[
Tri83
, 2.2.2.]
.
Additionally, the definitions of
\mathcal{C}^{s}
,
W^{s}_{p}
,
H^{s}_{p}
and
BMO
are stated in the next subsection.

Since we mainly use the homogeneous spaces in this work,
\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{p,q}
and
\dot{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}
, we only give their definition in this subsection and refer to
[
Tri83
, 2.3.1. and 2.3.4.]
for the definition of the inhomogeneous Triebel-Lizorkin and Besov-Lipschitz spaces.
Working with the homogeneous spaces poses a difficulty though. While their inhomogeneous counterparts are defined as subsets of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
, the space of tempered distributions, the elements of the homogeneous spaces are tempered distributions modulo polynomials,
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}/\mathcal{P}
. The corresponding spaces can still be identified with for example
L^{p}
though, because in each equivalence class there can only be one element with finite
L^{p}
-norm since for each polynomial
P\neq 0
we have
\|P\|_{L^{p}}=\infty
(cf.
[
Tri83
, 5.2.3. and 5.2.4.]
).

We now state the definition of the homogeneous Triebel-Lizorkin and Besov-Lipschitz spaces following
[
BC17
, Section 1]
(who in turn refer to
[
Tri83
, 5.1.]
and
[
Pee76
, Chapter 3]
).
In the following, for a function
\phi\colon{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
j\in\mathbb{Z}
we let
\phi_{j}(x)=2^{jn}\phi(2^{j}x)
denote the dyadic dilation of
\phi
.
Simultaneously,
\phi_{t}(x)=t^{-n}\phi(t^{-1}x)
denotes the standard dilation for
t>0
.
The type of dilation we are using should always be clear, depending on whether we are in a discrete or continuous scenario.
For this definition we further use convolutions between Schwartz functions and tempered distributions.
For
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
, the convolution
\phi\ast f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
with
\phi\in\mathcal{S}
is defined by

\langle\phi*f,\psi\rangle_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{S}}=\langle f,\tilde{\phi}\ast\psi\rangle_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{S}},\qquad\text{for all }\psi\in\mathcal{S},

where
\tilde{\phi}(x)=\phi(-x)
.
While these convolutions are first defined only as a tempered distribution, we actually have
\phi*f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
, see
[
Gra14
, Theorem 2.3.20]
.

Definition
(Triebel-Lizorkin and Besov-Lipschitz spaces).

Fix
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}
such that
\operatorname{supp}\widehat{\varphi}=\{1/2\leq|\xi|\leq 2\}
and for every
\xi\neq 0

\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\widehat{\varphi}(2^{-j}\xi)\widehat{\varphi}(2^{-j}\xi)=1.

Let
0<p,q\leq\infty
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
.
The homogeneous Besov-Lipschitz spaces are defined as

\dot{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}=\bigg\{f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}/\mathcal{P}\,;\,\|f\|_{\dot{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}}=\bigg(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(2^{j\alpha}\|\varphi_{j}*f\|_{L^{p}}\right)^{q}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{q}}<\infty\bigg\}.

(5.1.1)

For
p\neq\infty
, the homogeneous Triebel-Lizorkin spaces are defined as

\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{p,q}=\bigg\{f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}/\mathcal{P}\,;\,\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{p,q}}=\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(2^{j\alpha}|\varphi_{j}*f|\right)^{q}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{q}}\bigg\|_{L^{p}}<\infty\bigg\}.

(5.1.2)

For
p=\infty
, the corresponding Triebel-Lizorkin spaces are defined as

\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{\infty,q}=\bigg\{f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}/\mathcal{P}\,;\,\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{\infty,q}}=\sup_{Q}\bigg(\frac{1}{|Q|}\int_{Q}\sum_{j\geq-l(Q)}\left(2^{j\alpha}|\varphi_{j}*f|\right)^{q}dx\bigg)^{\frac{1}{q}}<\infty\bigg\}.

(5.1.3)

where the supremum is over all dyadic cubes
Q
and
l(Q)=\log_{2}(\text{ side length of $Q$ })
.

\square

For
q=\infty
, the interpretation is

\|f\|_{\dot{B}^{\alpha}_{p,\infty}}=\sup_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(2^{j\alpha}\|\varphi_{j}*f\|_{L^{p}}\right),\qquad\\
\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{p,\infty}}=\|\sup_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(2^{j\alpha}|\varphi_{j}*f|\right)\|_{L^{p}}

and for the special case of the Triebel-Lizorkin space
\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{\infty,\infty}

\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{\infty,\infty}}=\sup_{Q}\sup_{j\geq-l(Q)}\frac{1}{|Q|}\int_{Q}2^{j\alpha}|\varphi_{j}*f|dx.

Further note that the quasi-norms applied to polynomials indeed yield zero since the convolution of any polynomial
\rho\in\mathcal{P}
with any of the
\varphi_{j}
is zero. This can be seen on the side of the Fourier transforms since the support of a polynomials’ Fourier transform is always
\{0\}
.
We have

\langle\varphi_{j}\ast\rho,\psi\rangle_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{S}}=\langle\rho,\tilde{\varphi_{j}}\ast\psi\rangle_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{S}}=\langle{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}\rho,{\mathcal{F}}\tilde{\varphi_{j}}{\mathcal{F}}\psi\rangle_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{S}}=0

for all
\psi\in\mathcal{S}
.

It is well established that this definition is independent of the choice of the kernel
\varphi
in the sense that replacing
\varphi
by another kernel satisfying the same conditions yields equivalent norms, see
[
Tri83
, Theorem 5.1.5.]
. Sustaining the independence of the chosen kernel is also the reason for the deviating definition of
\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{\infty,q}
.
The dependence on the kernel when choosing the natural extension to
p=\infty
has been remarked in
[
Tri83
, 2.3.1.4.]
.
For a discussion of the chosen definition for
\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{\infty,q}
and the independence of the kernel, see
[
FJ90
, Section 5]
.
Originally, these definitions were formulated for a a family of kernels that were not necessarily all dilations of one single kernel - that is just the easiest way to realize these conditions (cf.
[
Pee76
, Chapter 3, pp. 47-49]
and
[
Tri83
, 5.1.3]
).

The definition via a single kernel and its dyadic dilations is more interesting to us though, since it naturally leads to a continuous version of the Triebel-Lizorkin and Besov-Lipschitz quasi-norms.
The summation over the convolutions with dyadic dilation
s can be replaced by an integral with the dilation parameter as continuous integration variable.
For
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
,
0<p<\infty
, and
0<q\leq\infty
we have

\|f\|_{\dot{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}}\approx\bigg(\int_{0}^{\infty}(t^{-\alpha}\|\varphi_{t}*f\|_{L^{p}})^{q}\frac{dt}{t}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{q}},

\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{p,q}}\approx\bigg\|\bigg(\int_{0}^{\infty}(t^{-\alpha}|\varphi_{t}*f|)^{q}\frac{dt}{t}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{q}}\bigg\|_{L^{p}}

for all
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}/\mathcal{P}
, see the introduction of
[
BPT96
]
and Section 6(d) for the arguments.
Additionally, Theorem 1 in
[
BT00
]
covers Triebel-Lizorkin spaces for
p=\infty
,

\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{\infty,q}}\approx\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}},r>0}\bigg\{\frac{1}{|B(x,r)|}\int_{B(x,r)}\int_{0}^{r}(t^{-\alpha}|\varphi_{t}*f|)^{q}\frac{dt}{t}\,dy\bigg\}^{\frac{1}{q}}

for all
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}/\mathcal{P}
.

Over the years, looser conditions for kernels
\psi\in\mathcal{S}
, under which replacing
\varphi
with
\psi
in (
5.1.1
), (
5.1.2
) and (
5.1.3
) yields equivalent norms, were developed (see
[
Tri83
]
,
[
BPT96
]
,
[
BT00
]
).
Of key interest to us though are the results in
[
BC17
]
.
There, suitable conditions for non-smooth kernels
\psi\notin\mathcal{S}
are established, specifically with fractional derivatives of the Poisson kernel in mind.

One of the problems arising when working with a non-smooth kernel
\psi\notin\mathcal{S}
is, that it is not possible to define the convolution
\psi*f
for arbitrary
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.
To deal with this problem, distributions of bounded growth are used in
[
BC17
]
.

Definition
(Distributions of growth
l
).

(i)

A tempered distribution
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
is of growth
l\geq 0
if for any
\phi\in\mathcal{S}
we have
\phi*f=\mathcal{O}(|x|^{l})
as
|x|\rightarrow\infty
.

(ii)

Assume
f
is a distribution of growth
l
.
Then if
(1+|\cdot|)^{l}\psi\in L^{1}
, the convolution
\psi*f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
is given as

\langle\psi*f,\phi\rangle_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{S}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\psi(x)(\widetilde{\phi}*f)(x)dx,\quad\phi\in\text{for all }\mathcal{S},

where
\widetilde{\phi}(x)=\phi(-x)
.

\square

While we are able to define the convolutions themselves by restricting the admissible distributions, as further unspecified tempered distributions the convolutions still make no sense in (
5.1.1
) or (
5.1.2
).
In the main result of
[
BC17
]
, additional conditions ensure that these convolutions are continuous functions.
Before we discuss this result, we give an example for distributions of growth
l\in\mathbb{N}
- polynomials.

Lemma
.

Let
\rho\in\mathcal{P}
,
l=\deg(\rho)
.
Then
\rho
is a distribution of growth exactly
l
, meaning that
\rho
is not a distribution of growth
\tilde{l}
for any
\tilde{l}<l
.

\square

Proof
.

We first show that
\rho
is indeed a distribution of growth
l
.
There exists
C_{1}>0
such that
|\rho(x)|\leq C_{1}(1+|x|)^{l}
for all
x\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
.
Now let
\phi\in\mathcal{S}
.
Then there exists
C_{2}>0
such that
|\phi(x)|\leq C_{2}(1+|x|)^{-n-1-l}
for all
x\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
.
Combining these two estimates, since for
y\notin B_{x}(x)
we have
|x-y|\geq|y|/2
,

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt|\phi*\rho(x)|&\leq&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\phi(x-y)\rho(y)|\,dy\\
&=&\int_{B_{\frac{x}{2}}(x)}|\phi(x-y)\rho(y)|\,dy+\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\setminus B_{\frac{x}{2}}(x)}|\phi(x-y)\rho(y)|\,dy\\
&\leq&C_{1}\|\phi\|_{L^{1}}(1+2|x|)^{l}+\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\setminus B_{\frac{x}{2}}(x)}C_{1}(1+|y|)^{l}\,C_{2}\left(1+\frac{|y|}{2}\right)^{-n-1-l}\,dy\\
&\leq&C_{1}\|\phi\|_{L^{1}}(1+2|x|)^{l}+2^{n+1+l}C_{1}C_{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}(1+|y|)^{-n-1}\,dy\\
&=&\mathcal{O}(|x|^{l})\quad\text{as }|x|\rightarrow\infty.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

Since the growth of this convolution is bounded, it is easy to see with Fubini’s theorem that this convolution in the standard integral sense coincides with the convolution in the distributional sense, see
[
Gra14
, Definition 2.3.13]
for the definition.
Therefore,
\rho
is a distribution of growth
l
.

Now assume
\tilde{l}<l
.
In order to show that
\rho
is no distribution of growth
\tilde{l}
, we construct a ray along which the polynomial grows according to its degree. We then examine how the convolution with a compactly supported, positive kernel behaves along that ray.
Let
\rho=\sum_{|\gamma|\leq l}c_{\gamma}x^{\gamma}
where the sum is over multiindices
\gamma\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}
.
Denote with
\tilde{\gamma}
the largest element of
\Gamma_{l}\coloneqq\{\gamma\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}\,;\,|\gamma|=l\text{ and }c_{\gamma}\neq 0\}
in terms of the lexicographic order.
Set

C\coloneqq\frac{1}{|c_{\tilde{\gamma}}|}\sum_{|\gamma|=l}|c_{\gamma}|,

v\coloneqq\left(C^{(l+1)^{n-i}}\right)_{i=1,\ldots,n}=\left(C^{(l+1)^{n-1}},C^{(l+1)^{n-2}},\ldots,C^{l+1},C\right)\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}.

The vector
v
will be the direction of the aforementioned ray.
We first observe that

c_{\tilde{\gamma}}\left|v^{\tilde{\gamma}}\right|-\sum_{|\gamma|=l,\gamma\neq\tilde{\gamma}}|c_{\gamma}|\left|v^{\gamma}\right|\geq\frac{|c_{\tilde{\gamma}}|}{C}\left|v^{\tilde{\gamma}}\right|.

(5.1.4)

This is clear if
\tilde{\gamma}=le_{n}
, because then
\Gamma_{l}=\{le_{n}\}
.
Therefore, assume
\tilde{\gamma}\neq le_{n}
.
For
\gamma\in\Gamma_{l}
,
\gamma\neq\tilde{\gamma}
, let
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
be minimal with the property that
\tilde{\gamma}_{i}>\gamma_{i}
.
Since
C\geq 1
,

\frac{\left|v^{\tilde{\gamma}}\right|}{\left|v^{\gamma}\right|}=\frac{\left(C^{(l+1)^{n-i}}\right)^{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}-\gamma_{i}}\left|(v_{i+1,\ldots,n})^{\tilde{\gamma}_{i+1,\ldots,n}}\right|}{\left|(v_{i+1,\ldots,n})^{\gamma_{i+1,\ldots,n}}\right|}\geq\frac{C^{(l+1)^{n-i}}\cdot 1}{\left(C^{(l+1)^{n-(i+1)}}\right)^{l}}=C^{(l+1)^{n-(i+1)}}\geq C.

With this estimate and the definition of
C
we obtain

|c_{\tilde{\gamma}}|\left|v^{\tilde{\gamma}}\right|=\frac{1}{C}\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{l}}|c_{\gamma}|\left|v^{\tilde{\gamma}}\right|=\frac{|c_{\tilde{\gamma}}|}{C}\left|v^{\tilde{\gamma}}\right|+\sum_{|\gamma|=l,\gamma\neq\tilde{\gamma}}|c_{\gamma}|\frac{\left|v^{\tilde{\gamma}}\right|}{C}\geq\frac{|c_{\tilde{\gamma}}|}{C}\left|v^{\tilde{\gamma}}\right|+\sum_{|\gamma|=l,\gamma\neq\tilde{\gamma}}|c_{\gamma}|\left|v^{\gamma}\right|

and therefore prove (
5.1.4
).
Thus, for
s\geq 1

|\rho(sv)|\geq s^{l}\left(c_{\tilde{\gamma}}\left|v^{\tilde{\gamma}}\right|-\sum_{|\gamma|=l,\gamma\neq\tilde{\gamma}}|c_{\gamma}|\left|v^{\gamma}\right|\right)-\sum_{|\gamma|<l}|c_{\gamma}|\left|(sv)^{\gamma}\right|\geq s^{l}\frac{|c_{\tilde{\gamma}}|}{C}\left|v^{\tilde{\gamma}}\right|-s^{l-1}\sum_{|\gamma|<l}|c_{\gamma}|\left|(v)^{\gamma}\right|

and apparently there is an
R_{1}>0
, such that for all
s>R_{1}

|\rho(sv)|\geq s^{l}\frac{|c_{\tilde{\gamma}}|}{2C}\left|v^{\tilde{\gamma}}\right|\geq s^{l}\frac{|c_{\tilde{\gamma}}|}{2C}.

Thus, we have shown that
\rho
grows sufficiently fast in the chosen direction.
We also need to transfer this growth to neighborhoods along the ray though.
Since the derivatives of
\rho
are polynomials of degree
l-1
, there exists
R_{2}>0
such that

|\nabla\rho(x)|\leq\frac{|c_{\tilde{\gamma}}|}{4C|v|^{l}}(1+|x|)^{l}

for all
x\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
,
|x|\geq R_{2}
.
Thus, for
x\in B_{1}(sv)
and
s>R_{3}\coloneqq\max\{R_{1},|v|^{-1}(R_{2}+1)\}

|\rho(x)|\geq|\rho(sv)|-\|\nabla\rho\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1}(sv))}\geq s^{l}\frac{|c_{\tilde{\gamma}}|}{2C}-\frac{|c_{\tilde{\gamma}}|}{4C|v|^{l}}(1+(|sv|-1))^{l}=s^{l}\frac{|c_{\tilde{\gamma}}|}{4C}.

(5.1.5)

Let
\phi\in\mathcal{S}
with
\operatorname{supp}(\phi)=\overline{B_{1}(0)}
and
\phi>0
on
B_{1}(0)
.
Then, for
s>R_{3}
,

|(\phi*\rho)(sv)|=\int_{B_{1}(sv)}\phi(sv-y)|\rho(y)|\,dy\geq\frac{|c_{\tilde{\gamma}}|\|\phi\|_{L^{1}}}{4C|v|^{l}}|sv|^{l}.

(5.1.6)

Therefore,
\rho
can not be a distribution of growth
\tilde{l}
.

\square

We now turn to the main result of
[
BC17
]
- the characterization of Triebel-Lizorkin and Besov-Lipschitz spaces with Poisson like kernels.
There are three main conditions the kernels
\psi\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
have to satisfy in order to obtain the norm equivalencies, reading as follows.
The parameters
\Lambda\geq 0
and
m,r\in\mathbb{R}
are chosen depending on the characterized space,
[\cdot]
denotes the integer part of the inserted number.

(C1)

(Cancellation)

{\mathcal{F}}(\psi)\in\mathcal{C}^{n+1+[\Lambda]}({\mathbb{R}^{n}}\setminus\{0\})
such that for every
|\kappa|\leq n+1+[\Lambda]

\partial_{\kappa}{\mathcal{F}}(\psi)=\mathcal{O}(|\xi|^{r-|\kappa|})\qquad\text{as }|\xi|\rightarrow 0.

(C2)

(Tauberian condition)

For every direction
\xi\in S^{n-1}
there exist
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
(depending on
\xi
) with
0<2a\leq b
such that for every
a<t<b

|{\mathcal{F}}(\psi)(t\xi)|>0.

(C3)

(Smoothness)

{\mathcal{F}}(\psi)\in\mathcal{C}^{n+1+[\Lambda]}({\mathbb{R}^{n}}\setminus\{0\})
such that for every
|\kappa|\leq n+1+[\Lambda]

\partial_{\kappa}{\mathcal{F}}(\psi)=\mathcal{O}(|\xi|^{-n-m})\qquad\text{as }|\xi|\rightarrow\infty.

Following
[
BC17
]
, we split the characterization into two theorems - one for each estimate required for the norm equivalency, stressing the different sets of assumptions.
Note that, since for our method we only need to estimate upwards against special cases of the Triebel-Lizorkin and Besov-Lipschitz quasi-norms, we technically only need the first theorem.
We still include the second one for completeness though.

Theorem
(Theorem 1.1 of
[
BC17
]
).

Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and
0<p,q\leq\infty
.
Let
l\geq 0
with
l>\alpha-\frac{n}{p}
.
Assume
(1+|\cdot|)^{l}\psi\in L^{1}
.

(i)

Let
f\in\dot{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}
.
Assume that
\psi
satisfies
(C1)
and
(C3)
for
\Lambda=\frac{n}{p}
,
r>\alpha
and
m>\Lambda-\alpha
.
Then there exists a polynomial
\rho\in\mathcal{P}
such that
f-\rho
is a distribution of growth
l
and we have

\bigg(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}(2^{j\alpha}\|\psi_{j}*(f-\rho)\|_{L^{p}})^{q}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{q}}\lesssim\|f\|_{\dot{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}},

and the continuous version

\bigg(\int_{0}^{\infty}(t^{-\alpha}\|\psi_{t}*(f-\rho)\|_{L^{p}})^{q}\frac{dt}{t}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{q}}\lesssim\|f\|_{\dot{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}}.

(ii)

Let
f\in\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{p,q}
.
Assume that
\psi
satisfies
(C1)
and
(C3)
for

\Lambda=\max\left\{\frac{n}{p},\frac{n}{q}\right\}
(with
\Lambda=n
when
p=q=\infty
),

r>\alpha
and
m>\Lambda-\alpha
.
Then there exists a polynomial
\rho\in\mathcal{P}
such that
f-\rho
is a distribution of growth
l
and
for
p<\infty
we have

\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}(2^{j\alpha}|\psi_{j}*(f-\rho)|)^{q}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{q}}\bigg\|_{L^{p}}\lesssim\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{p,q}},

and the continuous version

\bigg\|\bigg(\int_{0}^{\infty}(t^{-\alpha}|\psi_{t}*(f-\rho)|)^{q}\frac{dt}{t}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{q}}\bigg\|_{L^{p}}\lesssim\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{p,q}}.

For
p=\infty
we have

\sup_{Q}\bigg(\frac{1}{|Q|}\int_{Q}\sum_{j\geq-l(Q)}\left(2^{j\alpha}|\psi_{j}*(f-\rho)|\right)^{q}dx\bigg)^{\frac{1}{q}}\lesssim\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{\infty,q}},

and the continuous version

\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}},r>0}\bigg\{\frac{1}{|B(x,r)|}\int_{T(B(x,r))}(t^{-\alpha}|\psi_{t}*(f-\rho)|)^{q}\frac{dt}{t}\,dy\bigg\}^{\frac{1}{q}}\lesssim\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{\infty,q}},

where
T(B(x,r))=\{(y,t)\in{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}\,:\,|y-x|<r-t\}
is the “tent” over the ball
B(x,r)
.

\square

Proof
.

See
[
BC17
]
.
Theorem 1.1 being formulated for the Peetre maximal function applied to the convolutions poses no problem since the maximal function majorizes the convolution, see Remark 1.1(iv) in
[
BC17
]
.
The continuous version of the result with integrals instead of sums is confirmed but left for the reader to prove, see the end of the introduction in
[
BC17
]
.
For details regarding the precise formulation and modifications of the proof they refer to
[
BPT96
]
,
[
BPT97
]
and
[
BT00
]
.

Note that for the continuous version of the
\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{\infty,q}
-characterization we integrate over tents while in
[
BT00
]
they integrate over cylinders.
But since
|B(x,r)|=2^{-n}|B(x,2r)|
and because of the inclusions
T(B(x,r))\subset B(x,r)\times[0,r]\subset T(B(x,2r))
we have

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}},r>0}\bigg\{\frac{1}{|B(x,r)|}\int_{T(B(x,r))}(t^{-\alpha}|\varphi_{t}*f|)^{q}\frac{dt}{t}\,dy\bigg\}^{\frac{1}{q}}\\
\vskip 4.30554pt\leq\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}},r>0}\bigg\{\frac{1}{|B(x,r)|}\int_{B(x,r)\times[0,r]}(t^{-\alpha}|\varphi_{t}*f|)^{q}\frac{dt}{t}\,dy\bigg\}^{\frac{1}{q}}\\
\vskip 4.30554pt\leq\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}},r>0}\bigg\{\frac{1}{2^{-n}|B(x,2r)|}\int_{T(B(x,2r))}(t^{-\alpha}|\varphi_{t}*f|)^{q}\frac{dt}{t}\,dy\bigg\}^{\frac{1}{q}}\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

and therefore see that both versions are equivalent.

\square

Of course, the polynomials depending on
f
appearing in the estimates are undesirable.
We will deal with those later by showing that for our specific kernels we may always choose
\rho=0
, see Theorem
Theorem
.

Theorem
(Theorem 1.3 of
[
BC17
]
).

Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
,
0<p,q\leq\infty
.
Assume
f
is a distribution of growth
l\geq 0
.
Suppose
(1+|\cdot|)^{l}\psi\in L^{1}
and
\psi
satisfies
(C2)
as well as
(C3)
for every
m\in\mathbb{R}
and some
\Lambda\geq l
.
Then for every
j\in\mathbb{Z}
the convolution
\psi_{j}*f
is a continuous function.
Moreover,

(i)

if
(C3)
holds for
\Lambda=\max\left\{l,\frac{n}{p}\right\}
and every
m\in\mathbb{R}
respectively , then

\|f\|_{\dot{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}}\lesssim\bigg(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}(2^{j\alpha}\|\psi_{j}*f\|_{L^{p}})^{q}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{q}},

\|f\|_{\dot{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}}\lesssim\bigg(\int_{0}^{\infty}(t^{-\alpha}\|\psi_{t}*f\|_{L^{p}})^{q}\frac{dt}{t}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{q}}.

(ii)

if
(C3)
holds for
\Lambda=\max\left\{l,\frac{n}{p},\frac{n}{q}\right\}
and every
m\in\mathbb{R}
respectively, then for
p<\infty

\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{p,q}}\lesssim\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}(2^{j\alpha}|\psi_{j}*f|)^{q}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{q}}\bigg\|_{L^{p}},

\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{p,q}}\lesssim\bigg\|\bigg(\int_{0}^{\infty}(t^{-\alpha}|\psi_{t}*f|)^{q}\frac{dt}{t}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{q}}\bigg\|_{L^{p}}.

In the case
p=\infty
we have

\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{\infty,q}}\lesssim\sup_{Q}\bigg(\frac{1}{|Q|}\int_{Q}\sum_{j\geq-l(Q)}\left(2^{j\alpha}|\psi_{j}*f|\right)^{q}dx\bigg)^{\frac{1}{q}},

\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{\infty,q}}\lesssim\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}},r>0}\bigg\{\frac{1}{|B(x,r)|}\int_{T(B(x,r))}(t^{-\alpha}|\psi_{t}*f|)^{q}\frac{dt}{t}\,dy\bigg\}^{\frac{1}{q}},

where again
T(B(x,r))=\{(y,t)\in{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}\,:\,|y-x|<r-t\}
is the “tent” over
B(x,r)
.

\square

Proof
.

See
[
BC17
]
.
The continuous version of the result with integrals instead of sums is confirmed but left for the reader to prove, see the end of the introduction in
[
BC17
]
.
Because the mentioned “standard” Tauberian condition is weaker than
(C2)
, we do not need to make adjustments regarding the assumptions.
For details regarding the precise formulation and modifications of the proof they refer to
[
BPT96
]
,
[
BPT97
]
and
[
BT00
]
.

While in Theorem 1.3 it says “Suppose
(1+|\cdot|)^{l}\psi\in L^{1}
satisfies the Tauberian condition
(C2)
and that for every
m\in\mathbb{R}
, the smoothness condition
(C3)
holds with
\Lambda\geq l
”, considering the results such as Theorem 5.1 leading up to Theorem 1.3, the more precise, less misleading formulation seems to be “Suppose
(1+|\cdot|)^{l}\psi\in L^{1}
and
\psi
satisfies […]”.

With the same reasoning as in the proof of Theorem
Theorem
we integrate over tents instead of cylinders for the
\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{\infty,q}
-characterization.

\square

Concluding this subsection, we list two further results for Triebel-Lizorkin and Besov-Lipschitz spaces which we use throughout this work.

The first result we cover concerns some embeddings between different Triebel-Lizorkin and Besov-Lipschitz spaces.
In the following proposition, for two quasi-normed spaces
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
,
A_{1}\subset A_{2}
means that
A_{1}
is continuously embedded in
A_{2}
.
More specifically, we have
\|a\|_{A_{2}}\lesssim\|a\|_{A_{1}}
for all
a\in A_{1}
.
This result will be helpful when combining multiple space characterizations for some of the more complex estimates in Section
5.7
.

Proposition
(Elementary embeddings).

(i)

Let
0<q_{0}\leq q_{1}\leq\infty
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
. Then

B^{\alpha}_{p,q_{0}}\subset B^{\alpha}_{p,q_{1}}\quad\text{and}\quad\dot{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q_{0}}\subset\dot{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q_{1}}\quad\text{if }0<p\leq\infty

as well as

F^{\alpha}_{p,q_{0}}\subset F^{\alpha}_{p,q_{1}}\quad\text{and}\quad\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{p,q_{0}}\subset\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{p,q_{1}}\quad\text{if }0<p<\infty.

(ii)

Let
0<q_{0}\leq\infty
,
0<q_{1}\leq\infty
,
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and
\epsilon>0
. Then

B^{\alpha-\epsilon}_{p,q_{0}}\subset B^{\alpha}_{p,q_{1}}\quad\text{and}\quad\dot{B}^{\alpha-\epsilon}_{p,q_{0}}\subset\dot{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q_{1}}\quad\text{if }0<p\leq\infty

and

F^{\alpha-\epsilon}_{p,q_{0}}\subset F^{\alpha}_{p,q_{1}}\quad\text{and}\quad\dot{F}^{\alpha-\epsilon}_{p,q_{0}}\subset\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{p,q_{1}}\quad\text{if }0<p<\infty.

(iii)

Let
0<q\leq\infty
,
0<p<\infty
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
. Then

B^{\alpha}_{p,\min\{p,q\}}\subset F^{\alpha}_{p,q}\subset B^{\alpha}_{p,\max\{p,q\}}\quad\text{and}\quad\dot{B}^{\alpha}_{p,\min\{p,q\}}\subset\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{p,q}\subset\dot{B}^{\alpha}_{p,\max\{p,q\}}

\square

Proof
.

See
[
Tri83
, Proposition 2.3.2.2]
. The proofs for the homogeneous spaces are analogous.

\square

The second result we cover are the lifting properties of the Triebel-Lizorkin and Besov-Lipschitz spaces.
These lifting properties open up more possible norm equivalencies.
More specifically, based on the identifications of
\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{p,q}
,
F^{\alpha}_{p,q}
,
\dot{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}
or
B^{\alpha}_{p,q}
with spaces we already know, we can easily formulate equivalent, more intuitive norms for a wider range of Triebel-Lizorkin and Besov-Lipschitz spaces.
For example, the identification of Triebel-Lizorkin spaces with
H^{s}_{p}
and therefore
W^{m}_{p}
listed in the table at the start of the subsection is due to the following theorem.

Before stating the Theorem, we introduce the lifting operators, which will allow us to mediate between different Triebel-Lizorkin and Lipschitz-Besov spaces.
Let
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
, then

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554ptJ^{\sigma}f&\coloneqq{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}\left((1+|\xi|^{2})^{\frac{\sigma}{2}}{\mathcal{F}}(f)(\xi)\right)&\qquad\text{for }f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime},\\
\vskip 4.30554pt\dot{J}^{\sigma}f&\coloneqq{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}(|\xi|^{\sigma}{\mathcal{F}}(f)(\xi)&\qquad\text{for }f\in\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}=\mathcal{S}^{\prime}/\mathcal{P},\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

where
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
is the topological dual of

\mathcal{Z}=\{\phi\in\mathcal{S};\quad\partial_{\alpha}{\mathcal{F}}(\phi)(0)\quad\text{for all }\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}\}
.
Defining the Fourier transform on
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
via
\mathcal{Z}
analogous to the way the Fourier transform on
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
is defined via
\mathcal{S}
, the above definition of
\dot{J}^{\sigma}
makes sense.
For more details we refer to
[
Tri83
, 5.1]
.
We do not go into more depth regarding the spaces
\mathcal{Z}
and
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
since under the circumstances we work with the expressions will already be well defined in the standard sense.

Obviously, up to a constant
C_{\sigma}
we have

\dot{J}^{\sigma}f=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}C_{\sigma}I^{\sigma}f&\text{if }-n<\sigma<0,\\
C_{\sigma}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\sigma}{2}}}f&\text{if }\sigma>0,\end{array}\right.

for example for
f\in\mathcal{S}
.

Theorem
(Lifting property of Triebel-Lizorkin and Besov-Lipschitz spaces).

Let
\alpha,\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
and
0<q\leq\infty
.

(i)

If
0<p\leq\infty
, then
J^{\sigma}
maps
B^{\alpha}_{p,q}
isomorphically onto
B^{\alpha-\sigma}_{p,q}
while
\dot{J}^{\sigma}
maps
\dot{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}
isomorphically onto
\dot{B}^{\alpha-\sigma}_{p,q}
.
In particular,
\|J^{\sigma}f\|_{B^{\alpha-\sigma}_{p,q}}
and
\|\dot{J}^{\sigma}f\|_{\dot{B}^{\alpha-\sigma}_{p,q}}
are equivalent quasi-norms on
B^{\alpha}_{p,q}
and
\dot{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}
respectively.
Moreover, for
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

\sum_{|\kappa|\leq m}\|\partial_{\kappa}f\|_{B^{\alpha-m}_{p,q}}\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{|\kappa|=m}\|\partial_{\kappa}f\|_{\dot{B}^{\alpha-m}_{p,q}}

are equivalent norms on
B^{\alpha}_{p,q}
and
\dot{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}
respectively.

(ii)

If
0<p<\infty
, then
J^{\sigma}
maps
F^{\alpha}_{p,q}
isomorphically onto
F^{\alpha-\sigma}_{p,q}
while
\dot{J}^{\sigma}
maps
\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{p,q}
isomorphically onto
\dot{F}^{\alpha-\sigma}_{p,q}
.
In particular,
\|J^{\sigma}f\|_{F^{\alpha-\sigma}_{p,q}}
and
\|\dot{J}^{\sigma}f\|_{\dot{F}^{\alpha-\sigma}_{p,q}}
are equivalent quasi-norms on
F^{\alpha}_{p,q}
and
\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{p,q}
respectively.
Moreover, for
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

\sum_{|\kappa|\leq m}\|\partial_{\kappa}f\|_{F^{\alpha-m}_{p,q}}\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{|\kappa|=m}\|\partial_{\kappa}f\|_{\dot{F}^{\alpha-m}_{p,q}}

are equivalent norms on
F^{\alpha}_{p,q}
and
\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{p,q}
respectively.

\square

Proof
.

See
[
Tri83
, Theorem 2.3.8.]
and
[
Tri83
, Theorem 5.2.3.1.(i)]
.
Note that the equivalency for the standard derivatives is not stated explicitly for the homogeneous spaces, but heavily implied when for the proof of
[
Tri83
, Theorem 5.2.3.1.(iii)]
there is only a reference to the proof of the inhomogeneous version, which makes use of
[
Tri83
, Theorem 2.3.8.]
, which is the inhomogeneous version of the result in question.

\square

5.2  Triebel-Lizorkin and Besov-Lipschitz space characterizations

Since fractional derivatives of the Poisson kernel were the main motivation for
[
BC17
]
, it is no surprise that also fractional derivatives of the generalized Poisson kernel
p^{s}_{1}
satisfy the conditions of Theorem
Theorem
and
Theorem
, yielding the following result.

Theorem
(Triebel-Lizorkin and Besov-Lipschitz space characterizations).

Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
,
\beta>\max\{\alpha,0\}
,
0<p,q\leq\infty
,
s\in(0,2)
and
f\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
.

For
\beta\in(0,1]
,
\alpha<\beta
we have

\|f\|_{\dot{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}}\approx\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}|t^{-\frac{1}{q}-\alpha+\beta}P^{s}_{t}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}f(x)|^{p}\,dx\right)^{\frac{q}{p}}\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{q}},

(5.2.1)

and for
p\neq\infty

\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{p,q}}\approx\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}|t^{-\frac{1}{q}-\alpha+\beta}P^{s}_{t}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}f(x)|^{q}\,dt\right)^{\frac{p}{q}}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

(5.2.2)

Regarding standard derivatives in
x
, we have the following for
\alpha<1
,

\|f\|_{\dot{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}}\gtrsim\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}|t^{-\frac{1}{q}-\alpha+1}\nabla_{x}P^{s}_{t}f(x)|^{p}\,dx\right)^{\frac{q}{p}}\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{q}},

(5.2.3)

and for
p\neq\infty

\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{p,q}}\gtrsim\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}|t^{-\frac{1}{q}-\alpha+1}\nabla_{x}P^{s}_{t}f(x)|^{q}\,dt\right)^{\frac{p}{q}}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

(5.2.4)

Regarding derivatives in
t
, for
\alpha<s
we have

\|f\|_{\dot{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}}\approx\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}|t^{-\frac{1}{q}-\alpha+1}\partial_{t}P^{s}_{t}f(x)|^{p}\,dx\right)^{\frac{q}{p}}\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{q}},

(5.2.5)

and for
p\neq\infty

\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{p,q}}\approx\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}|t^{-\frac{1}{q}-\alpha+1}\partial_{t}P^{s}_{t}f(x)|^{q}\,dt\right)^{\frac{p}{q}}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

(5.2.6)

\square

Proof
.

The proof consists of four steps.
First, we recall the Fourier transform of the kernel
p^{s}_{1}
and collect some related facts that are vital for verifying the conditions
(C1)
-
(C3)
.
Second, we show that the kernels
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}p^{s}_{1}
,
\beta\in(0,1]
, and
\partial_{t}p^{s}_{1}=\partial_{t}k^{s}\big|_{t=1}
satisfy the conditions of
Theorem
and
Theorem
for suitably chosen parameters while the kernels
\partial_{x_{j}}p^{s}_{1}
only satisfy the conditions of
Theorem
, hence we only have an estimate instead of an equivalency in (
5.2.3
) and (
5.2.4
).
Third, we argue why we can always choose
\rho=0
in the context of Theorem
Theorem
.
Fourth, we show that the expressions from Theorem
Theorem
and Theorem
Theorem
for the listed kernels actually match with the expressions of this theorem.

Step 1:
We recall (
3.1.7
),

{\mathcal{F}}(p^{s}_{1})(\xi)=G_{s}(\xi)\coloneqq C\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda^{\frac{s}{2}}e^{-\lambda-\frac{|2\pi\xi|^{2}}{4\lambda}}\frac{d\lambda}{\lambda}.

(5.2.7)

Obviously the function
G_{s}
can be defined for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
since the exponential term dominates the integrand near zero as well as towards infinity and we have
G_{s}>0
on
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\setminus\{0\}
.
This exponential term will further allow us to use Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem when dealing with different derivatives of
G_{s}
.
From
[
Hao16
, Proposition 7.6(c)]
we obtain that

G_{s}(\xi)\leq Ce^{-\pi|\xi|},\quad\text{when }|\xi|\geq\frac{1}{\pi}

(5.2.8)

for some constant
C>0
.
On the other hand we have

G_{s}(\xi)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{llll}\mathcal{O}(|\xi|^{s})&\text{if }s<0,\\
\mathcal{O}(\operatorname{ln}\frac{2}{|\xi|})&\text{if }s=0,\\
\mathcal{O}(1)&\text{if }s>0\end{array}\right.\quad\text{as }\xi\rightarrow 0.

(5.2.9)

While the result is only formulated for
s>-n
, the proofs presented in
[
Hao16
]
are also valid for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
.
With Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem we see that

\partial_{\xi_{j}}G_{s}(\xi)=C\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda^{\frac{s}{2}}e^{-\lambda-\frac{|2\pi\xi|^{2}}{4\lambda}}\left(\frac{2\pi^{2}}{\lambda}\xi_{j}\right)\frac{d\lambda}{\lambda}=2\pi^{2}C\xi_{j}G_{s-2}(\xi).

(5.2.10)

It is easy to show via induction that for any multiindex
\kappa
we have

\partial_{\kappa}G_{s}(\xi)=\sum_{\gamma\leq\kappa}c_{\gamma}\xi^{\gamma}G_{s-(|\kappa|+|\gamma|)}(\xi)

(5.2.11)

for some constants
c_{\gamma}\in\mathbb{R}
depending on
\kappa
where the sum is over all multiindices
\gamma
that are smaller than or equal to
\kappa
in each component.
In particular,
G_{s}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}}\setminus\{0\})
.

Step 2:
We start the second part by checking the integrability of the kernels.
For our first kernel
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}p^{s}_{1}
, due to Lemma
Lemma
and Lemma
Lemma
we have

|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}p^{s}_{1}(x)|\leq\min\left\{\|p^{s}_{1}\|_{W^{1,\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})},\frac{C}{|x|^{n+\beta}}\right\}

for some constant
C>0
.
Confer (
3.1.3
) to confirm the boundedness as well as the necessary decay estimate for
p^{s}_{1}
.
Therefore, we obviously have
(1+|\cdot|)^{l}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}p^{s}_{1}\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
for any
0\leq l<\beta
.
The second kernel
\partial_{t}p^{s}_{1}
is given by

\partial_{t}p^{s}_{1}(x)=\frac{s}{(|x|^{2}+1)^{\frac{n+s}{2}}}-\frac{n+s}{(|x|^{2}+1)^{\frac{n+s}{2}+1}}.

Here, we have
(1+|\cdot|)^{l}\partial_{t}p^{s}_{1}\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
for any
0\leq l<s
.
For the kernels
\partial_{x_{i}}p^{s}_{1}
, again with (
3.1.3
), we obtain

|\partial_{x_{i}}p^{s}_{1}(x)|\leq\max\left\{\|\partial_{x_{i}}p^{s}_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})},\frac{C}{|x|^{n+1}}\right\}

which implies
(1+|\cdot|)^{l}\partial_{x_{i}}p^{s}_{1}\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
for any
0\leq l<1
.
These observations are the main reason for the specific restrictions on
\alpha
since in the context of
Theorem
we need the integrability for
l>\alpha-\frac{n}{p}
.
We therefore fix

l_{(-\Delta)^{\beta/2}}\in(\max\{0,\alpha\},\beta)
for
\alpha<\beta
as well as

l_{\partial_{t}}\in(\max\{0,\alpha\},s)
for
\alpha<s
and

l_{\nabla_{x}}\in(\max\{0,\alpha\},1)
for
\alpha<1
.
In the context of
Theorem
we can always choose
l=0
because we are only interested in functions
f\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
, which can be interpreted as distributions of growth
0
since the convolution of two Schwartz functions is a Schwartz function itself, see
[
Gra14
, Proposition 2.2.7]
.

In order to check
(C1)
,
(C2)
and
(C3)
, we need the Fourier transform of the kernels.
With the definition of the fractional Laplacian and (
5.2.7
) we have

{\mathcal{F}}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}p^{s}_{t})(\xi)=(2\pi|\xi|)^{\beta}{\mathcal{F}}(p^{s}_{1})(\xi)=C|\xi|^{\beta}G_{s}(\xi)

for some constant
C>0
.
With Lemma
Lemma
, Proposition
Proposition
and dominated convergence we obtain

{\mathcal{F}}(\partial_{t}p^{s}_{1})(\xi)=\partial_{t}c_{n,s}\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda^{\frac{s}{2}}e^{-\lambda-\frac{|t\xi|^{2}}{c\lambda}}\frac{d\lambda}{\lambda}=c_{n,s}\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda^{\frac{s}{2}}e^{-\lambda-\frac{|t\xi|^{2}}{c\lambda}}\left(-\frac{2|\xi|^{2}}{c\lambda}t\right)\frac{d\lambda}{\lambda}.

(5.2.12)

The conditions of the lemma are fulfilled due to (
3.1.3
).
Setting
t=1
,

{\mathcal{F}}(\partial_{t}p^{s}_{1})(\xi)=C|\xi|^{2}G_{s-2}(\xi)

for some constant
C>0
.
For the last kernel, with the usual interaction between Fourier transform and derivatives,

{\mathcal{F}}(\partial_{x_{i}}p^{s}_{1})(\xi)=C\xi_{i}G_{s}(\xi)

for some
C\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
.
Since
G_{s}>0
on
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\setminus\{0\}
, it is clear that the kernels
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}p^{s}_{1}
and
\partial_{t}p^{s}_{1}
fulfill condition
(C2)
.

To conclude the second step of the proof, it remains to check the behavior of the kernels’ Fourier transforms and their derivatives for
\xi\rightarrow 0
as well as for
|\xi|\rightarrow\infty
.
With (
5.2.11
) and since
\partial_{\kappa}|\xi|^{\beta}=\sum_{\gamma\leq\kappa}c_{\gamma}|\xi|^{\beta-2|\gamma|}\xi^{\gamma}
for some constants
c_{\gamma}\in\mathbb{R}
, we obtain

\partial_{\kappa}{\mathcal{F}}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}p^{s}_{1})(\xi)=\sum_{\eta\leq\kappa}c_{\eta}\partial_{\eta}|\xi|^{\beta}\partial_{\kappa-\eta}G_{s}(\xi)=\sum_{\eta\leq\kappa}\sum_{\gamma\leq\eta}\sum_{\tilde{\gamma}\leq\kappa-\eta}c_{\gamma,\tilde{\gamma}}\xi^{\gamma+\tilde{\gamma}}|\xi|^{\beta-2|\gamma|}G_{s-|\eta|-|\tilde{\gamma}|}(\xi)

with some constants
c_{\gamma,\tilde{\gamma}}\in\mathbb{R}
.
For the second kernel, via induction,

\partial_{\kappa}{\mathcal{F}}(\partial_{t}p^{s}_{1})(\xi)=\sum_{|\gamma|\leq|\kappa|+2}c_{\gamma}\xi^{\gamma}G_{s-|\kappa|-|\gamma|}(\xi)

with some constants
c_{\gamma}\in\mathbb{R}
with
c_{\gamma}=0
for
|\kappa|+|\gamma|<2
.
For the third kernel, also via induction,

\partial_{\kappa}{\mathcal{F}}(\partial_{x_{i}}p^{s}_{1})=\sum_{\gamma\leq\kappa+e_{i}}c_{\gamma}\xi^{\gamma}G_{s-|\kappa|-|\gamma|+1}(\xi)

with some constants
c_{\gamma}\in\mathbb{C}
and
e_{i}
denoting the corresponding standard base vector.
In particular, the Fourier transforms of all three kernels are in
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}}\setminus\{0\})
.
Therefore, the regularity condition in
(C1)
and
(C3)
is fulfilled.
Furthermore, since
G_{\sigma}
decays exponentially fast as
|\xi|\rightarrow\infty
for any
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
, see (
5.2.8
), all three kernels fulfill
(C3)
for any
\Lambda\geq 0
,
m\in\mathbb{R}
.
It remains to check the behavior of the derivatives as
\xi\rightarrow 0
.
For this we rely on (
5.2.9
) and obtain

\partial_{\kappa}{\mathcal{F}}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}p^{s}_{1})(\xi)=\sum_{\eta\leq\kappa}\sum_{\gamma\leq\eta}\sum_{\tilde{\gamma}\leq\kappa-\eta}\mathcal{O}(|\xi|^{\beta-|\gamma|+|\tilde{\gamma}|}(1+|\xi|^{s-|\eta|-|\tilde{\gamma}|}))=\mathcal{O}(|\xi|^{\beta-|\kappa|})\quad\text{as }\xi\rightarrow 0.

\partial_{\kappa}{\mathcal{F}}(\partial_{t}p^{s}_{1})(\xi)=\sum_{|\gamma|\leq|\kappa|+2}\mathcal{O}(|\xi|^{\gamma}|\xi|^{s-|\kappa|-|\gamma|})=\mathcal{O}(|\xi|^{s-|\kappa|})\quad\text{as }\xi\rightarrow 0.

For the kernels
\partial_{x_{i}}p^{s}_{1}
,
s\neq 1
, we have

\partial_{\kappa}{\mathcal{F}}(\partial_{x_{i}}p^{s}_{1})(\xi)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{llll}\mathcal{O}(|\xi|)&\text{if }\kappa=0,\\
\sum_{\gamma\leq\kappa+e_{i}}\mathcal{O}(|\xi|^{|\gamma|}(1+|\xi|^{s-|\kappa|-|\gamma|+1}))=\mathcal{O}(1+|\xi|^{1+s-|\kappa|})&\text{if }|\kappa|\geq 1\end{array}\right.

as
\xi\rightarrow 0
.
For
s=1
the only combination we have to treat differently is
|\kappa|+|\gamma|=2
because then we have

c_{\gamma}\xi^{\gamma}G_{s-|\kappa|-|\gamma|+1}(\xi)=\mathcal{O}\left(|c_{\gamma}||\xi|^{|\gamma|}\operatorname{ln}\frac{2}{|\xi|}\right)\quad\text{as }\xi\rightarrow 0.

One can easily check that
c_{\gamma}=0
for
|\kappa|=2
and
\gamma=0
.
For
\gamma\neq 0
we have
|\xi|^{|\gamma|}\operatorname{ln}\frac{2}{|\xi|}=\mathcal{O}(1)
as
\xi\rightarrow 0
and therefore end up with the same estimates as for
s\neq 1
.
Therefore, the kernel
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}p^{s}_{1}
fulfills
(C1)
for all
\Lambda\geq 0
and
r=\beta
, the second kernel
\partial_{t}p^{s}_{1}
for all
\Lambda\geq 0
and
r=s
.
The kernels
\partial_{x_{i}}p^{s}_{1}
fulfills
(C1)
for all
\Lambda\geq 0
and
r=1
.

We conclude that all these kernels fulfill the conditions of Theorem
Theorem
for
\alpha<\beta
with
l=l_{(-\Delta)^{\beta/2}}
as well as
\alpha<s
with
l=l_{\partial_{t}}
and
\alpha<1
with
l=l_{\nabla_{x}}
respectively.
Moreover,
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}p^{s}_{1}
and
\partial_{t}p^{s}_{1}
fulfill the conditions of Theorem
Theorem
for functions
f\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
with
\alpha<\beta
and
\alpha<s
respectively.

Step 3:

Let
\rho
be the polynomial from Theorem
Theorem
for
f\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
and the respective kernel.

f\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
is a distribution of growth
0
since again the convolution with a Schwartz function is another Schwartz function.
Therefore, if the distribution
f-\rho
is of growth
l
, then
\rho
has to be a distribution of growth
l
.
In particular,
\rho
then is of degree at most
\lfloor l\rfloor
due to Lemma
Lemma
.

For the first kernel, we have
l_{(-\Delta)^{\beta/2}}<\beta\leq 1
, implying that
\rho
is of degree
0
and therefore a constant,
\rho=c
.
We have

({(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}p^{s}_{1}*\rho)(x)=c\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}p^{s}_{1}(x-y)\,dy=c\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}p^{s}_{1}(y)e^{-2\pi iy\cdot 0}\,dy\equiv c{\mathcal{F}}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}p^{s}_{1})(0).

Because of
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}p^{s}_{1}
fulfilling the integrability condition as discussed at the beginning of the second step, we have
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}p^{s}_{1}\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
and therefore the continuity of the Fourier transform, see
[
Gra14
, Exercise 2.2.6]
.
Since
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}p^{s}_{1}
further fulfills
(C1)
for
r=\beta>0
,

{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}p^{s}_{1}*\rho=c{\mathcal{F}}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}p^{s}_{1})(0)=\lim_{\xi\rightarrow 0}c{\mathcal{F}}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}p^{s}_{1})(\xi)=0.

For the dilations of
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}p^{s}_{1}
we obtain
({(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}p^{s}_{1})_{t}*\rho={(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}p^{s}_{1}*(t^{-n}(\rho)_{t^{-1}})
via substitution and therefore have
({(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}p^{s}_{1})_{t}*\rho=0
with the same argument as above.
Therefore, we can always choose
\rho=0
in the context of Theorem
Theorem
since it does not affect the value of the left hand sides.

Since
l_{\nabla_{x}}<1
, this argument works analogously for the kernels
\partial_{x_{i}}p^{s}_{1}
.

Regarding the remaining kernel
\partial_{t}p^{s}_{1}
, we need to cover the two cases
s\leq 1
and
1<s<2
.
The former is again covered analogously to the kernels above since
l_{\partial_{t}}<s
.
In the latter case,
l_{\partial_{t}}\geq 1
is possible, meaning that
\rho
might be of degree one.
Since we already know that the convolution of the kernel with constants disappears, we only need to cover the monomials of degree
1
.
For
\rho=x_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,n
we have

(\partial_{t}p^{s}_{1}*\rho)(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\partial_{t}p^{s}_{1}(y)(x_{j}-y_{j})\,dy=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\partial_{t}p^{s}_{1}(y)y_{j}\,dy\equiv\frac{1}{2\pi i}\partial_{x_{j}}{\mathcal{F}}(\partial_{t}p^{s}_{1})(0)

where we used that
{\mathcal{F}}(-2\pi ix_{j}g(x))(\xi)=\partial_{\xi_{j}}{\mathcal{F}}(g)(\xi)
for any
g\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
satisfying
|x|g\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
, see the proof of
[
Gra14
, Proposition 2.2.11]
.
With
s>1
we have
(1+|\cdot|)\partial_{t}p^{s}_{1}\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
.
Moreover, because of this, the first order derivatives of the Fourier transform of
\partial_{t}p^{s}_{1}
are continuous.
With
\partial_{t}p^{s}_{1}
fulfilling
(C1)
for
r=s>1
,

\partial_{t}p^{s}_{1}*\rho=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\partial_{x_{j}}{\mathcal{F}}(\partial_{t}p^{s}_{1})(0)=\lim_{\xi\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{2\pi i}\partial_{x_{j}}{\mathcal{F}}(\partial_{t}p^{s}_{1})(\xi)=0.

Therefore, just as for the other kernels we can always choose
\rho=0
without affecting the estimates.

Step 4:

First, we investigate how the kernels behave under dilation.
Recall that
(p^{s}_{1})_{t}=p^{s}_{t}
.
Therefore, we obtain
\partial_{x_{i}}p^{s}_{t}=\partial_{x_{i}}(p^{s}_{1})_{t}=t^{-1}(\partial_{x_{i}}p^{s}_{1})_{t}
as well as

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\partial_{t}p^{s}_{t}(x)=\partial_{t}(p^{s}_{1})_{t}(x)&=&-nt^{-n-1}p^{s}_{1}(t^{-1}x)-t^{-n}t^{-2}x\cdot\nabla_{x}p^{s}_{1}(t^{-1}x)\\
&=&-nt^{-1}(p^{s}_{1})_{t}(x)-t^{-1}(x\cdot\nabla_{x}p^{s}_{1})_{t}(x)\\
&=&^{-1}(\partial_{t}p^{s}_{1})_{t}.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

For
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}p^{s}_{1}
, we use that
{\mathcal{F}}((g)_{t})(\xi)={\mathcal{F}}(g)(t\xi)
for
g\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
, see
[
Gra14
, Proposition 2.2.11]
.
That proposition is formulated for Schwartz functions but the result can be extended to
L^{1}
with the same arguments.
We then have

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt{\mathcal{F}}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}p^{s}_{t})(\xi)&=&(2\pi|\xi|)^{\beta}{\mathcal{F}}((p^{s}_{1})_{t})(\xi)&=&(2\pi|\xi|)^{\beta}{\mathcal{F}}((p^{s}_{1}))(t\xi)\\
&=&^{-\beta}(2\pi|t\xi|)^{\beta}{\mathcal{F}}((p^{s}_{1}))(t\xi)&=&^{-\beta}{\mathcal{F}}(({(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}p^{s}_{1})_{t})(\xi),\\
\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

that is
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}p^{s}_{t}=t^{-\beta}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}p^{s}_{1})_{t}
.

We now conclude this proof by showing the Besov-Lipschitz estimates utilizing the interaction of the differential operators with the
s
-harmonic extension, in particular some integration-by-parts properties of these operators.
For the kernel
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}p^{s}_{1}
, with Lemma
Lemma

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt(({(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}p^{s}_{1})_{t}*f)(x)&=&^{\beta}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}p^{s}_{t}(x-y)f(y)\,dy\\
&=&^{\beta}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}p^{s}_{t}(x-y){(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}f(y)\,dy\\
&=&^{\beta}P^{s}_{t}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}f)(x).\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

Since we have verified all the conditions for Theorems
Theorem
and
Theorem
in Step 2 and dealt with the appearing polynomials in Step 3, we now obtain (
5.2.1
),

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\|f\|_{\dot{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}}&\approx&\bigg(\int_{0}^{\infty}(t^{-\alpha}\|({(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}p^{s}_{1})_{t}*f\|_{L^{p}})^{q}\frac{dt}{t}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{q}}\\
&=&\bigg(\int_{0}^{\infty}(t^{-\frac{1}{q}-\alpha+\beta}\|P^{s}_{t}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}f\|_{L^{p}})^{q}\,dt\bigg)^{\frac{1}{q}}\\
&=&\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}|t^{-\frac{1}{q}-\alpha+\beta}P^{s}_{t}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}f(x)|^{p}\,dx\right)^{\frac{q}{p}}\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

for
\alpha<\beta
,
0<p,q\leq\infty
.
For the next kernel,
\partial_{t}p^{s}_{1}
, via Lemma
Lemma

((\partial_{t}p^{s}_{1})_{t}*f)(x)=t(\partial_{t}p^{s}_{t}*f)(x)=t\partial_{t}P^{s}_{t}f(x).

We then obtain (
5.2.5
) analogously to (
5.2.1
).
Regarding the kernels
\partial_{x_{i}}p^{s}_{1}
, also via Lemma
Lemma
,

((\partial_{x_{i}}p^{s}_{1})_{t}*f)(x)=t(\partial_{x_{i}}p^{s}_{t}*f)(x)=t\partial_{x_{i}}P^{s}_{t}f(x).

Assembling the estimates for these kernels and using that
\|\cdot\|_{L^{\tilde{p}}(\mathbb{R}^{k})}
is a quasi-norm for all
\tilde{p}>0
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we obtain (
5.2.3
),

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\|f\|_{\dot{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}}&\gtrsim&\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigg(\int_{0}^{\infty}(t^{-\frac{1}{q}-\alpha+1}\|\partial_{x_{i}}P^{s}_{t}f\|_{L^{p}})^{q}\,dt\bigg)^{\frac{1}{q}}\\
&\gtrsim&\bigg(\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{n}t^{-\frac{1}{q}-\alpha+1}\|\partial_{x_{i}}P^{s}_{t}f\|_{L^{p}}\bigg)^{q}\,dt\bigg)^{\frac{1}{q}}\\
&\gtrsim&\bigg(\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigg(t^{-\frac{1}{q}-\alpha+1}\bigg\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\partial_{x_{i}}P^{s}_{t}f|\bigg\|_{L^{p}}\bigg)^{q}\,dt\bigg)^{\frac{1}{q}}\\
&\approx&\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigg(\int_{0}^{\infty}(t^{-\frac{1}{q}-\alpha+1}\|\nabla_{x}P^{s}_{t}f\|_{L^{p}})^{q}\,dt\bigg)^{\frac{1}{q}}.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

The Triebel-Lizorkin estimates are obtained analogously for all these kernels.

\square

Remark
.

(i)

While we we have (
5.2.3
) and (
5.2.4
) only as estimates, these are listed as another equivalency in
[
LS20
, Theorem 10.8]
.
We did not get the equivalency because the kernels
\partial_{x_{i}}p^{s}_{1}
individually do not fulfill the Tauberian condition
(C2)
.
While
\nabla_{x}p^{s}_{1}
is not really a kernel in the sense of the results from
[
BC17
]
, it does sort of fulfill a Tauberian condition.
For every
\xi\in S^{n-1}
there exists
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
(depending on
\xi
) such that for every
t>0

|{\mathcal{F}}(\partial_{x_{i}}p^{s}_{1})(t\xi)|=2\pi|\xi_{i}|G_{s}(\xi)>0.

This makes it seem very likely that there indeed is an equivalency.

(ii)

We only needed
f\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
for two steps.
In the context of Theorem
Theorem
this ensured that
f\in\dot{F}^{s}_{p,q}
and
\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q}
while in the context of Theorem
Theorem
we used that
f
as a Schwartz function is a distribution of growth
0
in order to argue that we may choose
\rho=0
.
Naturally, the estimates against the Triebel-Lizorkin and Besov-Lipschitz quasi-norms of Theorem
Theorem
hold for all functions
f
that are distributions of growth
0
.

(iii)

Based on Theorem
Theorem
, we can easily obtain a variety of further space characterizations via Theorem
Theorem
, the lifting property of Triebel-Lizorkin and Besov-Lipschitz spaces.
For example, (
5.2.1
) and (
5.2.2
) hold for arbitrary
\beta>0
.
For
\beta>1
,
\alpha<\beta
and
0<p,q\leq\infty
as well as
f\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
, with (ii) we have

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\|f\|_{\dot{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}}&\approx&\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta-1}{2}}}f\|_{\dot{B}^{\alpha-(\beta-1)}_{p,q}}\\
&\approx&\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}\left|t^{-\frac{1}{q}-(\alpha-(\beta-1))+1}P^{s}_{t}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\left({(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta-1}{2}}}f(x)\right)\right|^{p}\,dx\right)^{\frac{q}{p}}\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\\
&=&\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}|t^{-\frac{1}{q}-\alpha+\beta}P^{s}_{t}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}f(x)|^{p}\,dx\right)^{\frac{q}{p}}\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

because
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta-1}{2}}}f
is a distribution of growth
0
due to Lemma
Lemma
and Lemma
Lemma
.
The respective extension for
\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{p,q}
is obtained analogously.

(iv)

The second example for applying the lifting property are double derivatives.
Based on (
5.2.5
) and (
5.2.5
), for
\alpha<s+1
,
0<p,q\leq\infty
and
f\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
we obtain

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\|f\|_{\dot{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}}&\approx&\sum_{j=1}^{n}\|\partial_{x_{j}}f\|_{\dot{B}^{\alpha-1}_{p,q}}\\
&\approx&\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigg(\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}|t^{-\frac{1}{q}-(\alpha-1)+1}\partial_{t}P^{s}_{t}(\partial_{x_{i}}f)(x)|^{p}\,dx\bigg)^{\frac{q}{p}}\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

Redistributing the additional derivative with Lemma
Lemma
, using the quasi-norm property of
\|\cdot\|_{L^{\tilde{p}}(\mathbb{R}^{k})}
for one direction of the estimate and by estimating the individual summands for the other direction,

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\|f\|_{\dot{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}}&\approx&\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigg(\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}|t^{-\frac{1}{q}-(\alpha-1)+1}\partial_{t}\partial_{x_{i}}P^{s}_{t}f(x)|^{p}\,dx\bigg)^{\frac{q}{p}}\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\\
&\approx&\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}|t^{-\frac{1}{q}-\alpha+2}\partial_{t}\nabla_{x}P^{s}_{t}f(x)|^{p}\,dx\right)^{\frac{q}{p}}\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

Analogously, for
\alpha<s+1
,
0<p<\infty
,
0<q\leq\infty
and
f\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
we obtain

\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{p,q}}\approx\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}|t^{-\frac{1}{q}-\alpha+1}\partial_{t}\nabla_{x}P^{s}_{t}f(x)|^{q}\,dt\right)^{\frac{p}{q}}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

Apparently, we could reiterate this process, adding arbitrary many derivatives.
This allows the characterization of
\dot{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}
and
\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{p,q}
for arbitrary
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
as long as enough derivatives are added.
Of course, there are analogous versions for (
5.2.3
) and (
5.2.4
).

\square

Due to the Tribel-Lizorkin characterization we now have some tools to deal with certain terms involving derivatives of the
s
-harmonic extension such as
\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}P^{s}_{t}f
,
\nabla_{x}\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}P^{s}_{t}f
or
P^{s}_{t}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}f)
.
We are still missing tools to deal with terms where the
s
-harmonic extension occurs unmodified.
Naturally, we wonder whether analogous results can be obtained for the unmodified kernel
p^{s}_{1}
.
Following the scheme of the proof for Theorem
Theorem
, one can easily see that the
p^{s}_{1}
satisfies the conditions of Theorem
Theorem
and Theorem
Theorem
for
\alpha<0
with
l=0
.
But since
\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}p^{s}_{1}\,dx\neq 0
in contrast to the other kernels, we are not able to ignore the polynomial
\rho
appearing in Theorem
Theorem
.

But with a simple trick we are able to circumvent these limitations.
Next to the estimates for the derivatives of the
s
-harmonic extension, we therefore also obtain an analogous estimate in terms of the unmodified
s
-harmonic extension.

Corollary
.

Let
0<p,q\leq\infty
and
s\in(0,2)
.
Denote with
F^{s}(x,t)=P^{s}_{t}f(x)
the
s
-harmonic extension of
f\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
.
Then for
\beta\in(0,n)

\|f\|_{\dot{B}^{-\beta}_{p,q}}\approx\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}|t^{-\frac{1}{q}+\beta}F^{s}(x,t)|^{p}\,dx\right)^{\frac{q}{p}}\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{q}},

(5.2.13)

and for
p\neq\infty

\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{-\beta}_{p,q}}\approx\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}|t^{-\frac{1}{q}+\beta}F^{s}(x,t)|^{q}\,dt\right)^{\frac{p}{q}}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

(5.2.14)

\square

Proof
.

We only show (
5.2.13
), (
5.2.14
) follows analogously.

I^{\beta}f
is a distribution of growth
0
since
I^{\beta}f\in L^{s}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
for some
1<s<\infty
due to Theorem
Theorem
.
Therefore, with (
5.2.1
), Remark
Remark
(ii),(iii) and the lifting property for homogeneous Besov-Lipschitz spaces we have

\|f\|_{\dot{B}^{-\beta}_{p,q}}\approx\|I^{\beta}f\|_{\dot{B}^{0}_{p,q}}\approx\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}|t^{-\frac{1}{q}-0+\beta}P^{s}_{t}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}I^{\beta}f)(x)|^{p}\,dx\right)^{\frac{q}{p}}\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}.

The result follows since
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}
and
I^{\beta}
are inverse.

\square

5.3  Lorentz spaces and an interpolation theorem

Besides the Triebel-Lizorkin and Besov-Lipschitz spaces, there is a second type of spaces which we need to introduce in more detail,
the Lorentz spaces
{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
,
0<p<\infty,0<q\leq\infty
.
They are a generalization of the Lebesgue spaces, offering a finer scale, which is not covered by the Triebel-Lizorkin and Besov-Lipschitz spaces.
The Lebesgue spaces are included in the Lorentz scala with
{L^{(p,p)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}=L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
.
Despite not defining Lorentz spaces for
p=\infty
, we therefore still use the convention
{L^{(\infty,\infty)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}=L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
.
These Lorentz-spaces are defined as

{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\coloneqq\{f\colon{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\,;\,f\text{ is measurable and }\|f\|_{{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}}<\infty\}.

For measurable functions
f\colon{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
the Lorentz space (quasi-)norm
\|\cdot\|_{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
is

\|f\|_{{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}}\coloneqq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(t^{\frac{1}{p}}f^{*}(t)\right)^{q}\frac{dt}{t}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}&\text{if }p,q\in(0,\infty),\\
\sup_{t>0}t^{\frac{1}{p}}f^{*}(t)&\text{if }q=\infty\end{array}\right.

where
f^{*}
is the decreasing rearrangement of
f
given by

f^{\ast}(t)\coloneqq\inf\{s>0\,;\,\mathcal{L}^{n}\{|f|>s\})\leq t\}

with
\mathcal{L}^{n}
denoting the
n
-dimensional Lebesgue-measure.

One key property to us will be that similar to Lebesgue spaces there is a Hölder inequality for the Lorentz spaces.

Theorem
(Hölder’s inequality in Lorentz spaces,
[
O’N63
]
).

Let
0<p_{1},p_{2},p<\infty
and
0<q_{1},q_{2},q\leq\infty
with

\frac{1}{p}=\frac{1}{p_{1}}+\frac{1}{p_{2}}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{1}{q}=\frac{1}{q_{1}}+\frac{1}{q_{2}}.

Then for measurable functions
f,g\colon{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
we have

\|fg\|_{{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}}\lesssim\|f\|_{{L^{(p_{1},q_{1})}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}}\|g\|_{{L^{(p_{2},q_{2})}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}}.

(5.3.1)

In particular, for
1=\frac{1}{p_{1}}+\frac{1}{p_{2}}
and
1=\frac{1}{q_{1}}+\frac{1}{q_{2}}
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|fg|\lesssim\|f\|_{{L^{(p_{1},q_{1})}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}}\|g\|_{{L^{(p_{2},q_{2})}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}}.

(5.3.2)

\square

The other key property to us is the weak type interpolation theorem for Lorentz spaces as given in
[
Hun66
, Section 3]
.
We use this theorem to obtain Lorentz estimates from
L^{p}
-estimates, adding another scale to our estimates without much effort.

Theorem
(Weak type interpolation theorem for Lorentz spaces,
[
Hun66
]
).

Let
T\colon{L^{(p_{i},q_{i})}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\rightarrow{L^{(\tilde{p}_{i},\tilde{q}_{i})}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
with
0<p_{i},\tilde{p}_{i}<\infty
,
0<q_{i},\tilde{q}_{i}\leq\infty
for
i=0,1
and
p_{0}<p_{1}
,
\tilde{p}_{0}\neq\tilde{p}_{1}
.
Assume that
T
is quasi-linear, that is that
T(f+g)
is defined whenever
Tf
and
Tg
are defined and

|T(f+g)|\lesssim|Tf|+|Tg|

almost everywhere.
Further assume that

\|Tf\|_{{L^{(\tilde{p}_{i},\tilde{q}_{i})}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}}\lesssim\|f\|_{{L^{(p_{i},q_{i})}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}},\quad i=0,1.

Then

\|Tf\|_{{L^{(\tilde{p}_{\theta},s)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}}\lesssim\|f\|_{{L^{(p_{\theta},q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}},

where
q\leq s
and, for
0<\theta<1
,

\frac{1}{p_{\theta}}=\frac{1-\theta}{p_{0}}+\frac{\theta}{p_{1}}
as well as

\frac{1}{\tilde{p}_{\theta}}=\frac{1-\theta}{\tilde{p}_{0}}+\frac{\theta}{\tilde{p}_{1}}
.

The theorem also holds for
(p_{1},q_{1})=(\infty,\infty)
or
(\tilde{p}_{i},\tilde{q}_{i})=(\infty,\infty)
.

\square

Remark
.

Note that the image of
(0,1)
under
\theta\mapsto p_{\theta}
is
(p_{0},p_{1})
.
Therefore, the interpolation theorem captures all
p
between
p_{0}
and
p_{1}
as well as all
\tilde{p}
between
\tilde{p}_{0}
and
\tilde{p_{1}}
but only in the specific relation dictated by the respective
\theta
.

\square

5.4  Building blocks:
BMO
, fractional Sobolev & Hölder space estimates

At the start of Subsection
5.1
, we listed identifications of Triebel-Lizorkin and Besov-Lipschitz spaces with a variety of well known and important function spaces.
Thanks to these, based on Theorem
Theorem
we now easily obtain a multitude of more specific space characterizations via the generalized Poisson operator.

The first couple of more specific space characterizations are related to the two common variants of fractional Sobolev spaces.
On the one hand, there are the Slobodeckij spaces
W^{s}_{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
.
For
1\leq p<\infty
and
0<s\notin\mathbb{N}
with
s=[s]+\{s\}
, where
[s]
is the integer part of
s
, they are defined as

W^{s}_{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})\coloneqq\left\{f\in W^{[s]}_{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})\,;\,\|f\|_{W^{s}_{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\coloneqq\|f\|_{W^{[s]}_{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}+\sum_{|\kappa|=[s]}[\partial_{\kappa}f]_{W^{\{s\},p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}<\infty\right\},

(5.4.1)

where the seminorm
[\cdot]_{W^{\nu,p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
,
\nu\in(0,1)
, is given by

[f]_{W^{\nu,p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\coloneqq\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{|f(x)-f(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{n+\nu p}}\,dx\,dy\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

Recalling the list from Subsection
5.1
, the Slobodeckij spaces can be identified with
F^{s}_{p,p}
.
On the other hand, there are the Bessel-potential spaces
H^{s}_{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
defined as

H^{s}_{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})\coloneqq\left\{f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})\,;\,\|f\|_{H^{s}_{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\coloneqq\left\|{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}\left((1+|\xi|^{2})^{\frac{s}{2}}{\mathcal{F}}(f)\right)\right\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}<\infty\right\}

for
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
1<p<\infty
. These spaces can be identified with
F^{s}_{p,2}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
.

Note that for
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
p\neq 2
we have
W^{m,p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})=H^{m}_{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})=F^{m}_{p,2}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})\neq F^{m}_{p,p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
.
Therefore, whenever we obtain results for the Slobodeckij spaces via the Triebel-Lizorkin identification, we need to somewhat counter-intuitively exclude the integer case, i.e. the classical Sobolev spaces.

Further note that both of these spaces are inhomogeneous while we only have the space characterization for homogeneous Triebel-Lizorkin spaces.
The corresponding
\dot{F}^{s}_{p,p}
-norm or
\dot{F}^{s}_{p,2}
-norm, which we obtain via the Poisson kernels in the following two propositions, might intuitively be described as the highest order seminorm contributing to the norm of the corresponding inhomogeneous space.

Proposition
(Slobodeckij space characterizations).

Let
1<p<\infty
and
s\in(0,2)
.
Denote with
F^{s}(x,t)=P^{s}_{t}f(x)
the
s
-harmonic extension of
f\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
.

Regarding the
x
-derivatives, for
\nu\in(0,1)
we have

\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{0}^{\infty}|t^{1-\frac{1}{p}-\nu}\nabla_{x}F^{s}(x,t)|^{p}\,dt\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\lesssim[f]_{W^{\nu,p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

(5.4.2)

Regarding the
t
-derivative, for
\nu\in(0,s)
,
\nu\neq 1
we have

\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{0}^{\infty}|t^{1-\frac{1}{p}-\nu}\partial_{t}F^{s}(x,t)|^{p}\,dt\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\approx[f]_{W^{\nu,p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

(5.4.3)

Regarding double derivatives, for
\nu\in(0,2)
,
\nu\neq 1
,

\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{0}^{\infty}|t^{2-\frac{1}{p}-\nu}\nabla_{x}^{2}F^{s}(x,t)|^{p}\,dt\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\lesssim[f]_{W^{\nu,p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})},

(5.4.4)

and for
\nu\in(0,1+s)
,
\nu\notin\mathbb{N}
,

\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{0}^{\infty}|t^{2-\frac{1}{p}-\nu}\partial_{t}\nabla_{x}F^{s}(x,t)|^{p}\,dt\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\lesssim[f]_{W^{\nu,p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

(5.4.5)

\square

Proof
(Estimates (
5.4.2
)-(
5.4.5
)).

According to
[
Tri92
, Theorem 2.3.3(ii)]
we have

\|f\|_{F^{\nu}_{p,p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\approx\|f\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}+\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{\nu}_{p,p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}

Therefore, since
F^{\nu}_{p,p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})=W^{\nu,p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
for
\nu\notin\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

\|f\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}+\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{\nu}_{p,p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\approx\|f\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}+[f]_{W^{\nu,p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

(5.4.6)

All of the occurring (semi-)norms are homogeneous, but for different orders.
For all
\lambda>0
we have

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\|f(\lambda\,\cdot)\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}&=&\lambda^{-\frac{n}{p}}\|f\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})},\\
\vskip 4.30554pt{[}f(\lambda\,\cdot){]}_{W^{\nu,p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}&=&\lambda^{\nu-\frac{n}{p}}[f]_{W^{\nu,p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})},\\
\vskip 4.30554pt\|f(\lambda\,\cdot)\|_{\dot{F}^{\nu}_{p,p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}&\approx&\lambda^{\nu-\frac{n}{p}}\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{\nu}_{p,p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})},\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

where the first equations are obtained easily via substitution and the last estimate is due to
[
Tri83
, Remark 5.1.3.4]
with the constants independent of
\lambda
.
These observations in combination with (
5.4.6
) yield

\lambda^{-\frac{n}{p}}\|f\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}+\lambda^{\nu-\frac{n}{p}}\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{\nu}_{p,p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\leq C\left(\lambda^{-\frac{n}{p}}\|f\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}+\lambda^{\nu-\frac{n}{p}}[f]_{W^{\nu,p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\right)

for some constant
C>0
independent of
\lambda
and
f
.
Thus,

\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{\nu}_{p,p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\leq\lambda^{-\nu}(C-1)\|f\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}+C[f]_{W^{\nu,p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

Since
\nu>0
, for
\lambda\rightarrow\infty
we obtain
\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{\nu}_{p,p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\lesssim[f]_{W^{\nu,p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
.
Analogously, we get the estimate
[f]_{W^{\nu,p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\lesssim\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{\nu}_{p,p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
and with that
[f]_{W^{\nu,p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\approx\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{\nu}_{p,p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
.

Therefore, we obtain (
5.4.2
) as a special case of (
5.2.4
), (
5.4.3
) from (
5.2.6
) and (
5.4.4
) as well as (
5.4.5
) with Remark
Remark
(iv) applied to (
5.2.4
) and (
5.2.6
) respectively.

\square

Proposition
(Bessel-potential space characterizations).

Let
1<p<\infty
,
0<q\leq\infty
and
s\in(0,2)
.
Denote with
F^{s}(x,t)=P^{s}_{t}f(x)
the
s
-harmonic extension of
f\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
.

Regarding the
x
-derivatives, for
\nu\in[0,1)
we have

\left\|x\mapsto\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}|t^{\frac{1}{2}-\nu}\nabla_{x}F^{s}(x,t)|^{2}\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\|_{{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}}\lesssim\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\nu}{2}}}f\|_{{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}},

(5.4.7)

Regarding the
t
-derivative, for
\nu\in(0,s)
we have

\left\|x\mapsto\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}|t^{\frac{1}{2}-\nu}\partial_{t}F^{s}(x,t)|^{2}\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\|_{{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}}\lesssim\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\nu}{2}}}f\|_{{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}}.

(5.4.8)

Regarding double derivatives, for
\nu\in[0,2)
we have

\left\|x\mapsto\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}|t^{\frac{3}{2}-\nu}\nabla_{x}^{2}F^{s}(x,t)|^{2}\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\|_{{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}}\lesssim\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\nu}{2}}}f\|_{{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}},

(5.4.9)

and for
\nu\in[0,1+s)
,

\left\|x\mapsto\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}|t^{\frac{3}{2}-\nu}\partial_{t}\nabla_{x}F^{s}(x,t)|^{2}\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\|_{{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}}\lesssim\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\nu}{2}}}f\|_{{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}}.

(5.4.10)

All these estimates also hold for
-n<\nu<0
with
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\nu}{2}}}f
replaced by the Riesz potential
I^{-\nu}f
.
In fact, for
0<\nu<n
we also have

\left\|x\mapsto\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}|t^{-\frac{1}{2}+\nu}F^{s}(x,t)|^{2}dt\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\|_{{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}}\lesssim\|I^{\nu}f\|_{{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}}.

(5.4.11)

\square

Proof
(Estimates (
5.4.7
)-(
5.4.11
)).

We first show the estimates for
p=q
, i.e. the
L^{p}
-norm.
Then, we obtain (
5.4.7
)-(
5.4.11
) via interpolation.

Step 1:

Due to Theorem
Theorem
and since
\dot{F}^{0}_{p,2}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})=L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
for
\nu\geq 0
, we have

\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{\nu}_{p,2}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\approx\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\nu}{2}}}f\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}

while for
-n<\nu<0

\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{\nu}_{p,2}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\approx\|I^{-\nu}f\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

Therefore, we obtain (
5.4.7
) and (
5.4.8
) for
q=p
from (
5.2.4
) and (
5.2.6
) respectively, (
5.4.11
) from Corollary
Corollary
. The
L^{p}
-versions of the other two estimates can be obtained with Remark
Remark
(iv).

Step 2:

Based on these
L^{p}
-estimates we now obtain the
L^{p,q}
-estiamtes via the weak type interpolation theorem for Lorentz spaces, Theorem
Theorem
, see
[
Hun66
, Section 3]
.
The details are only provided for (
5.4.7
), the same arguments work for the other equations.
We want to apply the interpolation theorem to the operator

g\rightarrow Tg\coloneqq\left(x\mapsto\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}|t^{\frac{1}{2}-\nu}\nabla_{x}P^{s}_{t}\dot{J}^{-\nu}g(x)|^{2}\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)

that is defined for
g\in\dot{J}^{\nu}({\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})})
.
Here,
\dot{J}^{\nu}
is the lifting operator from Theorem
Theorem
that up to constants equals the fractional Laplacians for
\nu\geq 0
and the Riesz potentials for
-n<\nu<0
.
For any
1<p_{0}<p_{1}<\infty
we have

\|Tg\|_{{L^{(p_{i},p_{i})}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}}=\|Tg\|_{L^{p_{i}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\lesssim\|\dot{J}^{\nu}\dot{J}^{-\nu}g\|_{L{p_{i}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}=\|g\|_{{L^{(p_{i},p_{i})}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}}

due to the
L^{p}
-estimates from above.
Furthermore, we have

|T(g_{1}+g_{2})|\leq|Tg_{1}|+|Tg_{2}|

due to the Minkowski inequality.
Usually, in order to apply Theorem
Theorem
the operator
T
has to be defined on all of
L^{p_{0}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})+L^{p_{1}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
.
We are only interested in interpolation estimates for functions
g\in\dot{J}^{\nu}({\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})})\subset L^{p_{0}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})\cap L^{p_{1}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
though.
Investigating the proof of Theorem
Theorem
in
[
Hun66
, Section 3]
, for this weaker result it suffices to have
T
defined on
L^{p_{0}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})\cup L^{p_{1}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
.

Therefore, let us define
T
on
L^{p_{i}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
for
i=0,1
.
This would be very easy, if all elements in
\dot{J}^{-\nu}(L^{p_{i}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}}))
were distributions of growth
0
since then
T
would already be defined on
L^{p_{i}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
without any further modifications due to Remark
Remark
.
Sadly, this is not always the case.

According to
[
Tri83
, Theorem 5.1.5]
,
\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
and therefore
{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
are dense in
\dot{F}^{-\nu}_{p_{i},2}
.
Thus, due to Theorem
Theorem

\dot{J}^{\nu}({\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})})
is dense in
L^{p_{i}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})=\dot{F}^{0}_{p_{i},2}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
.
Let
g\in L^{p_{i}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
.
Then there exists an approximating sequence
(g_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\dot{J}^{\nu}({\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})})
with
g_{n}\overset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}g
in
L^{p_{i}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
.
We have

Tg_{n}-Tg_{m}\leq Tg_{m}+T(g_{n}-g_{m})-Tg_{m}=T(g_{n}-g_{m})=T(g_{m}-g_{n})

and therefore via symmetry
|Tg_{n}-Tg_{m}|\leq T(g_{n}-g_{m})
.
Then
Tg_{n}
is a Cauchy sequence in
{L^{(p_{i},p_{i})}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
since

\|Tg_{n}-Tg_{m}\|_{L^{p_{i}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\leq\|T(g_{n}-g_{m})\|_{L^{p_{i}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\lesssim\|g_{n}-g_{m}\|_{L^{p_{i}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

Because the Lebesgue spaces are complete we can then set
Tg\coloneqq\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}Tg_{n}
.
This definition is independent of the chosen sequence as we obtain
\|T\tilde{g}_{n}-Tg_{n}\|_{L^{p_{i}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\lesssim\|\tilde{g}_{n}-g_{n}\|_{L^{p_{i}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
with the same argument as above for any other sequence
(\tilde{g}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\dot{J}^{\nu}({\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})})
.
Moreover, this choice is also well-defined when considering
g\in L^{p_{0}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})\cap L^{p_{1}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
;

\dot{J}^{-\nu}g
can be approximated with the same sequence of compactly supported smooth functions both in
\dot{F}^{-\nu}_{p_{0},2}
and
\dot{F}^{-\nu}_{p_{1},2}
, see the proof of
[
Tri83
, Theorem 2.3.3]
.
Applying
\dot{J}^{\nu}
to this sequence we obtain a sequence in
\dot{J}^{\nu}({\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})})
approximating
g
both in
L^{p_{0}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
as well as
L^{p_{1}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
.
Therefore, both approximations in
L^{p_{0}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
as well as
L^{p_{1}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
can be compared to this specific sequence, which yields the well-definedness with the above arguments.

It remains to show that the inequaltities for the quasi-linearity and boundedness are fulfilled on all of
L^{p_{0}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})\cup L^{p_{1}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
.
Let
g\in L^{p_{i_{1}}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
and
\tilde{g}\in L^{p_{i_{2}}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
such that
g+\tilde{g}\in L^{p_{i}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
for some choice of
i,i_{1},i_{2}\in\{0,1\}
.
When choosing
(g_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\tilde{g}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
as the specific approximation sequences for
g
and
g_{n}
as described above, then by linearity
(g_{n}+\tilde{g}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\dot{J}^{\nu}({\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})})
is that specific sequence for
g_{n}+\tilde{g}_{n}
.
Because of the
L^{p_{i_{0}}}
,
L^{p_{i_{1}}}
and
L^{p_{i}}
convergence, there exists a shared subsequence such that
Tg_{n_{k}}
,
T\tilde{g}_{n_{k}}
and
T(g_{n_{k}}+\tilde{g}_{n_{k}})
converge against
Tg
,
T\tilde{g}
and
T(g+\tilde{g})
respectively almost everywhere.
Hence,

|T(g+\tilde{g})|=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}|T(g_{n_{k}}+\tilde{g}_{n_{k}})|\leq\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left(|Tg_{n_{k}}|+|T\tilde{g}_{n_{k}}|\right)=|Tg|+|T\tilde{g}|

almost everywhere.
Regarding the boundedness of
T
, we have

\|Tg\|_{L^{p_{i_{1}}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\|Tg_{n}\|_{L^{p_{i_{1}}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\lesssim\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\|g_{n}\|_{L^{p_{i_{1}}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}=\|g\|_{L^{p_{i_{1}}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

In total, the slightly modified requirements for Theorem
Theorem
are met and for any
p\in(p_{0},p_{1})
,
0<q\leq\infty
and
f\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
we obtain

\left\|x\mapsto\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}|t^{\frac{1}{2}-\nu}\nabla_{x}F^{s}(x,t)|^{2}\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\|_{{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}}=\|T(J^{\nu}f)\|_{{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}}\lesssim\|J^{\nu}f\|_{{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}}.

Since
1<p_{0},p_{1}<\infty
can be chosen arbitrarily, this is (
5.4.7
).

\square

Remark
.

Since the estimates in Proposition
Proposition
are derived from Triebel-Lizorkin space estimates, there is a variety of additional weaker estimates which can analogously be obtained from some Triebel-Lizorkin space properties.
Most important to us will be the following example.
Let
k\geq 2
.
Due to Proposition
Proposition
, under the assumptions for (
5.4.7
) we have

\left\|x\rightarrow\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}|t^{1-\frac{1}{k}-\nu}\nabla_{x}P^{s}_{t}f(x)|^{k}\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{k}}\right\|_{L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\lesssim\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{\nu}_{p,k}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\lesssim\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{\nu}_{p,2}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

Following the proof of Proposition
Proposition
, we then obtain

\left\|x\rightarrow\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}|t^{1-\frac{1}{k}-\nu}\nabla_{x}P^{s}_{t}f(x)|^{k}\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{k}}\right\|_{{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}}\lesssim\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\nu}{2}}}f\|_{{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}}.

Of course, analogous versions hold for the other estimates of the proposition.

\square

Next, we cover the space of all functions with bounded mean oscillation, known as the
BMO
-space.
The
BMO
-seminorm is given by

[f]_{BMO}=\sup_{B\subset{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}\frac{1}{|B|}\int_{B}|f(y)-(f)_{B}|\,dy,

(5.4.12)

where the supremum is over all balls
B\subset{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
and
(f)_{B}\coloneqq|B|^{-1}\int_{B}f
.
We can then define the space
BMO=\{f\in L^{1}_{\text{loc}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})\,;\,[f]_{BMO}<\infty\}
.
The following result is a special case of
[
Ste93
, Chapter IV, §4.3, p.159, Theorem 3]
, a well known result covering the relation between
BMO
and certain sets of so called Carleson measures on
{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}
.
Stein already remarked that the result applies to certain Poisson-like kernels, see
[
Ste93
, Chapter IV, §4.4.3, p.165]
.
For us, this special case is another easy consequence from the Triebel-Lizorkin space characterizations.

Proposition
(BMO characterization).

Let
s\in(0,2)
.
Denote with
F^{s}(x,t)=P^{s}_{t}f(x)
the
s
-harmonic extension of
f\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
. Then

[f]_{BMO({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\gtrsim\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}},r>0}\left(\frac{1}{|B(x,r)|}\int_{T(B(x,r))}t|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}F^{s}(y,t)|^{2}\,dy\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(5.4.13)

Additionally, for
\beta>0

[f]_{BMO({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\approx\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}},r>0}\left(\frac{1}{|B(x,r)|}\int_{T(B(x,r))}t^{2\beta-1}|P^{s}_{t}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}f)(y)|^{2}\,dy\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(5.4.14)

and
