[f]_{BMO({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\gtrsim\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}},r>0}\left(\frac{1}{|B(x,r)|}\int_{T(B(x,r))}t^{2\beta+1}|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}P^{s}_{t}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}f)(y)|^{2}\,dy\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(5.4.15)

Here,
T(B(x,r))=\{(y,t)\in{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}\,:\,|y-x|<r-t\}
is the “tent” over
B(x,r)
.

\square

Proof
.

Recalling the list from the beginning of subsection
5.1
, we have
BMO=\dot{F}^{0}_{\infty,2}
.
In the proof of Theorem
Theorem
we have already seen that the kernels
\partial_{t}p^{s}_{1}
,
\partial_{x_{i}}p^{s}_{1}
and
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}p^{s}_{1}
,
\beta\in(0,1]
satisfy the conditions of Theorem
Theorem
with
\alpha=0
and allow the choice of
\rho=0
for the appearing polynomial.
Thus, we have (
5.4.13
) and (
5.4.14
) for
\beta\in(0,1]
as an immediate consequence of Theorem
Theorem
.

Sadly, Theorem
Theorem
, the lifting property, is only applicable to Triebel-Lizorkin spaces with
p<\infty
.
Therefore, we can not argue as in Remark
Remark
to obtain estimates for higher order kernels.
Following the same line of argument as for the proof Theorem
Theorem
though, we get that the kernels
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}p^{s}_{1}
,
\beta>1
, as well as
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}\partial_{t}p^{s}_{1}
and
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}\partial_{x_{i}}p^{s}_{1}
also satisfy the conditions of Theorem
Theorem
for
\alpha=0
,
p=\infty
and
q=2
.
This results in the rest of the estimates.

\square

By plugging suitable functions in these
BMO
-estimates, we obtain some additional estimates.

Corollary
.

Let
s\in(0,2)
.
Denote with
F^{s}(x,t)=P^{s}_{t}f(x)
the
s
-harmonic extension of
f\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
.
Then, for
0<\nu<1
,

[{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\nu}{2}}}f]_{BMO({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\gtrsim\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}},r>0}\left(\frac{1}{|B(x,r)|}\int_{T(B(x,r))}t^{1-2\nu}|\nabla_{y}F^{s}(y,t)|^{2}\,dy\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

(5.4.16)

and for
0<\nu<s
,

[{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\nu}{2}}}f]_{BMO({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\approx\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}},r>0}\left(\frac{1}{|B(x,r)|}\int_{T(B(x,r))}t^{1-2\nu}|\partial_{t}F^{s}(y,t)|^{2}\,dy\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(5.4.17)

Additionally, for
0<\nu<\min\{1+s,2\}
,

[{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\nu}{2}}}f]_{BMO({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\gtrsim\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}},r>0}\left(\frac{1}{|B(x,r)|}\int_{T(B(x,r))}t^{3-2\nu}|\nabla_{x}\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}F^{s}(y,t)|^{2}\,dy\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(5.4.18)

\square

Proof
.

We obtain (
5.4.16
) by plugging
\mathcal{R}_{i}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\nu}{2}}}f
into (
5.4.14
) with
\beta=1-\nu
since

P^{s}_{t}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{1-\nu}{2}}}\mathcal{R}_{i}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\nu}{2}}}f)(x)=P^{s}_{t}(\mathcal{R}_{i}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}}f)(x)=P^{s}_{t}(\partial_{x_{i}}f)(x)=\partial_{x_{i}}F^{s}(x,t).

This can be easily seen on the side of the Fourier transforms.
Thus, we have (
5.4.16
) since the Riesz transform maps
BMO
continuously onto itself, see
[
Ste93
, Chapter IV, §6.3a(b), p.179]
.

Regarding (
5.4.17
), we have

t^{1-\nu}\partial_{t}P^{s}_{t}f(x)=Ct^{s-\nu}\left({(-\Delta)^{\frac{s-\nu}{2}}}p^{2-s}_{t}\ast{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\nu}{2}}}f\right)(x)

For a detailed proof of this fact, see the proof of Estimate (
5.5.1
), for the interaction of dilations and fractional Laplacians see Step 4 in the proof of Theorem
Theorem
.
Therefore, plugging
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\nu}{2}}}f
into (
5.4.14
) with
\beta=s-\nu
and the
(2-s)
-harmonic extension, for the integrand we have

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LCL}\vskip 4.30554ptt^{2(s-\nu)-1}|P^{2-s}_{t}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{s-\nu}{2}}}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\nu}{2}}}f)(y)|^{2}=Ct^{2s-2\nu-1}|t^{1-s}\partial_{t}P^{s}_{t}f(y)|^{2}.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

Thus, we obtain (
5.4.17
).

In the same manner, we obtain (
5.4.18
) from (
5.4.14
).
By plugging in
\mathcal{R}_{i}{\mathcal{R}_{j}}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\nu}{2}}}f
for
\beta=2-\nu
, we obtain the estimate for
\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{x_{j}}F^{s}
.
By plugging in
\mathcal{R}_{i}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\nu}{2}}}f
for
\beta=1+s-\nu
and the
(2-s)
-harmonic extension, we obtain the estimate for
\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{t}F^{s}
.

\square

Remark
.

All estimates in Proposition
Proposition
, Proposition
Proposition
, and Proposition
Proposition
are actually listed as (norm-)equivalencies in
[
LS20
]
.
We only obtain the one sided estimates for two reasons.
First, for the kernels involving
x
-derivatives, this is due to the same reason we explained in Remark
Remark
(i).
Second, for the Lorentz scale estimates, results in the style of the classical interpolation theorems such as listed in
[
Hun66
, Section 3]
only yield one direction of the estimate.
Since proving the blackbox estimates in Subsection
5.7
and therefore also the main part only requires this one direction of the space characterizations, we do not further investigate the reverse estimates.

\square

We finish this subsection by covering the Hölder spaces.
For
\nu>0
, the corresponding Hölder-seminorm is

[f]_{\mathcal{C}^{\nu}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\coloneqq\left\{\begin{array}[]{llll}\sup_{x\neq y\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}\frac{|\nabla^{\lfloor\nu\rfloor}f(x)-\nabla^{\lfloor\nu\rfloor}f(y)|}{|x-y|^{\nu-\lfloor\nu\rfloor}}&\text{if }\nu\notin\mathbb{N},\\
\|\nabla^{\nu}f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}&\text{if }\nu\in\mathbb{N},\par\end{array}\right.

where
\lfloor\cdot\rfloor
is the floor function.
We may denote
[f]_{\operatorname{Lip}}=[f]_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}
.
The Hölder spaces are defined as

\mathcal{C}^{\nu}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})=\{f\in C^{\lfloor\nu\rfloor}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})\,;\,\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\nu}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}=\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\lfloor\nu\rfloor}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}+[f]_{\mathcal{C}^{\nu}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}<\infty\}.

Proposition
(Hölder space characterizations).

Let
s\in(0,2)
. Denote with
F^{s}(x,t)=P^{s}_{t}f(x)
the
s
-harmonic extension of
f\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
.
Then for
\nu\in(0,s)\setminus\mathbb{N}
we have

\sup_{(x,t)\in{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}t^{1-\nu}|\partial_{t}F^{s}(x,t)|\approx[f]_{\mathcal{C}^{\nu}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

(5.4.19)

Additionally, for any
\nu\in(0,1]
,

\sup_{(x,t)\in{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}t^{1-\nu}|\nabla_{x}F^{s}(x,t)|\lesssim[f]_{\mathcal{C}^{\nu}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

(5.4.20)

Regarding double derivatives, for
\nu\in(0,\min\{1+s,2\})\setminus\mathbb{N}
we have

\sup_{(x,t)\in{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}t^{2-\nu}|\nabla_{x}\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}F^{s}(x,t)|\lesssim[f]_{\mathcal{C}^{\nu}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

(5.4.21)

\square

Proof
.

According to the Besov-Lipschitz space identifications listed at the start of subsection
5.1
, we have
\mathcal{C}^{\nu}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})=B^{\nu}_{\infty,\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
for
\nu\notin\mathbb{N}
.
According to
[
Tri92
, 2.3.3(i)]
we also have

\|f\|_{B^{\nu}_{\infty,\infty}}\approx\|f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}+\|f\|_{\dot{B}^{\nu}_{\infty,\infty}}.

Therefore,

\|f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}+\|f\|_{\dot{B}^{\nu}_{\infty,\infty}}\approx\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}+[f]_{\mathcal{C}^{\nu}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.

(5.4.22)

For
\lambda>0
directly via the definition of the (semi-)norms we obtain

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\|f(\lambda\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}&=&\|f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\\
\vskip 4.30554pt\|f(\lambda\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}&=&\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}\\
\vskip 4.30554pt{[}f(\lambda\cdot){]}_{\mathcal{C}^{\nu}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}&=&\lambda^{\nu}[f]_{\mathcal{C}^{\nu}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

For the homogeneous Besov-Lipschitz norm, due to
[
Tri83
, Remark 5.1.3.4]
, we have

\|f(\lambda\cdot)\|_{\dot{B}^{\nu}_{\infty,\infty}}\approx\lambda^{\nu}\|f\|_{\dot{B}^{\nu}_{\infty,\infty}}.

With the same argument as in the proof of the estimates (
5.4.2
)-(
5.4.3
) we get the identification
\|f\|_{\dot{B}^{\nu}_{\infty,\infty}}\approx[f]_{\mathcal{C}^{\nu}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
.
Thus, for
\nu\notin\mathbb{N}
the estimates (
5.4.19
) and (
5.4.20
) follow immediately from Theorem
Theorem
while (
5.4.21
) follows with Remark
Remark
(iv).

It remains to show (
5.4.20
) for
\nu=1
.
The estimate follows immediately from Corollary
Corollary
since
[f]_{\mathcal{C}^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}=[f]_{\operatorname{Lip}}=\|\nabla f\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
.

\square

5.5  Building blocks: Square function estimates

Next to all the estimates and characterizations from the previous subsection, which are based on the
\dot{F}^{\alpha}_{p,q}
and
\dot{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}
characterizations, we also need some estimates that originate from the theory of so-called nontangential square functions.
These are related to singular integrals and maximal functions, confer for example
[
Ste93
, Chapter I, §6.3]
.

Let
\phi
on
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
be a kernel sufficiently small at infinity (e.g.,
\phi\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
) with
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\phi\,dx=0
.
Then the (regular) square function
s_{\phi}
is defined as

f\mapsto s_{\phi}(f),\qquad(s_{\phi}f)(x)=\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}|(f\ast\phi_{t})(x)|^{2}\frac{dt}{t}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

while the so-called “nontangential” square function
S_{\phi}
is given by

f\mapsto S_{\phi}(f),\qquad(S_{\phi}f)(x)=\left(\int_{\{(y,t);|x-y|<t\}}|(f\ast\phi_{t})(y)|^{2}\frac{dt\,dy}{t^{n+1}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Again,
\phi_{t}
denotes the standard dilation.
For these square functions we find an
L^{p}
-estimate in
[
Ste93
, Chapter I, §8.23]
, which we can extend to a Lorentz estimate.

Theorem
.

Let
1<p<\infty
,
0<q\leq\infty
.
Assume
\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
with
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\phi\,dx=0
and
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}>0
, such that for all
x\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}

|\phi(x)|\leq C(1+|x|)^{-n-\sigma_{1}},\qquad|\nabla\phi(x)|\leq C(1+|x|)^{-n-\sigma_{2}}.

Then for all
f\in{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
we have

\|s_{\phi}(f)\|_{{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}}\lesssim\|f\|_{{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}},

and

\|S_{\phi}(f)\|_{{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}}\lesssim\|f\|_{{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}}.

\square

Proof
.

The result from
[
Ste93
]
is originally formulated for kernels
\phi\in\mathcal{S}
, the proof in §8.23 bulding upon observations from §6.3 and §6.4.
According to
[
Ste93
, Chapter I, §8.23(c)]
, the decay condition can be relaxed for example to
|\phi(x)|\leq C(1+|x|)^{-n-1}
and
|\nabla\phi(x)|\leq C(1+|x|)^{-n-1}
.
In fact, the conditions we stated in the Theorem suffice.
First, we list the necessary adjustments to the proof in
[
Ste93
]
.
Second, we apply Theorem
Theorem
to obtain the full Lorentz scale.

Step 1:

The part of the proof given in §8.23 is unchanged, while in §6.3 and §6.4 some small adjustments are necessary.
In §6.4, instead of
\phi_{t}(x)\leq At|x|^{-n-1}
we obtain
\phi_{t}(x)\leq At^{\sigma_{1}}|x|^{-n-\sigma_{1}}
.
In §6.3, instead of
{\mathcal{F}}(\phi)(\xi)\leq A|\xi|
we have
{\mathcal{F}}(\phi)(\xi)\leq A|\xi|^{\tilde{\sigma}}
for
0<\tilde{\sigma}<\min\{1,\sigma_{1}\}
because
{\mathcal{F}}(\phi)(0)=0
and
{\mathcal{F}}(\phi)\in\mathcal{C}^{\tilde{\sigma}}
.
The former is a direct consequence of the integral over
\phi
vanishing.
We obtain the latter via Triebel-Lizorkin and Besov-Lipschitz space identifications.
For
\sigma<\sigma_{1}
we have

J^{\sigma}{\mathcal{F}}(\phi)=C{\mathcal{F}}((1+|x|)^{\sigma}\phi)\in L^{p}\quad\text{for all }2\leq p<\infty,

because
(1+|x|)^{\sigma}\phi\in L^{1}\cap L^{2}
if
|\phi(x)|\leq C(1+|x|)^{-n-\sigma_{1}}
.
Since
L^{p}=F^{0}_{p,2}
, with Theorem
Theorem
we obtain
{\mathcal{F}}(\phi)\in F^{\sigma}_{p,2}
for all
2\leq p<\infty
.
We then have
{\mathcal{F}}(\phi)\in F^{\sigma}_{p,2}\subset B^{\sigma}_{p,p}
due to Proposition
Proposition
(iii).
In order to manipulate the other parameters, we use a generalization of the classical Sobolev embedding theorem for Triebel-Lizorkin and Besov-Lipschitz spaces.
With
[
Tri83
, Theorem 2.7.1]
we get
{\mathcal{F}}(\phi)\in B^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{\infty,p}
for all
\tilde{\sigma}<\sigma
.
Finally, Proposition
Proposition
(i) yields
{\mathcal{F}}(\phi)\in B^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{\infty,p}\subset B^{\sigma}_{\infty,\infty}=\mathcal{C}^{\sigma}
.

Step 2:

The operators
s_{\phi}
and
S_{\phi}
are obviously quasi-linear due to the Minkowski inequality.
With Theorem
Theorem
we then obtain the above result, since we already have the boundedness regarding
L^{p}=L^{(p,p)}
for any
1<p<\infty
.

\square

Applying the non-tangential square function estimate to various modifications of our Poisson kernel, we now obtain a variety of estimates.
These are interesting, since they occur as counterpart to the integrals equivalent to the
BMO
-space norm, see Lemma
Lemma
.
The regular square function estimate also provides some estimates, but these are already covered via the Triebel-Lizorkin space characterizations.

Proposition
(Square function estimates).

Let
1<p<\infty
,
0<q\leq\infty
and
s\in(0,2)
.
Denote with
F^{s}(x,t)=P^{s}_{t}f(x)
the
s
-harmonic extension of
f\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
.

Regarding classical derivatives, for
\nu\in[0,s)
we have

\left\|x\mapsto\left(\int_{\{(y,t);|y-x|<t\}}t^{1-2\nu-n}|\partial_{t}F^{s}(y,t)|^{2}\,dy\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\|_{{L^{(p,q)}(Rn)}}\lesssim\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\nu}{2}}}f\|_{{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}},

(5.5.1)

and for
\nu\in[0,1)

\left\|x\mapsto\left(\int_{\{(y,t);|y-x|<t\}}t^{1-2\nu-n}|\nabla_{x}F^{s}(y,t)|^{2}\,dy\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\|_{{L^{(p,q)}(Rn)}}\lesssim\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\nu}{2}}}f\|_{{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}}.

(5.5.2)

Moreover, for any
\nu\in[0,\min\{1+s,2\})
we have

\left\|x\mapsto\left(\int_{\{(y,t);|y-x|<t\}}t^{3-2\nu-n}|\nabla_{x}\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}F^{s}(y,t)|^{2}\,dy\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\|_{{L^{(p,q)}(Rn)}}\lesssim\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\nu}{2}}}f\|_{{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}}.

(5.5.3)

Regarding fractional derivatives, for
\beta>0
and
\nu\in[0,\beta)
we have

\left\|x\mapsto\left(\int_{\{(y,t);|y-x|<t\}}t^{2\beta-2\nu-n-1}|P^{s}_{t}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}f)(y)|^{2}\,dy\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\|_{{L^{(p,q)}(Rn)}}\lesssim\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\nu}{2}}}f\|_{{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}}.

(5.5.4)

All these estimates also hold for
-n<\nu<0
when replacing
\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\nu}{2}}}f\|_{{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}}
with
\|I^{-\nu}f\|_{{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}}
.

\square

Proof
(Estimate (
5.5.1
)).

In order to show (
5.5.1
), we only need to bring the left side of the estimate in the form of a nontangential square function and verify that the respective kernels fulfill the conditions of Theorem
Theorem
.
We have

\left(\int_{\Gamma(x)}t^{1-2\nu-n}|\partial_{t}F^{s}(y,t)|^{2}\,dy\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}=\left(\int_{\Gamma(x)}t^{-1-n}|t^{1-\nu}(\partial_{t}p^{s}_{t}*f)(y)|^{2}\,dy\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

with
\Gamma(x)=\{(y,t)\,;\,|y-x|<t\}
.
In order to obtain the estimate, we need to find a kernel
q
that satisfies the conditions of Theorem
Theorem
and fulfills

t^{1-\nu}\partial_{t}p^{s}_{t}*f=q_{t}*{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\nu}{2}}}f.

Applying the Fourier transform to the left side and recalling (
5.2.12
), we obtain

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt{\mathcal{F}}(t^{1-\nu}\partial_{t}p^{s}_{t}*f)(\xi)&=&Ct^{2-\nu}|\xi|^{2}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda^{\frac{s-2}{2}}e^{-\lambda-\frac{|t\xi|^{2}}{c\lambda}}\frac{d\lambda}{\lambda}\right){\mathcal{F}}(f)(\xi)\\
&=&C|t\xi|^{2-\nu}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda^{\frac{s-2}{2}}e^{-\lambda-\frac{|t\xi|^{2}}{c\lambda}}\frac{d\lambda}{\lambda}\right){\mathcal{F}}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{\nu}{2}}}f)(\xi).\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

Taking into consideration the interaction between Fourier transform and dilation, the candidate for a fitting kernel is given by

q(x)={\mathcal{F}}^{-1}\left(\xi\mapsto C|\xi|^{2-\nu}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda^{\frac{s-2}{2}}e^{-\lambda-\frac{|\xi|^{2}}{c\lambda}}\frac{d\lambda}{\lambda}\right)\right)

(5.5.5)

for some constant
C\neq 0
.
Substituting
\tilde{\lambda}=\frac{|\xi|^{2}}{c\lambda}
, we obtain

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554ptC|\xi|^{2-\nu}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda^{\frac{s-2}{2}}e^{-\lambda-\frac{|\xi|^{2}}{c\lambda}}\frac{d\lambda}{\lambda}\right)&=&C|\xi|^{s-\nu}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{|\xi|^{2}}{c\lambda}\right)^{\frac{2-s}{2}}e^{-\lambda-\frac{|\xi|^{2}}{c\lambda}}\frac{d\lambda}{\lambda}\right)\\
&=&C|\xi|^{s-\nu}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\tilde{\lambda}^{\frac{2-s}{2}}e^{-\frac{|\xi|^{2}}{c\tilde{\lambda}}-\tilde{\lambda}}\frac{d\tilde{\lambda}}{\tilde{\lambda}}\right).\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

Therefore, due to
[
Hao16
, Proposition 7.6(a)]
and the definition of the fractional Laplacian, an explicit formula for the kernel is

q(x)=C{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s-\nu}{2}}}\left((|x|^{2}+1)^{-\frac{s-2-n}{2}}\right).

(5.5.6)

Combining Lemma
Lemma
and Lemma
Lemma
, the
L^{\infty}
-estimate and the decay estimate for the fractional Laplacian,

|q(x)|\leq C\frac{1}{(1+|x|)^{-n-s+\nu}},\qquad|\nabla_{x}q(x)|\leq C\frac{1}{(1+|x|)^{-n-s+\nu}}.

Because of
\lim_{\xi\rightarrow 0}{\mathcal{F}}(q)(\xi)=0
and the continuity of
{\mathcal{F}}(q)
, we further have
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}q\,dx=0
, compare Step 3 of the proof for Theorem
Theorem
.
Since
\nu<s
and therefore
s-\nu>0
, the conditions for the square function estimate are fulfilled.
Thus, we have shown (
5.5.1
).

The same arguments hold for
-n<\nu<0
, where we use that
{\mathcal{F}}(I^{-\nu}f)(\xi)=C|\xi|^{\nu}
.

\square

Remark
.

Based on (
5.5.5
), one might be tempted to immediately assume

q(x)=C{(-\Delta)^{\frac{2-\nu}{2}}}\left((1+|x|^{2})^{-\frac{n+s-2}{2}}\right).

This would only work for
n\geq 2
though, because for
[
Hao16
, Proposition 7.6(a)]
we need that
n+s-2>0

Instead, we basically use the following interesting interaction between fractional Laplacian and Poisson kernel on the side of the Fourier transforms.
For
0<\gamma<n
we have

{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}}(|x|^{2}+1)^{\frac{\gamma-n}{2}}=C(|x|^{2}+1)^{\frac{-\gamma-n}{2}}.

This interaction can easily be proven on the side of the Fourier transforms with the same transformations we used to obtain the explicit form of
q
.
Also, on the Fourier side, we can choose
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
.

\square

Proof
(Estimate (
5.5.2
)).

This estimate is obtained analogously to (
5.5.1
), starting from

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt{\mathcal{F}}(t^{1-\nu}\partial_{x_{i}}p^{s}_{t}*f)(\xi)&=&Ct^{1-\nu}\xi_{i}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda^{\frac{s}{2}}e^{-\lambda-\frac{|t\xi|^{2}}{c\lambda}}\frac{d\lambda}{\lambda}\right){\mathcal{F}}(f)(\xi)\\
&=&C|t\xi|^{1-\nu}\frac{t\xi_{i}}{|t\xi|}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda^{\frac{s}{2}}e^{-\lambda-\frac{|t\xi|^{2}}{c\lambda}}\frac{d\lambda}{\lambda}\right){\mathcal{F}}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{\nu}{2}}}f)(\xi).\\
\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

Recalling the Fourier symbol of the Riesz transforms, we then get the kernel

q(x)=C{(-\Delta)^{\frac{1-\nu}{2}}}\mathcal{R}_{i}\left((1+|x|^{2})^{-\frac{n+s}{2}}\right).

(5.5.7)

The conditions of Theorem
Theorem
are verified via the same arguments as above when additionally taking into account Lemma
Lemma
and Lemma
Lemma
, the decay estimate and the
L^{\infty}
-estimate for the Riesz transforms.

\square

Proof
(Estimate (
5.5.3
)).

For this estimate we have two different types of kernels.
Analogous to the proofs of the other two estimates, we start with

{\mathcal{F}}(t^{2-\nu}\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{x_{j}}p^{s}_{t}*f)(\xi)=C|t\xi|^{2-\nu}\frac{t\xi_{i}}{|t\xi|}\frac{t\xi_{j}}{|t\xi|}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda^{\frac{s}{2}}e^{-\lambda-\frac{|t\xi|^{2}}{c\lambda}}\frac{d\lambda}{\lambda}\right){\mathcal{F}}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{\nu}{2}}}f)(\xi)

and

{\mathcal{F}}(t^{2-\nu}\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{t}p^{s}_{t}*f)(\xi)=C\frac{t\xi_{i}}{|t\xi|}|t\xi|^{1+s-\nu}|t\xi|^{2-s}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda^{\frac{s-2}{2}}e^{-\lambda-\frac{|t\xi|^{2}}{c\lambda}}\frac{d\lambda}{\lambda}\right){\mathcal{F}}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{\nu}{2}}}f)(\xi).

We then obtain

q(x)=C{(-\Delta)^{\frac{2-\nu}{2}}}\mathcal{R}_{i}{\mathcal{R}_{j}}\left((1+|x|^{2})^{-\frac{n+s}{2}}\right),

and

q(x)=C{(-\Delta)^{\frac{1+s-\nu}{2}}}\mathcal{R}_{i}\left((1+|x|^{2})^{-\frac{n+2-s}{2}}\right)

respectively.
Following the same line of argument as for the other two estimates, both types of kernels fulfill the conditions of Theorem
Theorem
.

\square

Proof
(Estimate (
5.5.4
)).

Again, we first need to find a suitable kernel, so that the left side of the estimate is in the form of a nontangential square function.
We have

{\mathcal{F}}(t^{\beta-\nu}P^{s}_{t}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}f))(\xi)=C|t\xi|^{\beta-\nu}\,{\mathcal{F}}((p^{s}_{1})_{t})\,{\mathcal{F}}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{\nu}{2}}}f).

Therefore, a suitable kernel is

q(x)=C{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta-\nu}{2}}}\left((1+|x|^{2})^{-\frac{n+s}{2}}\right).

Following the arguments from the proof of (
5.5.1
), this kernel satisfies the conditions of Theorem
Theorem
.

\square

Corollary
.

Let
1<p<\infty
,
0<q\leq\infty
and
s\in(0,2)
.
Denote with
F^{s}(x,t)=P^{s}_{t}f(x)
the
s
-harmonic extension of
f\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
.
Then for
0<\nu<n
we have

\left\|x\mapsto\left(\int_{\{(y,t);|y-x|<t\}}t^{2\nu-n-1}|F^{s}(y,t)|^{2}\,dy\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\|_{{L^{(p,q)}(Rn)}}\lesssim\|I^{\nu}f\|_{{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}}.

(5.5.8)

\square

Proof
.

Insert
I^{\nu}f
into (
5.5.4
) with
\beta=\nu
.

\square

Remark
.

Same as in the subsection above, the estimates in Proposition
Proposition
are listed as (norm-)equivalencies in
[
LS20
]
.
Here, in order to obtain equivalency for the square-function estimates, we would need to verify that the kernels are nondegenerate as described in
[
Ste93
, Chapter I, §8.23(b)]
, which is basically the Tauberian condition
(C2)
, see
[
Ste93
, Chapter IV, §4.3, p.159]
.
And again, for the Lorentz scale estimates, our interpolation theorem only yields one direction of the estimate.

\square

5.6  Building blocks: Maximal function and pointwise estimates of
P^{s}_{t}f

In addition to the Triebel-Lizorkin and Besov-Lipschitz space characterizations as well as the square function estimates, we further need pointwise estimates, which offer more than for example Corollary
Corollary
and Corollary
Corollary
.
The central vehicle for these pointwise estimates will be the maximal function, which we already briefly mentioned at the beginning of the previous subsection.
For
f\in L^{1}_{\text{loc}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
the maximal function
\mathcal{M}f
is defined by

(\mathcal{M}f)(x)\coloneqq\sup_{\delta>0}\frac{1}{|B(x,\delta)|}\int_{B(x,\delta)}|f(y)|\,dy.

(5.6.1)

The idea is to obtain pointwise estimates of the
s
-harmonic extension against the maximal function of
f
or derivatives of
f
with the following proposition from
[
Ste93
, Chapter II, §2.1]
.

Proposition
.

Assume that a measurable function
\psi\colon{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
has a radial majorant which is non-increasing, bounded and integrable.
Let
f\in L^{1}_{\text{loc}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
.
Then, for
F\colon{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
F(x,t)=(f\ast\psi_{t})(x)
, where
\psi_{t}
is the standard dilation of
\psi
, we have

\sup_{(y,t)\,;\,|y-x|<t}|F(y,t)|\lesssim\mathcal{M}f(x).

\square

These estimates are useful because in turn the Lorentz space norm of the maximal function can be estimated against the respective Lorentz space norm of the original function with the following theorem from
[
Ste93
, Chapter I, §3.1]
.

Theorem
.

Let
f
be a function defined on
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
.

(i)

If
f\in L^{p}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
,
1\leq p\leq\infty
, then
\mathcal{M}f
is finite almost everywhere.

(ii)

If
f\in{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
,
1<p<\infty
,
0<q\leq\infty
or
p=q=\infty
, then
\mathcal{M}f\in{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
and

\|\mathcal{M}f\|_{{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}}\lesssim\|f\|_{{L^{(p,q)}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}}.

\square

Proof
.

From
[
Ste93
, Chapter I, §3.1]
we obtain (i) and (ii) for
q=p
.
We then obtain the full Lorentz version of (ii) via interpolation with Theorem
Theorem
.
The maximal function is defined for all
f\in L^{1}_{\text{loc}}\supset L^{p_{0}}+L^{p_{1}}
,
1<p_{0},p_{1}\leq\infty
.
Obviously, the maximal function is quasi linear.
Therefore, Theorem
Theorem
is applicable.

\square

We will use this theorem in combination with the following pointwise estimates.
Besides the promised estimates for the unmodified
s
-harmonic extension, we obtain some additional pointwise estimates for
\partial_{t}P^{s}_{t}f
and
\nabla_{x}P^{s}_{t}f
.
Note that we take the supremum over the same set, which already occured in the nontangential square functions, that is
\{(y,t)\,;\,|y-x|<t\}
.

Proposition
(Pointwise estimates with the maximal function).

Let
s\in(0,2)
and
f\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
.
Denote with
F^{s}(x,t)=P^{s}_{t}f(x)
.
Then

\sup_{(y,t)\,;\,|y-x|<t}|F^{s}(y,t)|\lesssim\mathcal{M}f(x).

(5.6.2)

For
\sigma\in[0,s]
, regarding the derivative in
t
direction,

\sup_{(y,t)\,;\,|y-x|<t}|t^{1-\sigma}\partial_{t}F^{s}(y,t)|\lesssim\mathcal{M}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{\sigma}{2}}}f)(x).

(5.6.3)

For
\sigma\in[0,1]
, denoting
\nabla^{\sigma}=\nabla I^{1-\sigma}=\mathcal{R}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\sigma}{2}}}=(\mathcal{R}_{i}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\sigma}{2}}})_{i}
,

\sup_{(y,t)\,;\,|y-x|<t}|t^{1-\sigma}\nabla_{x}F^{s}(y,t)|\lesssim\mathcal{M}(\nabla^{\sigma}f)(x).

(5.6.4)

Finally, for any
0<\sigma<n
,

\sup_{(y,t)\,;\,|y-x|<t}t^{\sigma}|F^{s}(y,t)|\lesssim\mathcal{M}(I^{\sigma}f)(x).

(5.6.5)

\square

Proof
(Estimate (
5.6.2
)).

Estimate (
5.6.2
) is a direct consequence of Proposition
Proposition
since
P^{s}_{t}f=C_{n,s}(p^{s}_{t}*f)=C_{n,s}(p^{s}_{1})_{t}*f
and

p^{s}_{1}(x)=\frac{1}{(1+|x|^{2})^{\frac{n+s}{2}}}

is radial, non-increasing, bounded and integrable.

\square

Proof
(Estimate (
5.6.3
)).

Following the proof of Proposition
Proposition
, more specifically (
5.5.6
), we have

t^{1-\sigma}\partial_{t}F^{s}(x,t)=(q_{t}*{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\sigma}{2}}}f)(x)

with

q(x)=C{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s-\sigma}{2}}}\left((|x|^{2}+1)^{\frac{s-2-n}{2}}\right).

For
\sigma<s
, just as in the proof of Proposition
Proposition
, we obtain

|q(x)|\leq C\frac{1}{(1+|x|)^{n+s-\sigma}}

with Lemma
Lemma
and Lemma
Lemma
.
For
\sigma=s
we have

|q(x)|=C\frac{1}{(1+|x|)^{n+2-s}}.

Either way, we have a radial, non-increasing, bounded and integrable majorant.
Therefore, Proposition
Proposition
yields (
5.6.3
) because
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\sigma}{2}}}f\in L^{1}_{\text{loc}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
since
f\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
.

\square

Proof
(Estimate (
5.6.4
)).

From the proof of Proposition
Proposition
, more specifically (
5.5.7
), we know that

t^{1-\sigma}\partial_{x_{i}}F^{s}(x,t)=(q_{t}*\mathcal{R}_{i}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\sigma}{2}}}f)(x)

where

q(x)=C{(-\Delta)^{\frac{1-\sigma}{2}}}\left((1+|x|^{2})^{-\frac{n+s}{2}}\right).

For
\sigma<1
, combining Lemma
Lemma
and Lemma
Lemma
, we have

q(x)\leq C(1+|x|^{2})^{-n-(1-\sigma)},

which is a radial, non-increasing, bounded and integrable majorant.
For
\sigma=1
,
q(x)
itself is radial, non-increasing, bounded and integrable.
Therefore, with Proposition
Proposition
we have

\sup_{(y,t)\,;\,|y-x|<t}|t^{1-\sigma}\partial_{x_{i}}F^{s}(y,t)|\lesssim\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{R}_{i}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\sigma}{2}}}f)(x).

Combining these equations for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
leads to (
5.6.4
).

\square

Proof
(Estimate (
5.6.5
)).

Via the Fourier symbols of fractional Laplacian and Riesz potential we obtain

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554ptt^{\sigma}F^{s}(x,t)&=&\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}t^{\sigma}p^{s}_{t}(x-y)\,f(y)\,dy\\
&=&\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}t^{\sigma}(2\pi\xi)^{\sigma}{\mathcal{F}}(p^{s}_{t})(\xi)\,(2\pi\xi)^{-\sigma}{\mathcal{F}}(f)(\xi)\\
&=&\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}t^{\sigma}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\sigma}{2}}}p^{s}_{t}(x-y)\,I^{\sigma}f(y)\,dy.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

At the beginning of step 4 in the proof of
Theorem
, we saw that
{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\sigma}{2}}}p^{s}_{t}=t^{-\sigma}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{\sigma}{2}}}p^{s}_{1})_{t}
.
Therefore,

t^{\sigma}F^{s}(x,t)=(q_{t}*I^{\sigma}f)(x)

with

q(x)={(-\Delta)^{\frac{\sigma}{2}}}p^{s}_{1}={(-\Delta)^{\frac{\sigma}{2}}}\frac{1}{(|x|^{2}+1)^{\frac{n+s}{2}}}.

As in the proof of the other estimates, combining Lemma
Lemma
and Lemma
Lemma
, we obtain a radial, non-increasing, bounded and integrable majorant.
Thus, (
5.6.4
) follows with Proposition
Proposition
.

\square

Remark
.

In the same way that (
5.6.5
) is the extension of (
5.6.2
), the estimates (
5.6.3
) and (
5.6.4
) can also be extended to the range of
-n<\sigma<0
as

\sup_{(y,t)\,;\,|y-x|<t}|t^{1-\sigma}\partial_{t}F^{s}(y,t)|\lesssim\mathcal{M}(I^{-\sigma}f)(x)

and

\sup_{(y,t)\,;\,|y-x|<t}|t^{1-\sigma}\nabla_{x}F^{s}(y,t)|\lesssim\mathcal{M}((\mathcal{R}_{i}I^{-\sigma}f)_{i})(x).

\square

Remark
.

Plugging in
\partial_{x_{i}}F^{s}(x,t)=P^{s}_{t}(\partial_{x_{i}}f)(x)
for
F^{s}(x,t)
and
\tilde{\sigma}=1+\sigma
in (
5.6.3
), we obtain

\sup_{(y,t)\,;\,|y-x|<t}|t^{2-\tilde{\sigma}}\partial_{t}\nabla_{x}F^{s}(y,t)|\lesssim\mathcal{M}((\mathcal{R}_{i}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\tilde{\sigma}}{2}}}f)_{i})(x)

(5.6.6)

for any
\tilde{\sigma}\in[0,1+s]
, with Remark
Remark
in mind.
Analogously, from (
5.6.4
) we obtain

\sup_{(y,t)\,;\,|y-x|<t}|t^{2-\tilde{\sigma}}\nabla_{x}^{2}F^{s}(y,t)|\lesssim\mathcal{M}((\mathcal{R}_{i}\mathcal{R}_{j}{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\tilde{\sigma}}{2}}}f)_{i,j})(x)

(5.6.7)

for any
\tilde{\sigma}\in[0,2]
.

\square

5.7  Blackbox estimates from
{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}
to
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}

Having now collected a variety of estimates for the
s
-harmonic extension, we can combine these results with relatively little effort to more complex, custom estimates involving multiple
{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
-functions.
We obtain three different estimates.
First, there is a Lorentz-estimate, where we obtain an estimate against the Lorentz-space norms of the occurring functions’ fractional derivatives.
Second, we have a
BMO
-estimate, where one
BMO
-norm appears next to the Lorentz space norms.
Analogous to this
BMO
-estimate, we also obtain a Hölder space estimate.

Proposition
(Lorentz-estimate).

Let
s\in(0,2)
and
2\leq k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Assume
p_{i}\in(1,\infty)
,
q_{i}\in[1,\infty]
for
i\in\{1,\ldots k\}
with

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{1}{p_{i}}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{1}{q_{i}}=1.

Denote with
F^{s}_{i}(x,t)=P^{s}_{t}f_{i}(x)
the extension of
f_{i}\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
,
i\in\{1,\ldots k\}
.
Then for
s_{i}\in(-n,0)
,
i=1,2
, and
s_{i}\in(-n,0]
,
i\geq 3
, the following estimate holds.

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}t^{-1-s_{1}-\ldots-s_{k}}|F^{s}_{1}(x,t)|\,|F^{s}_{2}(x,t)|\ldots|F^{s}_{k}(x,t)|\,dx\,dt\\
\vskip 4.30554pt\qquad\lesssim\|I^{-s_{1}}f_{1}\|_{L^{(p_{1},q_{1})}}\,\|I^{-s_{2}}f_{2}\|_{L^{(p_{2},q_{2})}}\ldots\|I^{-s_{k}}f_{k}\|_{L^{(p_{k},q_{k})}}.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

For any
i\in\{1,\ldots k\}
, the following variations of the estimate hold.
Whenever choosing
s_{i}>0
, replace
\|I^{-s_{1}}f_{i}\|_{L^{(p_{i},q_{i})}}
with
\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s_{1}}{2}}}f_{i}\|_{L^{(p_{i},q_{i})}}
.

(a)

Replacing
|F^{s}_{i}(x,t)|
with
t|\partial_{t}F^{s}_{i}(x,t)|
allows
s_{i}\in(-n,s)
(
s_{i}\in(-n,s]
for
i\geq 3
).

(b)

Replacing
|F^{s}_{i}(x,t)|
with
t|\nabla_{x}F^{s}_{i}(x,t)|
allows
s_{i}\in(-n,1)
(
s_{i}\in(-n,1]
for
i\geq 3
).

(c)

Replacing
|F^{s}_{i}(x,t)|
with
t^{2}|\nabla_{x}\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}F^{s}_{i}(x,t)|
allows
s_{i}\in(-n,\min\{2,1+s\})
for
i=1,2
and
s_{i}\in(-n,\min\{2,1+s\}]
for
i\geq 3
.

The estimate also holds if
(p_{i},q_{i})=(\infty,\infty)
for any
i\geq 3
, except for variations (b) and (c).

\square

Proof
.

There are two different options to treat the additional functions, i.e. the
F_{i}
-terms for
i\geq 3
.
First, ignoring the extended parameter range for
i\geq 3
, we may show the result via two Hölder estimates and the Bessel-potential space estimates from Subsection
5.4
.

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LCL}\vskip 4.30554pt\mathcal{I}&\coloneqq&\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}t^{-1-s_{1}-s_{2}-\ldots-s_{k}}|F^{s}_{1}(x,t)|\,|F^{s}_{2}(x,t)|\ldots|F^{s}_{k}(x,t)|\,dx\,dt\\
&=&\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{-\frac{1}{k}-s_{1}}|F^{s}_{1}(x,t)|\ldots t^{-\frac{1}{k}-s_{k}}|F^{s}_{k}(x,t)|\,dt\,dx\\
&\leq&\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}|t^{-\frac{1}{k}-s_{1}}F^{s}_{1}(x,t)^{k}\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{k}}\ldots\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}|t^{-\frac{1}{k}-s_{k}}F^{s}_{k}(x,t)|^{k}\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{k}}\,dx\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

Applying Theorem
Theorem
, the Hölder estimate for Lorentz spaces, we obtain

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LCL}\vskip 4.30554pt\mathcal{I}&\lesssim&\left\|x\mapsto\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}|t^{-\frac{1}{k}-s_{1}}F^{s}_{1}(x,t)|^{k}\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{k}}\right\|_{L^{(p_{1},q_{1})}}\ldots\left\|x\mapsto\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}|t^{-\frac{1}{k}-s_{k}}F^{s}_{k}(x,t)|^{k}\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{k}}\right\|_{L^{(p_{k},q_{k})}}\\
&\lesssim&\|I^{-s_{1}}f_{1}\|_{L^{(p_{1},q_{1})}}\ldots\|I^{-s_{k}}f_{k}\|_{L^{(p_{k},q_{k})}}.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

The last estimate is due to Remark
Remark
and (
5.4.11
).
For variation (a), instead of (
5.4.11
) apply (
5.4.8
), for variation (b) apply (
5.4.7
) and for variation (c) apply the combination of (
5.4.9
) and (
5.4.10
).

For the second option, we use the pointwise estimates from Proposition
Proposition
to treat the
F_{i}
-terms for
i\geq 3
, which makes the use of Remark
Remark
obsolete.
For any
i\geq 3
we apply (
5.6.5
) and extract

\sup_{t>0}t^{-s_{i}}|F_{i}^{s}(x,t)|\leq\mathcal{M}(I^{-s_{i}}f_{i})(x)

from the inner integral before the first Hölder estimate.
Following the same line as above, we then have

\mathcal{I}\lesssim\|I^{-s_{1}}f_{1}\|_{L^{(p_{1},q_{1})}}\|I^{-s_{2}}f_{2}\|_{L^{(p_{2},q_{2})}}\|\mathcal{M}(I^{-s_{3}}f_{3})\|_{L^{(p_{3},q_{3})}}\ldots\|\mathcal{M}(I^{-s_{k}}f_{k})\|_{L^{(p_{k},q_{k})}}.

We obtain the result with Theorem
Theorem
. For variation (a), instead of (
5.6.5
) we use Remark
Remark
and (
5.6.3
).
For variations (b) and (c), we use (
5.6.4
) and a combination of (
5.6.6
) and (
5.6.7
).
The occurring Riesz transforms pose no problem due to their
L^{p}
-boundedness, see Proposition
Proposition
.
The case
(p_{i},q_{i})=(\infty,\infty)
is not compatible with the variations (b) and (c) because the
L^{p}
-boundedness of the Riesz transforms does not extend to
p=\infty
.

\square

In order to piece together the following BMO estimate, we need an additional estimate from
[
Ste93
]
. This estimate will replace the
1
-dimensional Hölder estimates from the proofs of Proposition
Proposition
, yielding terms related to Carleson-measures such as in the
BMO
-space characterizations and square functions such as in Proposition
Proposition
.
The result can be found in
[
Ste93
, Chapter IV, §4.4, p.162, Proposition]
.

Lemma
.

Let
\Phi,G
be measurable functions given on
{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}
. Then

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}\Phi(x,t)G\,(x,t)\,dx\,dt\\
\vskip 4.30554pt\lesssim\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}},r>0}\left(\frac{1}{|B(x,r)|}\int_{T(B(x,r))}t|\Phi(y,t)|^{2}dy\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\int_{\{(y,t);|y-x|<t\}}|G(y,t)|^{2}\frac{dtdy}{t^{n+1}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}dx\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

where
T(B(x,r))=\{(y,t)\in{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}\,:\,|y-x|<r-t\}
is the “tent” over
B(x,r)
.

\square

Equipped with this additional tool, we can now tackle the
BMO
-estimates.

Proposition
(
BMO
-estimate).

Let
s\in(0,2)
and
2\leq k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Assume
p_{i}\in(1,\infty)
,
q_{i}\in[1,\infty]
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
with

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{1}{p_{i}}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{1}{q_{i}}=1.

Denote with
\Phi^{s}(x,t)=P^{s}_{t}\phi(x)
,
F_{i}^{s}(x,t)=P^{s}_{t}f_{i}(x)
for
\phi,f_{i}\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
,
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
.
Then for
s_{1}\in(-n,0)
and
s_{i}\in(-n,0]
,
i\in\{2,\ldots,k\}
, the following estimate holds.

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}t^{-s_{1}-s_{2}-\ldots-s_{k}}|\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}\Phi^{s}(x,t)|\,|F_{1}^{s}(x,t)|\,|F_{2}^{s}(x,t)|\ldots|F_{k}^{s}(x,t)|\,dx\,dt\\
\vskip 4.30554pt\qquad\lesssim[\phi]_{BMO}\|I^{-s_{1}}f_{1}\|_{L^{(p_{1},q_{1})}}\|I^{-s_{2}}f_{2}\|_{L^{(p_{2},q_{2})}}\ldots\|I^{-s_{k}}f_{k}\|_{L^{(p_{k},q_{k})}}.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

The estimate also holds in the following variations.

(a)

|\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}\Phi^{s}(x,t)|
can be replaced with
|t^{\beta-1}P^{s}_{t}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}\phi)(x)|
for any
\beta>0
.

(b)

|\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}\Phi^{s}(x,t)|
can be replaced with
|t^{\beta}\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}P^{s}_{t}({(-\Delta)^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}\phi)(x)|
for any
\beta>0
.

Additionally, for any
i\in\{1,\ldots k\}
, the following variations of the estimate hold.
Whenever choosing
s_{i}\geq 0
, replace
\|I^{-s_{1}}f_{i}\|_{L^{(p_{i},q_{i})}}
with
\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s_{1}}{2}}}f_{i}\|_{L^{(p_{i},q_{i})}}
.

(c)

Replacing
|F_{i}^{s}(x,t)|
with
t|\partial_{t}F_{i}^{s}(x,t)|
allows
s_{i}\in(-n,s]
(
s_{1}\in(-n,s)
for
i=1
).

(d)

Replacing
|F_{i}^{s}(x,t)|
with
t|\nabla_{x}F_{i}^{s}(x,t)|
allows
s_{i}\in(-n,1]
(
s_{1}\in(-n,1)
for
i=1
).

(e)

Replacing
|F_{i}^{s}(x,t)|
with
t^{2}|\nabla_{x}\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}F_{i}^{s}(x,t)|
allows
s_{i}\in(-n,\min\{1+s,2\}]
for
i\geq 2
,
s_{1}\in(-n,\min\{1+s,2\})
for
i=1
.

The estimate also holds if
(p_{i},q_{i})=(\infty,\infty)
for any
i\geq 2
, except for variations (d) and (e).

\square

Proof
.

To proof these estimates, we first apply Lemma
Lemma
.
The first term then corresponds exactly to the
BMO
-space characterizations.
The second term resembles the square function estimates from Subsection
5.4
.
Before we apply these, similar to the proof of Proposition
Proposition
we first extract the supremum of the
F_{i}
- terms,
i\geq 2
.

Denote
\Gamma(x)\coloneqq\{(y,t);|y-x|<t\}
for
x\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
.
With Lemma
Lemma
we have

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LCLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\mathcal{I}&\coloneqq&\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}t^{-s_{1}-s_{2}-\ldots-s_{k}}|\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}\Phi^{s}||F_{1}^{s}(x,t)||F_{2}^{s}(x,t)|\ldots|F_{k}^{s}(x,t)|\,dx\,dt\\
&\lesssim&\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}},r>0}\left(\frac{1}{|B(x,r)|}\int_{T(B(x,r))}t|\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}\Phi(y,t)|^{2}dy\,dt\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&&\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\int_{\Gamma(x)}\left(t^{-s_{1}}|F_{1}^{s}(y,t)|t^{-s_{2}}|F_{2}^{s}(y,t)|\ldots t^{-s_{k}}|F_{k}^{s}(y,t)|\right)^{2}\frac{dtdy}{t^{n+1}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}dx.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

Due to (
5.6.5
) we have
\sup_{\Gamma(x)}t^{-s_{i}}|F_{i}^{s}(y,t)|\lesssim\mathcal{M}(I^{-s_{i}}f_{i})(x)
for all
i\in\{2,\ldots,k\}
.
Applying (
5.4.13
), we obtain

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\mathcal{I}&\lesssim&[\phi]_{BMO}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}\mathcal{M}(I^{-s_{2}}f_{2})(x)\ldots\mathcal{M}(I^{-s_{k}}f_{k})(x)\left(\int_{\Gamma(x)}t^{-2s_{1}-n-1}|F_{1}^{s}(y,t)|^{2}\,dt\,dy\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}dx\\
&\lesssim&[\phi]_{BMO}\|\mathcal{M}(I^{s_{2}}f_{2})\|_{L^{(p_{2},q_{2})}}\ldots\|\mathcal{M}(I^{s_{k}}f_{k})\|_{L^{(p_{k},q_{k})}}\|I^{-s_{1}}f_{1}\|_{L^{(p_{1},q_{1})}}\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

where for the last estimate we applied Theorem
Theorem
, the Hölder estimate for Lorentz spaces, and then immediately the square function estimate (
5.5.8
).
We then obtain the result with Theorem
Theorem
(ii).

The variations are obtained analogously by swapping out the respective estimates.
For variations (a) and (b), instead of (
5.4.13
) we use (
5.4.14
) and (
5.4.15
) respectively.
For variations (c) or (d), instead of (
5.6.5
) and (
5.5.8
) we use (
5.5.1
) and (
5.6.3
) or (
5.5.2
) and (
5.6.4
).
For variation (e), we use (
5.5.3
) and a combination of (
5.6.6
) and (
5.6.7
).
Additionally, for variations (d) and (e), we need the
L^{(p,q)}
-boundedness of the Riesz transform, which we obtain via interpolation from Proposition
Proposition
, the
L^{p}
-boundedness.

The annotation regarding
(p_{i},q_{i})=(\infty,\infty)
is obvious.
For variations (d) and (e) this does not work though, since the
L^{p}
-boundedness of the Riesz transforms only holds for
1<p<\infty
.

\square

Proposition
(Hölder-space-estimates).

Let
s\in(0,2)
and
2\leq k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Assume
p_{i}\in(1,\infty)
,
q_{i}\in[1,\infty]
for
i\in\{1,\ldots k\}
with

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{1}{p_{i}}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{1}{q_{i}}=1.

Denote with
\Phi^{s}(x,t)=P^{s}_{t}\phi(x)
and
F^{s}_{i}(x,t)=P^{s}_{t}f_{i}(x)
the extensions of
\phi,f_{i}\in{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})}
,
i\in\{1,\ldots k\}
.
Then for
\nu\in(0,\min\{1,s\})\setminus\mathbb{N}
,
s_{i}\in(-n,0)
for
i=1,2
, and
s_{i}\in(-n,0]
for
i\geq 3
, the following estimate holds.

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}t^{-\nu-s_{1}-\ldots-s_{k}}|\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}\Phi^{s}(x,t)|\,|F^{s}_{1}(x,t)|\ldots|F^{s}_{k}(x,t)|\,dx\,dt\\
\vskip 4.30554pt\qquad\lesssim[\phi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\nu}}\,\|I^{-s_{1}}f_{1}\|_{L^{(p_{1},q_{1})}}\ldots\|I^{-s_{k}}f_{k}\|_{L^{(p_{k},q_{k})}}.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

The estimate continues to hold under the following variations.

(a)

For
\nu=0
replace
[\phi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\nu}}
with
\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}
.

(b)

Replacing
|\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}\Phi^{s}(x,t)|
with
|\partial_{t}\Phi^{s}(x,t)|
allows
\nu\in(0,s)\setminus\mathbb{N}
.

(c)

Replacing
|\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}\Phi^{s}(x,t)|
with
|\nabla_{x}\Phi^{s}(x,t)|
allows
\nu\in(0,1]
.

(d)

Replacing
|\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}\Phi^{s}(x,t)|
with
t|\nabla_{x}\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}\Phi^{s}(x,t)|
allows
\nu\in(0,\min\{2,1+s\})\setminus\mathbb{N}
.

(e)

Replacing
[\phi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\nu}}
with
[{(-\Delta)^{\frac{\nu}{2}}}\phi]_{BMO}
allows to choose
s_{2}
as the
s_{i}
for
i\geq 3
.

Additionally, the following replacements are possible for any
i\in\{1,\ldots k\}
.
Whenever choosing
s_{i}\geq 0
, replace
\|I^{-s_{1}}f_{i}\|_{L^{(p_{i},q_{i})}}
with
\|{(-\Delta)^{\frac{s_{1}}{2}}}f_{i}\|_{L^{(p_{i},q_{i})}}
.

(f)

Replacing
|F^{s}_{i}(x,t)|
with
t|\partial_{t}F^{s}_{i}(x,t)|
allows
s_{i}\in(-n,s)
(
s_{i}\in(-n,s]
for
i\geq 3
).

(g)

Replacing
|F^{s}_{i}(x,t)|
with
t|\nabla_{x}F^{s}_{i}(x,t)|
allows
s_{i}\in(-n,1)
(
s_{i}\in(-n,1]
for
i\geq 3
).

(h)

Replacing
|F^{s}_{i}(x,t)|
with
t^{2}|\nabla_{x}\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}F^{s}_{i}(x,t)|
allows
s_{i}\in(-n,\min\{2,1+s\})
for
i=1,2
and
s_{i}\in(-n,\min\{2,1+s\}]
for
i\geq 3
.

The estimate also holds if
(p_{i},q_{i})=(\infty,\infty)
for any
i\geq 3
, except for variations (g) and (h).

\square

Proof
.

Combining (
5.4.19
) and (
5.4.20
), we obtain

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\mathcal{I}&\coloneqq&\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}t^{1-\nu-1-s_{1}-\ldots-s_{k}}|\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}\Phi^{s}(x,t)||F^{s}_{1}(x,t)|\ldots|F^{s}_{k}(x,t)|\,dx\,dt\\
&\lesssim&[\phi]_{\mathcal{C}^{\nu}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}t^{-1-s_{1}-\ldots-s_{k}}|F^{s}_{1}(x,t)|\ldots|F^{s}_{k}(x,t)|\,dx\,dt.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

The result then follows with Proposition
Proposition
.
For variation (a), instead of (
5.4.19
) and (
5.4.20
) apply (
5.6.3
) and (
5.6.4
) followed up by Theorem
Theorem
.
For variation (b) apply only (
5.4.19
), for variation (c) only (
5.4.20
) and for variation (d) apply (
5.4.21
).

Regarding variation (e), we follow the proof for Proposition
Proposition
, but instead of the
BMO
-characterizations from Proposition
Proposition
, we choose a suitable estimate from Corollary
Corollary
.

\square

6  Outlook: Possible extensions of the method

There seem to be two main options to further extend the method.

On the one hand, we may further modify the blackbox estimates, for example by implementing the Triebel-Lizorkin and Besov-Lipschitz characterizations in a more general form.
However, there are some limitations.
We would loose the extended parameter range for additional functions, which we originally obtained due to pointwise estimates, confer the proof of Proposition
Proposition
.
Additionally, for the
BMO
-estimate we only used the Triebel-Litzorkin space characterization of
BMO
, which we might replace with a more general
F^{s}_{\infty,q}
norm.
If we wanted to replace other norms with more general equivalents though, we would need Triebel-Lizorkin space characterizations reminiscent of the nontangential square function estimates.
But since the regular square function estimates are special cases of the space characterizations which we obtained from the Bui-Candy result, it does not seem too unlikely that Triebel-Lizorkin spaces can be characterized by expressions similar to nontangential square functions.

On the other hand, we might modify the means of extending from
{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
to
{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}
.
For example, in Subsection
3.3
, we only obtained the Chanillo commutator estimate for Riesz potentials of order
<1
.
In general, the extension by Cafarelli and Silvestre in
[
CS07
]
only works for fractional Laplacians of order
<2
.
In order to prove this estimate for higher orders, we would also need a higer order extension, which has been introduced for example in
[
RS16
]
or
[
Yan13
]
.
It might also be possible to extend other operators with suitably chosen extensions.
Lenzmann and Schikorra mention the Bessel potential operator
(1-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{s}}
as possible example.
We have already seen this operator as the lifting operator for inhomogeneous Triebel-Lizorkin and Besov-Lipschitz spaces.

Besides all these possible modifications, we do not expect the applications, which we have shown here, to be exhaustive for the unmodified method.
There should be a variety of other estimates, which are possible to prove with this method.
For example, Lenzmann and Schikorra suggest sharp limit space estimates as in
[
BL14
]
and
[
Li19
]
as particularly interesting possible applications.

Appendix A
Appendix

A.1  Stokes’ Theorem for differential forms on
{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}

Theorem
(Stokes’ Theorem for differential forms on
{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}
).

Let
w
be an
n
-form on
{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}
with

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554ptw=\sum_{1\leq i_{1}\leq\ldots\leq i_{n}\leq n+1}w_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}}~dx^{i_{1}}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx^{i_{n}}\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

where
w_{1,\ldots,n}
is integrable on
\partial{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}
and all
w_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}}
as well as their derivatives are integrable on
{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}
.
Then

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}dw=\int_{\partial{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}w=\int_{\partial{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}w_{1,\ldots,n}.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

\square

Proof
.

For compactly supported
w
the statement is true. The according proof is well known since it is an essential part of the usual proof of Stoke’s Theorem for compactly supported differential forms on arbitrary oriented smooth manifolds, see for example Theorem 16.11 in
[
Lee12
]
.

For arbitrary
w
we will approximate
w
via compactly supported differential forms.
We obtain these approximations by multiplying
w
with a special compactly supported function.
Let
\tilde{\eta}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}([0,\infty))
with

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\tilde{\eta}(r)=\begin{cases}1&\text{for }0\leq r\leq 1\\
0&\text{for }2\leq r\end{cases},\qquad\tilde{\eta}(r)\in[0,1]\quad\text{for }1\leq r\leq 2.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

For
R>0
we define
\eta_{R}\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}})
by
\eta_{R}(z)\coloneqq\tilde{\eta}\left(\frac{|z|}{R}\right)
.
Thus, we have

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\eta_{R}(z)=\begin{cases}1&\text{for }0\leq|z|\leq R\\
0&\text{for }2R\leq|z|\end{cases},\qquad\eta_{R}(z)\in[0,1]\quad\text{for }R\leq|z|\leq 2R.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

Regarding the derivatives of
\eta_{R}
, we obtain

\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}\eta_{R}(z)=0\quad\text{for }0\leq|z|\leq R\text{ or }2R\leq|z|,\qquad\|\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}\eta_{R}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\frac{\|\tilde{\eta}^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}}}{R}.

Apparently,
(\eta_{R}w)_{R>0}
are compactly supported differential forms that converge to
w
pointwise.
Since
w_{1,\ldots,n}
is integrable on
\partial{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}
, by Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem,

\lim_{R\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\partial{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}\eta_{R}w=\lim_{R\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\partial{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}\eta_{R}w_{1,\ldots,n}=\int_{\partial{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}w_{1,\ldots,n}.

Now turning to the term with the exterior derivative, we observe

\lim_{R\rightarrow\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}d(\eta_{R}w)=\lim_{R\rightarrow\infty}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}d\eta_{R}\wedge w+\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}\eta_{R}\,dw\right).

Thanks to the integrability of the
w_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}}
and the choice of
\eta_{R}
,

\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{L}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}l}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{C}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}c}}\lx@add@Preamble@PI{\newcolumntype{R}{>{\displaystyle\setlength{\arraycolsep}{4pt}}r}}\begin{array}[]{>{\vspace*{1ex}}LLLLLLLLLLLL}\vskip 4.30554pt\left|\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}d\eta_{R}\wedge w\right|&\leq\|\nabla_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}}\eta_{R}\|_{L^{\infty}}\sum_{1\leq i_{1}\leq\ldots\leq i_{n}\leq n+1}\int_{B_{2R}(0)\setminus B_{R}(0)}|w_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}}|\\
&\leq\frac{\|\tilde{\eta}^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}}}{R}\sum_{1\leq i_{1}\leq\ldots\leq i_{n}\leq n+1}\|w_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}}\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}})}\overset{R\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0.\end{array}\vskip-4.30554pt

Together with Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem and the integrability of the derivatives of the
w_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}}
and therefore of
dw
, we obtain

\lim_{R\rightarrow\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}d(\eta_{R}w)=0+\lim_{R\rightarrow\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}\eta_{R}\,dw=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}dw.

Since
\eta_{R}w
are compactly supported differential forms for
R>0
,

\int_{\partial{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}w=\lim_{R\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\partial{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}\eta_{R}w=\lim_{R\rightarrow\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}}d(\eta_{R}w)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}dw.

\square
